At the corner of a long, straight street in the far East End—brick upon brick like a ledger ruled by a joyless clerk—there stands a house that has the indecency to remember. The other houses merely endure. This one insists.

It draws me as a smell draws you: not the clean smell of bread, but the sour steam that escapes a pot when you lift the lid too late. I will be walking with no more intention than a spoon has ambition, and then, without any vote being taken, I find my feet hurrying me through the city’s din—through those thoroughfares where the naphtha lamps flare like cheap stage-lighting, and faces shine with that patient, lead-coloured resolve London polishes onto people as a cook polishes pewter with salt and vinegar. I pass into dimmer streets where blinds swell and shrink with monstrous shadows; into narrow, noisome lanes where gutters simmer with children, and every open doorway pours out riot as though some unseen hand keeps topping it up.

And always the sound comes first. Wheels gnash. Hooves strike. A distant quarrel rises and falls like a kettle trying to decide whether it will boil. A barrel organ begins a tune, and is murdered midway by laughter. Then, as I near my corner, the noises thin, not because the world grows gentler, but because my memory grows louder.

There the house waits—larger than its neighbours, older by a generation of soot. It was built when this street was still a country lane edging marshes; when a man could hear reeds hissing and the sea muttering to itself, instead of iron shrieking and commerce congratulating its own voice. Even now, if you stand close and refuse to be hurried, you can catch beneath the modern racket a quieter under-sound, like the last trace of a dialect on a tongue that has been bullied into speaking properly.

Two sides of it are encompassed by what was once a garden, though calling it that now is like calling dripping tea-leaves a banquet. It is a barren patch of stones and dust where clothes hang in perpetual surrender, flapping and snapping like flags for an army that has mislaid its cause. About the door remain the bones of a porch; the stucco has fallen away and left the place’s naked insincerity frankly on show—an old hypocrite in its shirt-sleeves.

Sometimes I drift here by day. In daylight, the house plays at not knowing me. Slatternly women gossip round the area gates. A cry goes up, hoarse and wailing—“Coals—any coals—three and sixpence a sack—co-o-o-als!”—chanted with the chronic melancholy that comes of calling into ears that have learned to shut like purses. The sun, unpitying, makes every stain confess. The house, ashamed of its own shabbiness, turns its face away and will not see me as I pass.

Not until Night—merciful to the ugly and therefore, in London, very busy—draws her veil across the street’s sordid features does the old place soften. Then, like some fond nurse remembered after years, it opens wide its arms to welcome me. The life it now shelters quiets for a time behind its walls. The “King of Prussia” opposite lets its last flare die out. And the house begins to talk.

It talks not in sentences, but in footsteps and creaks, in draughts that stir old smells from cracks and corners: damp brick, worn wood, cheap paraffin, stale beer blown across from the public-house, and—faintly, stubbornly—something domestic that refuses extinction, like toast once made on a grudging fire. The floors are thick with the ghost-pressure of feet. The walls are full of voices. I tell you, a violin long played upon learns a music of its own; and a house long lived in—long argued in, long wept in, long laughed in—learns a speech that has no dictionary.

Then into the silent street come the well-remembered footsteps; in and out the creaking gate pass, not seeing me, the well-remembered faces. We talk of them as two cronies turn the torn leaves of an old album where faded portraits, in forgotten fashions, lean forward to whisper. And we do it with that peculiar tenderness old things have: a smile, a sigh, and many an “Ah me,” and “Dear, dear,” as though the dead were merely late to supper.

A bent, worn man comes towards us with quick, impatient steps, yet every fifty yards or so he stops, leaning heavily on his high Malacca cane, as if even his impatience must pause to catch its breath.

“It is a handsome face, is it not?” I ask, gazing at him as the shadow frames it.

“Aye,” answers the old House, “handsome enough. Handsomer still before you and I knew it—before mean care furrowed it with fretful lines. Care is like bad salt: it draws the moisture out of everything and leaves it hard.”

“I never could make out,” the house continues, musingly, “whom you took after; for they were a handsome pair, your father and your mother—though Lord! what a couple of children!”

“Children!” I say, startled. For my father must have been past five and thirty before the house could have known him, and my mother’s face is close to mine in the darkness, so that I see the grey hairs mingling with the bonny brown.

“Children,” repeats the old House, irritably. Old folk—whether built of brick or bone—do not care to have their judgments questioned. “The most helpless pair of children I ever set eyes upon. Who but a child would conceive the notion of repairing his fortune by becoming a solicitor at thirty-eight? Or, having conceived such a notion, would select the outskirts of Poplar as a likely centre in which to put up his door-plate?”

“It was considered a rising neighbourhood,” I reply, a little resentful—because no son likes to hear the family wisdom criticised, even when, deep down, he suspects the critic is right. “All sorts and conditions of men, whose affairs were in connection with the sea, would, it was thought, come to reside hereabout, so as to be near the new docks; and had they, it is not unreasonable to suppose they would have quarrelled and disputed with one another, much to the advantage of a cute solicitor convenient to their hand.”

“Stuff and nonsense,” retorts the old House shortly. “Why, the mere smell of the place would have kept a sensible man away.” And then—if a brick façade can twist itself, it does; the grim face before me turns goblin. “And he, of all men, as ‘the cute solicitor,’ giving advice to shady clients eager to get out of trouble by the shortest way! Can you fancy it? He who for two years starved himself, living on five shillings a week—before you came to London, when he was here alone. Even your mother knew nothing of it till years afterwards—so that no man should be a penny the poorer for having trusted his good name. Do you think the crew of chandlers and brokers, dock-hustlers and freight-wreckers would have found him useful, even had they settled here?”

I have no answer; nor does the old House wait for one. It never did have patience with human slowness—having observed, for decades, the leisurely manner in which people ruin their own lives.

“And your mother!” it goes on. “Would any but a child have taken that soft-tongued wanton to her bosom, and not have seen through acting so transparent? Would any but the veriest child—never ought to have been let out alone—have thought to dree her weird in such folly? Children! Poor babies they were, both of them.”

“Tell me,” I say—for at such times all my stock of common sense cannot convince me the old House is mere clay and mortar. From its walls so full of voices, from its floors so thick with footsteps, surely it has learned to live. “Tell me—was there any truth in the story?”

“Truth!” snaps the old House. “Just truth enough to plant a lie upon; and Lord knows not much ground is needed for that weed. I saw what I saw, and I know what I know. Your mother had a good man, and your father a true wife—but it was the old story: a man’s way is not a woman’s way, and a woman’s way is not a man’s way, so there lives ever doubt between them.”

“But they came together in the end,” I say, remembering.

“Aye, in the end,” answers the House. “That is when you begin to understand, you men and women—when you come to the end.”

A small face peeps shyly at me through the railings—the grave face of an angel that has not been washed as recently as angels are supposed to be. The moment I turn my head it darts back and disappears, as if modesty were a game of hide-and-seek and Heaven played by London rules.

“What has become of her?” I ask.

“She? Oh, she’s well enough,” replies the House. “Lives close here. You must have passed the shop. You might have seen her had you looked in. She weighs fourteen stone, about; and has nine children living. She would be pleased to see you.”

“Thank you,” I say, with a laugh that is not wholly a laugh. “I do not think I will call.”

Yet I still hear, absurdly, the pit-pat of her tiny feet dying down the long street—sound lingering where sight fails, like a half-remembered phrase in a tongue you spoke before you learned the proper one.

The faces thicken round me. A large, looming, rubicund visage smiles kindly, and brings back into my heart that odd old mixture: instinctive liking held in check by conscientious disapproval, as though affection were a stew that must not be allowed to boil over respectability.

I turn from it, and see a massive, clean-shaven face with the ugliest mouth and the loveliest eyes I ever have known in a man.

“Was he as bad, do you think, as they said?” I ask my ancient friend.

“Shouldn’t wonder,” the old House answers. “I never knew a worse—nor a better.”

The wind whisks that face aside and shows me a little old woman hobbling nimbly by aid of a stick. Three corkscrew curls on each side of her head bob with every step, like punctuation marks dancing to an unheard tune. As she draws near, making the most alarming grimaces, I hear her whisper, as though confiding to herself some fascinating secret: “I’d like to skin ’em. I’d like to skin ’em all. I’d like to skin ’em all alive!”

It is a fiendish sentiment; and yet I only laugh, for memory has a strange palate and can find sweetness in the sharpest things. The little old lady, with one final contortion surpassing all dreams, limps beyond my ken.

Then, as though choosing contrasts, follows a fair, laughing face. I saw it in life only a few hours ago—at least, not it, but the poor daub Evil has painted over it, hating the sweetness underneath. I stand gazing, wishing it were among the dead who change not. And as I stand, there drifts back from the shadows that other face again—the wicked mouth and the tender eyes—so that once more I am helpless between the two I loved so well: he from whom I learned my first steps in manhood, she from whom I caught my first glimpse of the beauty and the mystery of woman.

Again the cry rises from my heart, as if it were an old chorus the house will always make me sing: Whose fault was it—yours or hers?

And again I hear his mocking laugh: “Whose fault? God made us.”

Thinking of her, and of the love I bore her—which was the love of a young pilgrim to a saint—it comes into my blood to hate him. But when I look into his eyes and see the pain that lives there, my pity grows stronger than my misery, and I can only echo his words: “God made us.”

Merry faces and sad; fair faces and foul; they ride upon the wind. Yet the centre round which they circle remains always the same: a little lad with golden curls more suitable to a girl than to a boy, with shy, awkward ways and a silent tongue, and a grave, old-fashioned face. He stands there as the kernel of the whole business—small, quiet, not understanding the language life speaks in, yet obliged to live by it all the same, like a child handed a foreign menu and told to order for the table.

Turning from him to my old brick friend, I ask, softly: “Would he know me, could he see me, do you think?”

“How should he?” answers the old House. “You’re so different to what he would expect. Would you recognise your own ghost, think you?”

“It is sad to think he would not recognise me,” I say.

“It might be sadder if he did,” grumbles the House.

We are both silent awhile. I know what the House is thinking; it always thinks the same thing when it has worked itself into tenderness.

Soon it speaks, as I expected. “You—writer of stories—why don’t you write a book about him? There is something that you know.”

It is the old House’s favourite theme. I never visit but it suggests it, like an elderly relative who cannot meet you without remarking that you look pale and ought to eat more.

“But he has done nothing,” I object.

“He has lived,” answers the old House. “Is not that enough?”

“Aye, but only in London, in these prosaic modern times,” I persist. “How of such can one make a story that shall interest the people?”

The old House waxes impatient. “‘The people!’” it retorts. “What are you all but children in a dim-lit room, waiting until one by one you are called out to sleep? And one mounts upon a stool and tells a tale to the others gathered round. Who shall say what will please them, what will not?”

So I leave it and go home with musing footsteps through softly breathing streets. Even the city, when it pauses between its louder hours, has a kind of hush that might be called peace if it were not so full of distant machinery clearing its throat. I ponder the house’s words. Is it only an old nurse thinking all the world must be interested in her nursling, or is there wisdom in this counsel?

Then, to my guidance or misguidance, comes the thought of a certain small section of the Public who often of an evening commands of me a story; and who, when I have told her of dreadful giants and gallant youths who slay them, of woodcutter’s sons rescuing maidens from Ogre-guarded castles; of princesses the most beautiful in the world, of princes with magic swords—still unsatisfied creeps closer, saying, “Now tell me a real story,” and, for my comprehending, adds: “You know—about a little girl who lived in a big house with her father and mother, and who was sometimes naughty, you know.”

So perhaps among the many there may be some who, for a moment, will turn aside from tales of haughty Heroes ruffling it in Court and Camp, to listen to the story of a very ordinary lad who lived with very ordinary folk in a modern London street, and who grew up to be a very ordinary sort of man—loving a little and grieving a little, helping a few and harming a few, struggling and failing and hoping.

If any such there be, let them come round me.

But let none grow indignant as they listen, saying, “This rascal tells us but a humdrum story, where nothing is as it should be,” for I warn all beforehand: I tell but of things that I have seen. My villains, I fear, are but poor sinners, not altogether bad; and my good men but sorry saints. My princes do not always slay their dragons; alas, sometimes the dragon eats the prince. The wicked fairies often prove more powerful than the good. The magic thread leads sometimes wrong, and even the hero is not always brave and true.

So let those come round me only who will be content to hear but their own story told by another—saying as they listen, “So dreamt I. Ah, yes, that is true, I remember.”

Fate, having a taste for practical jokes, arranged that I should be born in May. June, everybody knows, is the month in which babies ought to make their debut if they have any regard for comfort and reputation; in June the world has a kind word ready, the air smells of cut hay, and the very sun seems to say, “Come in, my boy; we’ve been expecting you.” But May—May is the month that looks you over, sniffs, and says, “Oh. You.”

Mrs. Fursey—my nurse and the chief spokeswoman of Providence in our household—always insisted my arrival was premature because I had been impatient to begin. She was a thin, knobbly woman, full of angles and convictions, with a thimble that clicked like a beetle’s jaw and a mind that kept superstitions as other people keep jam: tightly corked, carefully hoarded, and brought out on special occasions to spread upon everything.

The special occasion for my coming, as she explained it, was that news had reached the house two hours earlier of the ruin of my father’s mines by inundation. My poor mother, in attempting to reach the bell during the ensuing agitation, fell against a cheval-glass and broke it—thus, Mrs. Fursey assured me, sealing the first seven years of my existence with disaster, because Welsh blood, like Welsh cakes, holds a great deal in it that no amount of kneading will ever get out.

“And I must confess,” she added, sighing, “it do look as though there might be truth in the saying, after all.”

This was a new tone. Hitherto her system had been to persuade me I was an exceptionally lucky child. For example: I could retire to bed at six o’clock—while less favoured boys were allowed to remain up until eight or nine, growing old and haggard on the carpet. Also, at the first sniffle of a cold, it became my privilege to lunch on linseed gruel and sup on brimstone and treacle—the treacle being, as I discovered, a mere decoy sent forward to draw the innocent into range, while the brimstone did the actual fighting. Other boys, she said, were left alone when they were ill. Poor, neglected creatures—how I envied them in my wilder moments.

So at her sudden hint that luck might not be my natural atmosphere, I stared at her in honest alarm.

“Then ain’t I a lucky little boy?” I asked.

The good woman saw she had slipped a stitch.

“Oh, you—you’re lucky enough,” she said hurriedly. “I was thinking of your poor mother.”

“Isn’t mamma lucky?”

“Well, she hasn’t been too lucky since you came.”

“Wasn’t it lucky, her having me?”

“I can’t say it was, at that particular time.”

“Didn’t she want me?”

Mrs. Fursey belonged to the school of thought that holds childhood should enter a room apologising, remain apologising, and leave apologising.

“Well,” she said, “I daresay she could have done without you.”

I remember the scene as clearly as if it were still sizzling in the grate. I sat on a low chair by the nursery fire, one knee hugged in my locked hands, while Mrs. Fursey’s needle scratched and clicked against her thimble with the steady industry of an insect. Outside, I could hear the house itself—the minute domestic orchestra: the far clink of a pan-lid, the whisper of coals settling, a sparrow’s sudden quarrel in the ivy. All sound seemed to lean in, attentive.

And into my small soul knocked, for the first time, the large question.

Without moving, I asked:

“Then why did she take me in?”

The thimble stopped. Silence fell so sharply I could hear the fire give a little sigh.

“Took you in! What’s the child talking about? Who’s took you in?”

“Why, mamma. If she didn’t want me, why did she take me in?”

Even while I asked it—dignified, resentful, certain I had cornered all grown-up logic—I felt an odd gratitude that she had. My imagination leapt to the terrible alternative: myself at the bedroom window, refused like a parcel without a label. I saw the stork—large, solemn, officious—holding me out by the neck as Tom Pinfold held out a fish by the tail, only to have Anna sniff once and shut the kitchen door in his face. Would the stork, too, have gone away scratching his head with one of his long compass-legs, muttering, “Well, I never,” and carrying me elsewhere?

And how had he carried me? In the garden I had watched a blackbird with a worm, and the worm, though perhaps safe enough, always looked most unhappy—writhing with a sort of polite protest. Had I wriggled in like fashion? Had I made myself disagreeable? And where would the stork have taken me then? Perhaps to Mrs. Fursey’s cottage; it stood nearest. But I knew Mrs. Fursey would not have taken me in; she never took anything in unless it was lint. Next lived Mr. Chumdley the cobbler, a lame man in a half-underground shop, hammering boots with hands as black as treacle before it’s been sweetened. He looked like a poor-spirited ogre. I should have hated being his little boy. Perhaps nobody would have taken me. I grew pensive at the thought of being rejected by the whole village—an unwanted morsel, like a crust nobody quite trusts.

This produced a new question, which I asked in a low voice, lest the answer should be as chilly as the thought.

“Nurse, where did I come from?”

“Why, I’ve told you often. The stork brought you.”

“Yes, I know. But where did the stork get me from?”

Mrs. Fursey paused a long time. Perhaps she feared conceit. But even she saw that a boy cannot be left in mid-air.

“Oh,” she said at last, “from Heaven.”

“But I thought Heaven was a place where you went to,” I objected, “not where you comed from.” I remember my “comed” because at that time my irregular verbs were a standing scandal to my mother. “Goned,” too, was a favourite; I had forged it myself, with the pride of an inventor.

Mrs. Fursey made a point of ignoring my grammar. She had been told not to meddle in that department; my mother held that conflicting opinions only muddled a child—an observation that proved, in practice, a good deal wider than verbs.

“You came from Heaven,” repeated Mrs. Fursey, “and you’ll go to Heaven—if you’re good.”

“Do all little boys and girls come from Heaven?”

“So they say,” she answered, in a tone suggesting she would not stake her thimble on it.

“And did you come from Heaven, Mrs. Fursey?”

“Of course I did. Where do you think I came from?”

At once, Heaven suffered a serious loss of prestige. I had already wondered why everybody I knew was going there—so I was always assured, though I noticed no particular eagerness in the preparations. Now, linked as it was with Mrs. Fursey’s origin, Heaven seemed less a radiant country and more a sort of depot that issued nurses.

But her statement stirred up another sorrow in me, sharp as vinegar.

“Didn’t they want me in Heaven then, either?” I asked. “Weren’t they fond of me up there?”

The wretchedness in my voice must have penetrated even Mrs. Fursey’s well-padded certainty.

“Oh, they liked you well enough, I daresay,” she said, softening a little. “I like you—but I like to get rid of you sometimes.”

There was no disputing that. Even then I suspected that my famous six o’clock bedtime occasionally crept back toward half-past five with the stealth of a cat.

Her comfort did not comfort me. The facts seemed plain: God did not want me; He was glad to get rid of me. My mother did not want me; she could have done without me. Nobody wanted me. Why was I here?

And then, sudden as the opening and shutting of a door into a lit room, there came into me the sense that Something—somewhere—must have need of me, or I could not be. Something I belonged to, and that belonged to me; Something as much part of me as I of it. I could not have named it, but it warmed my chest like a swallowed spark. Years later, the son of a Portuguese Jew would give words to what I had felt. At that moment I only knew, very distinctly, that I was alive, and that I was I.

The next instant the door shut, and I was again merely a puzzled boy by the nursery fire, faced with life and Mrs. Fursey.

A recollection—half overheard, half invented—bounced into my head.

“Nurse, why haven’t we got a husband?”

Mrs. Fursey stared as if I had suggested we keep one in the coal-scuttle.

“What maggot has the child got into its head now?” she demanded. “Who hasn’t got a husband?”

“Why, mamma.”

“Don’t talk nonsense, Master Paul. You know your mamma has got a husband.”

“No, she ain’t.”

“And don’t contradict. Your mamma’s husband is your papa, who lives in London.”

“What’s the good of him!”

“You wicked child, you! Where’s your commandments? Your father is in London working hard to earn money to keep you in idleness, and you sit there and say, ‘What’s the good of him!’ I’d be ashamed to be such an ungrateful little brat.”

I had not meant ingratitude. I was only repeating words I had heard the day before between my mother and my aunt—those two, who could quarrel as naturally as kettles can boil.

My aunt had said, with her cheerful venom: “There she goes, moping again. Drat me if ever I saw such a thing to mope as a woman.”

My aunt was qualified to speak. She grumbled all day about everything, but did it with a bright face—like a person singing a dirge to a dance tune.

My mother had been standing, hands clasped behind her (a favourite pose), looking out through the high French window into the garden. It must have been spring; I remember crocuses—white and yellow—stuck in the grass like little careful flames.

“I want a husband,” my mother had answered in a voice so childishly ridiculous I had looked up from my fairy story, half expecting her to have shrunk into a little girl. “I hate not having a husband.”

“Help us and save us,” said my aunt. “How many more does a girl want? She’s got one.”

“What’s the good of him all that way off,” my mother pouted. “I want him here where I can get at him.”

I had heard of this father of mine often enough—he existed, apparently, somewhere in London, and to him we owed the blessings of bread, butter, and respectability. But my childish mind, trying to fit information to experience, had made him into something rather like a hymn: always spoken of, never seen, impressive but intangible. I agreed with my mother that such a being, however revered, was no substitute for the flesh-and-blood fathers other boys possessed—the big masculine thing that hoisted one pig-a-back round the garden or took one to sail boats.

“You don’t understand me, nurse,” I explained carefully; “what I mean is a husband you can get at.”

“Well, and you’ll ‘get at him,’ poor gentleman, one of these days,” said Mrs. Fursey. “When he’s ready for you he’ll send for you, and then you’ll go to him in London.”

I saw she still did not understand, but I suspected that spelling it out would make her drop her thimble in horror. So I asked, very plainly:

“How do you get to London; do you have to die first?”

“I do think,” said Mrs. Fursey, in the tone of resigned despair, “that, without exception, you are the silliest little boy I ever came across. I’ve no patience with you.”

“I am very sorry, nurse,” I began. “I thought—”

“Then,” she interrupted, speaking with the voice of many generations, “you shouldn’t think. London,” she continued—because her experience told her that peace is best purchased with information—“is a big town, and you go there in a train. Some time—soon now—your father will write to your mother that everything is ready. Then you and your mother and your aunt will leave this place and go to London, and I shall be rid of you.”

“And shan’t we come back here ever any more?”

“Never again.”

“Then I’ll never play in the garden again, never go down to the pebble-ridge to tea, or to Jacob’s tower?”

“Never again.” She seemed to savour the phrase; it came out like something from a prayer-book: solemn, final, and faintly enjoyable.

“And I’ll never see Anna, or Tom Pinfold, or old Yeo, or Pincher, or you, ever any more?”

“Never any more. You’ll go away and begin an entirely new life. And I do hope, Master Paul,” she added, warming to moral instruction, “it may be a better one. That you will make up your mind to—”

Whatever improvement she wished me to make up my mind to, I did not hear. The fire hissed softly as a sap-log broke; outside, the house gave its usual domestic sounds—yet suddenly they seemed like the noises of a place one is leaving for ever: the clink of crockery that would go on clinking without me, the footstep in the passage that would learn to forget my name. My whole world, with its garden and pebble-ridge and tower and people and dog, was being taken up like a cloth after tea and shaken into nothing.

One went away, leaving pleasant places, never to return. One left one’s play and one’s friends and the familiar noises of one’s own rooms, and stepped into a strange land. Life was indeed odd; and to understand it one must sit very still and stare into the fire while grown-ups talked nonsense overhead.

That night, when my mother came to kiss me good-night, I turned my face to the wall and pretended to be asleep, as children do when they are offended with the universe. But when I felt her soft curls brush my cheek, my pride melted like butter near a warm plate. I caught her neck in my arms and pulled her face down close to mine.

“I suppose you couldn’t send me back now, could you?” I whispered. “You see, you’ve had me so long.”

“Send you back?”

“Yes. I’d be too big for the stork to carry now, wouldn’t I?”

My mother knelt by the bed till our faces were level. Her eyes—always too large for her childish face—held mine, and the fear that had been worrying me fell away as if it had been only a bad dream.

“Who has been talking foolishly to a foolish little boy?” she asked, keeping my arms clasped round her.

“Oh, nurse and I were discussing things,” I said, “and she said you could have done without me.” Now it sounded absurd; clearly it had been only Mrs. Fursey’s way of seasoning conversation.

My mother drew me closer.

“And what made her think that?”

“Well,” I admitted, “I came at a very awkward time, didn’t I; when you had a lot of other troubles.”

My mother laughed—then grew grave, as she often did, like sunshine suddenly shaded by a cloud.

“I did not know you thought about such things,” she said. “We must be more together, you and I, Paul, and you shall tell me all you think, because nurse does not quite understand you. It is true there was trouble; it came just at that time. But I could not have done without you. I was very unhappy, and you were sent to comfort me and help me to bear it.”

This was better. It made me feel less like an unwanted parcel.

“Then it was lucky, your having me?” I asked.

Again she laughed; again that earnest look came, as if she were about to say something that mattered more than fairy tales.

“Will you remember what I am going to say?” she asked.

I wriggled up to sit, solemn with importance.

“I’ll try,” I said. “But I ain’t got a very good memory, have I?”

“Not very,” she smiled. “But if you think about it a good deal it will not leave you. When you are a good boy, and later on, when you are a good man, then I am the luckiest little mother in all the world. And every time you fail, that means bad luck for me. You will remember that after I’m gone, when you are a big man, won’t you, Paul?”

So, with two earnest childish faces close together in the lamplight, I promised. I smile now at the scene; yet I think—though I have little to boast of—there might have been even less had I forgotten altogether.

From that day my mother grows in my memory, and Mrs. Fursey, with all her promontories, recedes. We had sunny mornings in the neglected garden, where leaves made a soft shifting music above our heads and the air smelled of damp earth and crushed green things; twilight evenings in the window seat, half hidden behind dark red curtains, where we talked in whispers (why we whispered I never knew) of good men and noble women, ogres and fairies, saints and demons. The house had its own soundscape then, a kindly one: bees blundering among blossoms, a distant cart on the lane, the small applause of sparrows in the hedge—no shriek of machinery, no iron roar of progress. Our thoughts, like our days, were allowed to have quiet.

Possibly our education lacked method. My mother’s mind was a pantry into which everything delightful had been put—legends, poems, history, nursery tales—without labels, and when she cooked for me she seasoned freely, so that chronology became a stew where kings, heroes, and nursery villains bobbed up together. Aphrodite rose from the sea ready armed to meet King Canute, who sat throned upon the sand and bade her come no nearer lest she wet his feet. In a forest glade I saw King Rufus fall to a poisoned arrow shot by Robin Hood; but sweet Queen Eleanor sucked the poison from his wound, and so he lived. Oliver Cromwell, having killed King Charles, married his widow and was stabbed by Hamlet. Ulysses, in the Argo, discovered America. Romulus and Remus slew the wolf and rescued Little Red Riding Hood. Good King Arthur, for letting the cakes burn, was murdered by his uncle in the Tower of London. Prometheus, bound to the Rock, was saved by good St. George. Paris gave the apple to William Tell.

What did it matter? The world was full. It needed only rearranging—like a cupboard after a feast.

Sometimes of an afternoon we climbed the steep winding path through the woods, past awful precipices that seemed made for ghosts to practise on, by grassy swards where fairies danced o’ nights, by briar and bracken sheltering caves in which, I was sure, fearsome creatures lived waiting for someone to ask the wrong question. High above the creeping sea we reached the open plateau where old Jacob’s ruined tower stood. “Jacob’s Folly” they called it, and some “The Devil’s Tower,” for legend said old Jacob met his master there in windy weather to wave false wrecking lights to troubled ships. Who Jacob had been, or why he built the thing, I never learned; only that the fishermen hated his name, and swore that on stormy nights strange lights still flickered behind the ivy-curtained windows.

But by day no place was more inviting. The short moss-grass before its broken door was springy underfoot; lichen freckled its stones like flour on a baker’s board. From the top platform you could see distant mountains faint as spectres, and silent ships appearing and vanishing; below lay farm lands and the grave sweet river, winding through them with a soundless patience.

Smaller and poorer the world has grown since then. Now behind those hills lie smoky towns and dingy villages; but then they screened a country of wonder where princesses dwelt in castles and cities shone with gold. Now the ocean is a matter of six days and a Custom House. Then, had one set sail upon it, one would have travelled far beyond moonlight, beyond the gate of clouds, to the magic land of the blood-red shore, t’other side o’ the sun. I never dreamt in those days that a world could be made so mean by measuring it.

On the topmost platform ran a wooden seat inside the parapet, and there, hand in hand, sheltered from the wind that always worried the tower, my mother would people earth and air with myth and legend. Perhaps unwisely; I do not know. They were beautiful fancies—or she made them so, turning them always toward love and pity, as do the tales that live. Often, looking into my eyes, she would remember herself and add, hastily respectable: “But that, you know, is only an old superstition, and of course there are no such things nowadays.” Yet sometimes, overtaken by shadows on the homeward path, we would hurry quickly through the dim woods holding tightly to each other’s hands, and the forest’s noises—owl, twig-snap, the rush of unseen wings—would make even her whisper.

Spring fattened into summer; summer thinned into autumn. Then one morning my aunt and I, sitting at breakfast, saw my mother through the open window come skipping up the garden path. She held a letter open in her hand and waved it above her head, singing:

“Saturday, Sunday, Monday, Tuesday, then comes Wednesday morning.”

She caught me up and began dancing with me round the room.

My aunt, steadily eating bread and butter, observed as if addressing the ceiling: “Just like ’em all. Goes mad with joy. What for? Because she’s going to leave a decent house to live in a poky hole in the East End of London, and keep one servant.”

My aunt regarded the second person as a grammatical luxury; she spoke not to people but about them, flinging opinions like dish-water. It allowed the victim, if self-respecting, to pretend it was impersonal philosophy. Unfortunately my mother had never learned that art.

“It’s not a poky hole,” she retorted. “It’s an old-fashioned house, near the river.”

“Plaistow marshes!” snorted my aunt. “Calls it the river!”

“So it is the river,” said my mother. “The river is the other side of the marshes.”

“Let’s hope it will always stop there,” said my aunt.

“And it’s got a garden,” my mother went on, refusing to taste the poison. “Which is quite an unusual feature in a London house. And it isn’t the East End; it is a rising suburb. And you won’t make me miserable because I am too happy.”

“Drat the woman!” said my aunt. “Why can’t she sit down and give us our tea before it’s all cold?”

“You are a disagreeable thing!” cried my mother.

“Not half milk,” said my aunt, untroubled. My aunt’s peace was the peace of a person who can always find something wrong and therefore is never surprised.

For three days my mother packed and sang; and a dozen times a day unpacked and laughed, hunting for things that were always at the bottom of the very last box she looked into. Anna, waiting for a certain undergarment of my aunt’s which shall remain nameless out of respect for the dead and for laundry, offered practical wisdom.

“If I were you, ma’am,” said Anna, “I’d look into the last box you’re going to look into first.”

The garment was eventually found in the first box—meaning the one my mother had meant to search first, but, following Anna’s suggestion, had nobly reserved for the last. This caused my mother to be quite short with Anna on the subject of wasted time.

By Tuesday afternoon everything stood ready. We were to start early Wednesday morning.

That evening, missing my mother indoors, I went to the garden and found her, as I expected, on her favourite seat under the great lime tree. The leaves above her made a soft, whispering roof, and the whole place smelled of green sweetness. Yet to my surprise there were tears in her eyes.

“But I thought you were glad we were going,” I said.

“So I am,” she answered, wiping her eyes only to make room for more.

“Then why are you crying?”

“Because I’m sorry to leave here.”

Grown-up people with their contradictory ways were a continual puzzle to me then. I am not sure I understand them even now—myself included.

The case of my father and mother was, from the beginning, a dish that refused to follow any printed recipe. They had been separated for years; and yet, when I came upon them together in that big corner house, it was as if somebody had taken two people of nearly forty and nearly thirty, dusted them with flour, and—by some trick of heat and hope—raised them back into boy and girl. Seen through a child’s eyes, they were not “middle-aged.” They were simply “old enough to be allowed to do things,” and at the same time young enough to do them.

Had any enquiring bachelor or maiden asked my opinion of marriage, I should have described it as a condition in which the husband cannot sit still in an office unless his wife is within calling distance, like salt within reach of the hand. Twenty times a day he would cry from behind his door:

“Maggie, are you doing anything important? I want to talk to you about a matter of business.”

… “Maggie, are you alone? Oh, all right, I’ll come down.”

And I should have said the wife is a woman whose eyes, when they fall upon her man, catch light the way a saucepan catches flame; who is glad where he is and troubled where he is not. But of course that would have been only our household’s method, and no more to be trusted as a general rule than Susan’s method of making tea.

I should also have offered advice on the alarms and excursions incidental to living with a married couple still practicing their happiness in public. I would have urged visitors to keep their own feet tucked away under the table, lest—mistaken for other feet—they should be trodden upon and pressed with meaning. And I would have warned people against entering any room unheralded by what the theatre calls “noise without.” It is trying to the nerves to turn a handle, step in, and be greeted by the sound of two people springing apart like startled pigeons, followed by a wild scuttling, and then to find them sitting stiffly in opposite corners, deep in book or needlework, as if they had always been there and had never in their lives heard of cushions.

Personally, I exercised little influence. My sudden appearances came to be met by, “Oh, it’s only Spud,” in a tone of relief, generally accompanied by a sofa cushion, as if I were not so much a child as a small domestic inconvenience that happened to be beloved. But my aunt—Aunt Fan—was a different matter. They stood in awe of her without knowing why, and she obliged them by doing everything possible to keep their imaginations from having to work.

To spare their feelings she erred, if at all, on the side of excess. She never moved a footstep about the house except to the music of a sustained and penetrating cough. She could have coughed the roof off a workhouse. My father once remarked—ungratefully, I must confess—that the total quantity of bark produced by my aunt in a day would have done credit to the dying efforts of a hospital-load of consumptives; and that for a robust, perfectly healthy lady the nervous expenditure must be prodigious. Also, that no fear might live with them that her eyes had seen aught not intended, she had a habit of entering any room backwards, closing the door loudly and with difficulty before turning round. In dark passages she would walk singing, so that the very wallpaper might bear witness to her innocence.

No woman alive could have done more; yet—such is human nature—neither my father nor my mother seemed grateful. Indeed, the more sympathy she showed, the more irritated they became, as if sympathy were pepper applied to a bruise.

“I believe, Fanny, you hate seeing Luke and me happy together,” said my mother one day, coming up from the kitchen to find my aunt preparing for entry into the drawing-room by dropping teaspoons at five-second intervals outside the door. “Don’t make yourself so ridiculous.”

“Hate it!” returned my aunt. “Why should I? Why shouldn’t a pair of turtle-doves bill and coo, when their united age is only a little over seventy, the pretty dears?”

Her mildness used to astonish me. She had the gift, common in elders and in cows, of answering with complete composure when any other creature would have tossed its horns.

My father, on the other hand, grew positively vindictive, and I remember well the first time I knew him lose his temper. We were walking by the canal bank. What brought it on I forget; with my father one seldom knew what would be the spark, because he always looked as though he were made of good temper, until suddenly there would be a little explosion and then he would be ashamed of the smoke.

“Your aunt—” My father may not have intended it, but whenever he spoke of my aunt his tone conveyed the impression that he held me personally responsible for her existence. This used to weigh upon me. “Your aunt is the most cantankerous, the most—” He broke off and shook his fist towards the setting sun, as if it had been in correspondence with her. “I wish to God your aunt had a comfortable little income of her own, with a freehold cottage in the country—by God I do!”

And then, ashamed of his brutality, he softened at once, like butter meeting heat.

“Not but what sometimes, of course, she can be very nice, you know,” he added. “Don’t tell your mother what I said just now.”

Another who followed our domestic comedy with sympathetic interest was Susan, our maid-of-all-work—the first of a long and varied series, extending unto the advent of Amy, to whom the blessing of Heaven. Susan was stout and elderly, liable to sudden fits of sleepiness, the result, we were given to understand, of “trouble.” Her heart, she told us with pride, was always in the right place. I cannot say of her hands that they always were.

She could never look at my father and mother sitting anywhere near each other without flopping down and weeping awhile, the sight of connubial bliss reminding her of the past glories of her own married state. These outbursts were frequently timed to occur over vegetables.

Being an earnest enquirer, I attempted to grasp the ins and outs of Susan’s past. I never succeeded. Whether her answers were framed to elude curiosity or whether they were merely the product of a naturally incoherent mind, I cannot say; but their tendency was to convey confusion, as if her history had been written in a dialect she herself no longer spoke and was now trying to translate into ours with missing words.

On Monday I have seen Susan shed tears of regret into Brussels sprouts because she had been debarred by pressure of other duties from lately watering “his” grave, which, I gathered, was at Manor Park. On Tuesday I have listened, blood chilled, to her recital of what she meant to do should she ever again enjoy the luxury of getting her fingers near the scruff of his neck.

“But I thought, Susan, he was dead,” was my very natural comment.

“So did I, Master Paul,” replied Susan. “That was his artfulness.”

“Then he isn’t buried in Manor Park Cemetery?”

“Not yet; but he’ll wish he was, the half-baked monkey, when I get hold of him.”

“Then he wasn’t a good man?”

“Who?”

“Your husband.”

“Who says he ain’t a good man?” Her flying leaps from tense to tense bewildered me more than irregular verbs. “If anybody says he ain’t I’ll gouge their eye out!”

I hastened to assure her I had been enquiring, not asserting.

“Brings me a bottle of gin—for my headaches—every time he comes home,” she continued, as if producing documentary evidence. “Every blessed time.”

And at some such point I would retire to the clearer atmosphere of German grammar or mixed fractions, which at least did not threaten to gouge my eye out.

We suffered a good deal from Susan in one way and another; for, having regard to the admirable position of her heart, we all felt it our duty to overlook mere failings of the flesh—except Aunt Fan, who never pretended to be a sentimentalist.

“She’s a lazy hussy,” announced my aunt one morning while rinsing. “A gulping, snorting, lazy hussy, that’s what she is.”

There was some excuse for her indignation. It was then eleven o’clock and Susan was still sleeping off an attack of what she called “new-ralgy.”

“She has seen a good deal of trouble,” said my mother, wiping.

“And if she was my cook and housemaid,” replied my aunt, “she would see more, the slut!”

“She’s not a good servant in many respects,” admitted my mother, “but I think she’s good-hearted.”

“Oh, drat her heart,” snapped my aunt. “The right place for that heart of hers is on the doorstep. And that’s where I’d put it—and her and her box alongside it—if I had my way.”

Susan’s departure did occur not long afterwards, and it came with the suddenness of a pan left too long on the fire. It was on a Saturday night. My mother came upstairs pale.

“Luke,” she said, “do please run for the doctor.”

“What’s the matter?” asked my father.

“Susan,” gasped my mother. “She’s lying on the kitchen floor breathing in the strangest fashion and quite unable to speak.”

“I’ll go for Washburn,” said my father. “If I am quick I shall catch him at the dispensary.”

Five minutes later my father came back panting, followed by the doctor.

This was a big black-bearded man who had the knack of seeming bigger than he was—partly by sheer bulk, partly by the way he arrived as if the world were a door that needed slamming. He came down the kitchen stairs two at a time, shaking the whole house, and with him came a change in the sound of everything: the boards seemed to creak in a different key, the very air in the stairwell thickened as if it were bracing itself.

He brushed my mother aside, bent over the unconscious Susan—who lay on her back with her mouth wide open—rose, looked at my father and mother with their troubled faces, and then opened his own mouth.

Out of it came a roar of laughter the like of which I had never heard.

The next moment he had seized a pail half full of water and flung it over Susan. She opened her eyes and sat up, blinking and dripping like a surprised seal.

“Feeling better?” said the doctor, still holding the pail. “Have another dose?”

Susan began to gather herself for speech—the thick kind that comes from the bottom of the chest and not from the top of the mind—but before she could get out her first word he had pushed the three of us outside and slammed the door behind us.

From the top of the stairs we could hear Susan’s rancorous voice raging fiercer and fiercer, drowned every now and then by the man’s savage laughter. And whenever she flagged for breath he would yell encouragement, as if conducting a kennel.

“Bravo! Go it, my beauty, give it tongue! Bark, bark! I love to hear you.”

He applauded, clapped his hands, stamped his feet; the house became an instrument and he played it with noise. I remember thinking—though I had no words then for such thoughts—that there are sounds which feed you and sounds which merely fill you up. Aunt Fan’s cough had been “noise without,” a curtain drawn for decency. This was noise without mercy: a great industrial laughter, as if a whole factory had learned one joke and refused to stop repeating it.

“What a beast of a man,” said my mother.

“He is really a most interesting man when you come to know him,” explained my father.

“I don’t ever mean to know him,” replied my mother, stiffly.

But it is only concerning the past that we possess knowledge.

The riot below ceased at length. A new voice began—quiet, emphatic—speaking as if laying down terms in a language Susan could understand. Then we heard the doctor’s step again upon the stairs.

My mother held her purse open in her hand. As he entered she went forward with that trembling severity women use when they mean to be dignified and are uncertain whether the world will assist them.

“How much do we owe you, Doctor?” she asked.

He closed the purse and gently pushed it back.

“A glass of beer and a chop, Mrs. Kelver,” he answered, “which I am coming back in an hour to cook for myself.”

My mother stood staring, as if he had demanded a hymn-book.

“And as you will be without any servant,” he continued, “you had better let me cook some for you at the same time. I am an expert at grilling chops.”

“But, really, Doctor—” began my mother.

He laid his huge hand upon her shoulder, and my mother sat down upon the nearest chair as if the hand had turned her into furniture.

“My dear lady,” he said, “she’s a person you never ought to have had inside your house. She’s promised me to be gone in half an hour, and I’m coming back to see she keeps her word. Give her a month’s wages, and have a clear fire ready for me.”

And before my mother could reply he had slammed the front door. Even his leaving was loud.

“What a very odd sort of a man,” said my mother, when she recovered breath enough for criticism.

“He’s a character,” said my father. “You might not think it, but he’s worshipped about here.”

“I hardly know what to make of him,” said my mother. “I suppose I had better go out and get some chops,” which she did.

Susan went, sober as a judge on Friday, her great anxiety being to get out before the doctor returned; she fled his laughter as if it were a dog that had smelled her. The doctor himself arrived true to his time, and I lay awake—for no human being ever slept, or even quite remembered the desire to sleep, while Dr. Washburn was anywhere near—and listened to gusts of laughter sweeping through the house like sudden squalls down the chimney.

Even my aunt laughed that supper time. When the doctor laughed it seemed to me the bed shook under me. Not liking to be out of it, I did what spoilt little boys—and even spoilt little girls—sometimes do under similar stress: I wrapped the blanket round my legs and pattered downstairs, setting my face to express the sudden desire of a sensitive and possibly short-lived child for parents’ love.

My mother pretended to be angry, but that was only her company manners. Besides, I really had—if not exactly a pain—an extremely uncomfortable sensation (one common to me about that period) as of having swallowed the dome of St. Paul’s.

The doctor said it was frequent with children—the result of too early hours and too much study—and, taking me on his knee, he wrote then and there a diet chart for me, as if prescribing a new religion.

It included one tablespoonful of golden syrup four times a day, and one ounce of sherbet to be placed upon the tongue and taken neat ten minutes before each meal.

Even as a child I understood the importance of language. There are grown people who can offer you affection and still make it sound like a bill. Dr. Washburn could offer you syrup and sherbet and make it sound like a feast, like a secret heirloom passed down in the only dialect that mattered—taste.

That evening will always live in my remembrance. My mother was brighter than I had ever seen her. A flush sat on her cheek and a sparkle in her eye. She held a small painted screen to shield her face from the fire, and looking across at her I felt something new wake in me—the sense of beauty, born not from pictures but from the way firelight makes a face into a story.

I slipped down from the doctor’s knee, blanket still round me, and squatted on the edge of the fender where I could steal furtive glances up into her face when I thought no one was noticing. The room smelt of hot iron and singed fat, of beer opened and settling, of chops waiting their turn. In that smell there was comfort more convincing than any sermon: the promise that, for the next few minutes at least, nobody would go hungry.

My father too seemed bigger and more dignified, talking with a vigour and enjoyment that sat newly on him. Aunt Fan was quite witty and agreeable—for her. Even I asked one or two questions at which, for some reason, everybody laughed; which determined me to remember and ask those same questions again on some future occasion, as one keeps a successful seasoning.

That was the great charm of the man: by the magnetic spell of his magnificent vitality he drew from everyone their best. In his company clever people waxed into intellectual giants, while the dull sat amazed at their own originality. Conversing with him, Podsnap might have been piquant and Dogberry incisive.

But best of all was listening to his own talk. Of what he spoke I could tell you no more than the children of Hamelin could have told the tune the Pied Piper played. I only know that at the tangled music of his strong voice the walls of that mean room faded away, and beyond I saw a brave laughing world calling to me—a world full of joyous fight, where some won and some lost, and it mattered not a jot because whatever came of it there was a right royal game for all; a world where merry gentlemen feared neither life nor death, and Fate was but Master of the Revels.

And yet even then, beneath his laughter, I heard something else: the power of a voice to dominate a room, to make other voices fall into line or fall silent. Aunt Fan’s cough, my mother’s careful severity, Susan’s thick threats—each had been its own dialect, and here was a man who could translate them all into the one booming language everybody understood, whether they liked it or not. It was thrilling and slightly alarming, like hearing a brass band march through your own head.

Such was my first introduction to Dr. Washburn—or, to give him the name by which he was known in every slum and alley of that quarter, Dr. Fighting Hal. In a minor key that evening was an index to the whole man. Often he would wrinkle his nose like a dog before it bites, and then he was more brute than man—brutish in instinct, appetite, pleasure, fun. Or his deep blue eyes would grow soft as a mother’s, and then you might have thought him an angel in a soft felt hat and a coat so loose-fitting as to suggest the possibility of wings folded away underneath.

Often I have tried to make up my mind whether it has been better for me or worse that I ever came to know him; but as easy would it be for the tree to say whether the rushing winds and the wild rains have shaped it or mis-shaped.

Susan’s place remained vacant for some time. My mother explained to the few friends who occasionally came from afar to see us that her “housemaid” she had been compelled suddenly to discharge, and that we were waiting for the arrival of a new and better specimen. But the months passed and we still waited; and my father, on the rare days when a client would ring the office bell, would after pausing a decent interval open the front door himself, then call downstairs indignantly and loudly to know why “Jane” or “Mary” could not attend to their work.

And my mother, that the bread-boy or the milkman might not put it about the neighbourhood that the Kelvers in the big corner house kept no servant, would hide herself behind a thick veil and fetch all things herself from streets a long way off, as if food purchased near home tasted of disgrace.

For this family of whom I am writing were, I confess, weak and human. Their poverty they were ashamed of as though it were a crime; and in consequence their life was full of paltry and useless subterfuge. The larder, I fancy, was very often bare, but the port and sherry with the sweet biscuits stood always on the sideboard; and the fire had often to be low in the grate that my father’s tall hat might shine resplendent and my mother’s black silk rustle on Sundays.

But I would not have you sneer, thinking all pretence must spring from snobbishness and never from mistaken self-respect. There are fine gentlemen writers—men whose world is bounded on the east by Bond Street—who see in the struggles of poverty to hide its darns only matter for jest. I cannot laugh. I know the long hopes and fears that centre round the hired waiter; the long cost of the cream and the ice jelly ordered the week before from the confectioner’s. To me it is pathetic, not ridiculous. Heroism is not all of one pattern. Dr. Washburn, had the Prince of Wales come to see him, would have put his bread and cheese and jug of beer upon the table and helped His Royal Highness to half. But my father and mother’s tea was very weak that Mr. Jones or Mr. Smith might have a glass of wine should they come to dinner.

I remember the one egg for breakfast: my mother arguing that my father should have it because he had business to attend to; my father insisting that my mother should eat it, she having to go out shopping; a compromise being effected by their dividing it between them, each clamouring for the white as the most nourishing. The language of “nourishing” in our house was often only another way of saying “you first,” and there are sacrifices that cannot be translated without losing their taste.

However little the meal looked upon the table when we started, I always rose well satisfied.

To me this life came as a good time. That I was encouraged to eat treacle in preference to butter seemed admirable. Treacle has a smell like Sunday fairs and sticky fingers; butter is merely butter. Personally, I preferred sausages for dinner; and a supper of fried fish and potatoes, brought in stealthily in a carpet-bag, was infinitely more enjoyable than the set meal where nothing was of interest till one came to the dessert. The fish would steam up the bag with its vinegar breath; the potatoes would sweat and soften; and the whole thing had the excitement of contraband, as if we were smuggling a banquet past the customs officers of respectability.

What fun there was about it all. The cleaning of the doorstep by night, when from the ill-lit street a gentleman with a piece of sacking round his legs might very well pass for a somewhat tall charwoman. I would keep watch at the gate to give warning should any one that looked like a possible late caller turn the corner of the street, coming back now and then in answer to a low whistle to help my father grope about in the dark for the hearthstone. He was always mislaying the hearthstone, as if it too were ashamed to be found in such company.

How much better, helping to clean knives or running errands, than wasting all one’s morning dwelling upon the shocking irregularity of certain classes of French verbs; or making useless calculations as to how long X, walking four and a quarter miles an hour, would be overtaking Y, whose powers were limited to three and a half but who had started two and three quarter hours sooner—the whole argument reduced to sheer pedantry by reason of no information being afforded concerning the respective thirstiness of X and Y.

Even my father and mother were able to take it lightly, with plenty of laughter and no groaning that I ever heard. For over all lay the morning light of hope; and what prisoner, escaping from his dungeon, ever stayed to think of his torn hands and knees when beyond the opening he could see sunlight glinting through brambles?

“I had no idea,” said my mother, “there was so much to do in a house. In future I shall arrange for the servants to have regular hours, and a little time to themselves, for rest. Don’t you think it right, Luke?”

“Quite right,” replied my father. “And I’ll tell you another thing we’ll do. I shall insist on the landlord’s putting a marble doorstep to the next house we take; you pass a sponge over marble and it is always clean.”

“Or tesselated,” suggested my mother.

“Or tesselated,” agreed my father. “But marble is more uncommon.”

Only once can I recall a cloud, and it came—like many clouds—as a culinary proposal.

It was a Sunday. We were in the middle of dinner when my mother, speaking across the table, said, “We might save the rest of that stew, Luke; there’s an omelette coming.”

My father laid down his spoon very gently, as if an egg had cracked inside him.

“An omelette!”

“Yes,” said my mother. “I thought I would like to try again.”

My father stepped into the back kitchen—we dined in the kitchen as a rule; it saved much carriage—and returned with the wood chopper.

“What ever are you going to do, Luke, with the chopper?” asked my mother.

“Divide the omelette,” replied my father.

My mother began to cry.

“Why, Maggie—!” said my father, frightened at once, as men are frightened when a joke hits a place it was not aimed for.

“I know the other one was leathery,” sobbed my mother, “but it was the fault of the oven, you know it was, Luke.”

“My dear,” said my father, “I only meant it as a joke.”

“I don’t like that sort of joke,” said my mother. “It isn’t nice of you, Luke.”

To be candid, my mother did not like jokes against herself. Indeed, when I come to think of it, I have never met a woman who did, nor man either. Some words cannot be made harmless by laughter; they keep their edge like a badly washed knife.

There had grown up a comradeship between my father and myself, for he was the youngest thing I had met with as yet. Sometimes my mother seemed young, and later I met boys and girls nearer to my own age; but they grew, while my father remained always the same. The hair about his temples was turning grey, and when you looked close you saw crow’s-feet and lines, especially about the mouth; but his eyes were the eyes of a boy, his laugh the laugh of a boy, his heart the heart of a boy. So we were very close.

In a narrow strip of ground we called our garden we would play a cricket of our own, encompassed about by novel rules made necessary by locality: all hitting to leg forbidden as endangering neighbouring windows; hitting to off likewise discouraged as causing a temporary adjournment while batter and bowler went through the house and out into the street to recover the ball from some predatory crowd of urchins to whom it had evidently appeared as a gift direct from Heaven.

Sometimes, rising very early, we would walk across the marshes to bathe in a small creek that led down to the river—muddy work, necessitating much washing of legs on the return. And on rare days we would take the train to Hackney and walk to the bridge, and row up the river Lea, perhaps as far as Ponder’s End. And always, whether it was mud on the legs or treacle on the spoon, there ran beneath our days that mingled music of laughter and footsteps and doors, the sound of a household doing its best to be brave—and to sound, if possible, a little grander than it felt.

“Louisa!” my father’s voice came booming up the kitchen stairs like a kettle-drum in a church tower. “Are you all asleep? Here have I had to answer the front door myself!”

The house, which had been murmuring all morning with the small homely music of knives on boards and peas rattling into a basin, went still at that. Then came the slam of his office door—sharp as a cleaver on bone.

A quarter of an hour later his bell began to jangle: brisk, authoritative, and entirely out of tune with the kitchen’s gentler clinks. My mother, wrestling with potatoes in stiff wash-leather gloves, looked up; my aunt Fan, with her thumbs in a pile of pods, cocked her head like a fowl hearing thunder.

“Once for Louisa, twice for James, isn’t it?” my aunt observed, as though the bell were a code worth decoding.

The bell rang again, louder, angrier—metal scolding metal.

“You go, Paul,” said my mother. “Say that Louisa—” But the words stuck halfway; a flush ran over her face as if someone had opened the oven door. In a flutter she stripped off her gloves, and before I could set down my slate she had swept past me. “No—don’t stop your lessons. I’ll go myself.”

She went; and the kitchen resumed its work with a nervous haste. The potatoes, freed from their skins, looked pale and patient; the peas in my aunt’s bowl lay like green beads, silent witnesses.

Then, very softly, the kitchen door opened. My mother’s hand appeared through the crack and beckoned to me as though she were summoning an accomplice to a crime.

“Walk on your toes,” she whispered, and demonstrated at once.

I walked on my toes. The stairs, being old and having a conscience, creaked louder than usual—as if offended by secrecy and determined to publish it to the street. Up we went all the same, and into my parents’ bedroom, where the wardrobe—relic of grander days, smelling faintly of lavender and camphor—kept, like a shrine, my best suit; or, to be exact, my better.

Never had I worn it on a weekday morning. My mother brushed aside every question that wasn’t directly concerned with getting into it quickly. Buttons were attacked; collars were coaxed; and when I was complete, down to the new shoes—Bluchers, we called them then—she took my hand and we crept down again, silent, alert, and absurdly solemn.

“Shan’t I want my cap?” I breathed.

My mother shook her head as though caps belonged to ordinary life, and ordinary life had been suspended. She shut the street door with a bang; and at once I understood the pantomime. This sort of “business”—comic, tragic, or both—was becoming familiar in our house.

With my mother’s hand on my shoulder, we entered my father’s office.

To me, as a child, my father’s profession never had the look of anything serious. It seemed a game with props: big books that might as well have been castle walls, bundles of documents lavishly labelled and suspiciously blank, japanned tin boxes with thunderous titles and empty insides. “Sutton Hampden, Esq.” was, in practice, my mother’s work-box. “Drayton Estates” produced, so far as I could see, only apples; and “Mortgages,” I discovered much later, had nothing whatever to do with poems, though in my mind the word had always sounded as if it ought to rhyme with “gorgeous.”

Now my father rose to meet us and came forward, trying to look carved out of law and gravity, though his hair stood up in those familiar tufts that came of rumpling it while he talked. He always reminded me—more than I liked to admit—of an overgrown schoolboy playing at “business” for the amusement of smaller children.

“This is the youngster,” he said, and handed me along as if I were an exhibit. “Tall for his age, isn’t he?”

The man who received me was very fat, very prosperous, and very certain of it. He shifted a cigar of the most offensive smell from one corner of his thick mouth to the other, and held out a hand that closed round mine with a damp, confident grip. Its rings flashed; its nails were not as clean as the rings suggested they ought to be. He flapped me on the head with the other hand; the gesture was meant kindly, but it felt like a seal’s fin. In my natural history book a walrus had a face I had long distrusted; this gentleman’s hand gave me the same impression.

“Like ’is mother,” he remarked, his small eyes bright as two polished beads. “Wonderfully like ’is mother, ain’t ’e?” Then he winked one of those eyes at my father. “And that’s about the ’ighest compliment I can pay ’im, eh?”

When his eyes came back to me, I tried to think quickly of something pleasant about him. I had the uncomfortable feeling he could see straight through my thoughts, as through the frosted glass in a door.

“And where are you thinkin’ of sendin’ ’im?” he went on. “Eton or ’Arrow?”

“We haven’t quite made up our minds,” replied my father. “At present we are educating him at home.”

“You take my tip,” said the fat man, wagging the cigar as if it were a pointer on a blackboard, “and learn all you can. Look at me! If I’d ’ad the opportunity of being a schollard, I wouldn’t be here offerin’ your father an extravagant price for doin’ my work; I’d be able to do it myself.”

“You seem to have got on very well without it,” my father laughed; and indeed the man’s waistcoat rose and fell like a well-fed tide beneath a massive gold chain, and his fingers sparkled with rings as though his hands had been dipped in a jeweller’s tray.

“I’d ’ave done better with it,” he grunted.

“But you look very clever,” I said, which was meant to please him and also, in a child’s way, was true: cleverness, I then believed, was simply the quality of getting what you wanted and looking as if you deserved it.

He roared with laughter, and his whole body shook, wobbling like a great bowl of blancmange.

“Well, old Noel Hasluck’s not exactly a fool,” he conceded. “But I’d like myself better if I could talk about somethin’ else than business, and didn’t drop my aitches. And so would my little gell.”

“You have a daughter?” asked my mother, instantly warmed. A child, to my mother, was always a bridge flung across any strangeness.

“I won’t tell you about ’er,” Mr. Hasluck answered, with a sudden softening that looked odd on so much flesh. “But I’ll just bring ’er to see you now and then, ma’am, if you don’t mind. She don’t often meet gentle-folks, an’ it’ll do ’er good.”

My mother glanced at my father; and before her glance could become a question, Mr. Hasluck waved it away with easy mastery.

“You needn’t be afraid, ma’am, that she’s anythin’ like me,” he said, good-temperedly. “Nobody ever believes she’s my daughter, except me and the old woman. She’s a little lady, she is. Freak o’ nature, I call it.”

“We shall be delighted,” said my mother, in the tone she used when offering second helpings: firm kindness, no condescension.

“Well, you will when you see ’er,” Mr. Hasluck replied, as if the matter were settled by the laws of the universe.

As I was being ushered out he pressed a half-crown into my hand with the cheerful tyranny of a man who paid for whatever he touched, including other people’s pride.

“No squanderin’ it on the ’eathen,” he called after me. “You spend that on a Christian tradesman.”

It was the first money I ever remember having that was truly mine to spend. Up to then, any coin that floated into my small life had been immediately tethered by improving visions: Sunday gloves, a Latin grammar with my own name inside, books with good boys in them who did good things with tidy faces. Now, however, I was left free.

Freedom, I discovered, is an anxious thing.

For nights I lay awake, the half-crown burning in my mind as if it had been heated on the range. I tramped the streets with it in my pocket, feeling it drag my trousers down by sheer responsibility. I stared into shop windows until my breath fogged the glass; I asked prices I had no intention of paying; I made up my mind and unmade it again in the space of a single paving-stone.

Advice, when I begged for it, was denied.

“You must learn to think for yourself,” said my father, who could talk for ten minutes on “commercial aptitude” without once noticing that I was only eight and hated commerce.

“No, dear,” said my mother. “Mr. Hasluck wanted you to spend it as you like. If I told you, that would be spending it as I liked. Your father and I want to see what you will do.”

The good little boys in books bought gifts for their parents or gave away money to people in distress. For this I hated them with the clean, unmixed malice the lower nature always feels towards the higher.

I tried my aunt Fan.

“If somebody gave you half-a-crown,” I asked, “what would you buy with it?”

“Side-combs,” said my aunt promptly. She was forever losing or breaking her side-combs, as if her hair were engaged in a private war.

“But I mean if you were me.”

“Drat the child!” said my aunt. “How do I know what he wants if he don’t know himself? Idiot!”

So the half-crown grew into a family affliction. Even my mother’s patience began to show small cracks, like a pie-crust that has been handled too much.

Then, one day, I ended it.

In a back street there was a plumber’s shop, smelling of metal and damp wood, with brass taps and rolls of lead piping and cistern contrivances displayed as if they were jewels. Among them, propped neatly, were squares of coloured glass—cheap stained panes such as you saw in lavatory doors and staircase windows. Some held stars at their centres; others had severe little patterns that tried to be grand and succeeded in being merely tidy.

I bought a dozen.

The plumber, an affable man, seemed glad to see me in the way that tradesmen are glad to see foolish boys with money. He wrapped the panes in paper with practised hands and threw in two extra out of generosity—or out of the belief that I was the sort of customer who, once started, might some day build a house entirely out of lavatory windows.

Why I bought them I did not know at the time, and I do not know now. They pleased me—perhaps because they made ordinary daylight look like something from a story, because they turned the common into the coloured without changing anything but the way you saw it. Or perhaps because, like many of Mr. Hasluck’s gifts, they carried their own peculiar curse and I was merely obedient to it.

My mother cried when she saw them. Not loudly; she simply sat down as if her knees had turned to dough and covered her face with her hands.

My father got no further than: “But what are you going to do with them?” and, since my mind had purchased them long before my reason arrived, I could not tell him.

My aunt Fan alone attempted comfort.

“If a person fancies coloured glass,” she said, “then he’s a fool not to buy coloured glass when he gets the chance. We haven’t all the same tastes.”

In the end I cut myself badly with them and, after bleeding in a manner that made everyone feel justified, consented to their being thrown into the dust-bin. Yet looking back I think less of folly than of Fate. I have met many people since who were recipients of old Hasluck’s half-crowns—men who slapped their pockets and blessed the day they first met that “Napoleon of Finance,” as later his friends came to call him. But it ever ended so: coloured glass and cut fingers. Fairy gold, perhaps: bright in the hand, awkward in life.

The next time Mr. Hasluck knocked at our door a maid in cap and apron opened it.

That was only the beginning.

New oilcloth appeared in the passage and shone like a wet fish. Lace curtains—those neighborhood banners of sudden respectability—hung in our windows and announced our prosperity to every passer-by. Greatest marvel of all, at least to my awed eyes: my father’s Sunday clothes came down into weekday service, while new ones took their place in the wardrobe, that old stronghold of gentility where we had always gone for comfort when the outer walls looked thin.

“Seeing that everything was all right,” my mother would say, as if “all right” were a condition you could lay out on the bed and stroke smooth.

She would spread the lilac silk gown and soothe down its rustling waves; she would linger over its deep frosted Honiton flounces as lovingly as over a good pie-crust that had turned out exactly as planned. She might enter the room looking tired and pinched, but as she folded and refolded the Indian shawl—the one that could go through a wedding-ring but never would when you asked it—her spirits rose like bread in warmth. By the time the great cameo brooch was pinned and the poke bonnet had been shaken and petted into decency, she would be humming, sometimes even singing softly to herself, nodding to words that were usually about somebody too old and somebody too bold and another too cold, “so he wouldn’t do for me,” and stepping lightly as if money had taken a little weight off her shoulders.

I remember one evening—before the advent of this Hasluck—when I climbed out of bed because trouble was within me.

Creatures, indescribable but heavy, had sat upon my chest; after which I had fallen downstairs in a dream, slowly and reasonably for the first few hundred flights, then with indecent haste for the next million miles or so, until I found myself in the street with nothing on but my nightshirt. Personally, I was shocked; but nobody else in the dream seemed to mind. I hailed a two-penny ’bus and climbed in, and then discovered I had no pockets with which to pay. I jumped out and ran, and the conductor came after me. My feet were like lead; with every step he gained—until, with a scream, I made one mighty effort and woke.

Feeling the need of comfort, I wrapped some clothes round me and crept downstairs. The office was dark; but to my surprise a light shone from under the drawing-room door. I opened it.

The candles in the silver candlesticks were lit. In state, one in each easy-chair, sat my father and mother in their best clothes: my father in buckled shoes and frilled shirt, which I had never before seen him wear; my mother with the Indian shawl about her shoulders and on her head the cap of ceremony that lay, three hundred and sixty days of the year, in its round wicker nest lined with silk. They started guiltily when I came in—like children caught playing at grown-up.

I congratulate myself that I had enough sense—or instinct—not to ask questions.

Three hours earlier they had been cleaning the ground-floor windows, my father outside, my mother within, with all lights carefully extinguished. Now their whole manner had changed. Their voices were altered: softer, more measured, as if they had stepped into another dialect of themselves—one with longer vowels and fewer worries.

My father brought sherry and sweet biscuits from the sideboard. He poured a glass for my mother and handed it as if presenting a prize. They drank to each other, while I sat between them and ate the biscuits, which were crisp and sugary and tasted of occasion. The conversation was of Byron’s poems and the great glass palace in Hyde Park.

I wonder, am I disloyal setting this down? Perhaps to others it shows only a foolish man and woman dressing up in their own lives. But the memory is tender to me. Virtues are what we expect from those we love; failings we try to forget. It is over their follies that affection smiles and lingers.

To me personally, old Hasluck’s coming—and all that followed—made perhaps more difference than to anyone else. My father became busy all day; if not in his office then away in what I still thought of as the grim city of giants. My mother’s days filled with new social and domestic duties, and for a time I was left much to my own resources.
