Rambling—“bummelling,” as the Germans call it—was my bent. My mother would have checked it, but my father said:

“Don’t molly-coddle him. Let him learn to be smart.”

“I don’t think the smart people are always the nicest,” my mother demurred. “I don’t call you at all smart, Luke.”

My father looked surprised, then considered as if weighing evidence.

“I should call myself smart—in a sense,” he explained at last.

“Perhaps you are right, dear,” my mother said. “And of course boys are different from girls.”

Sometimes I wandered towards Victoria Park, which then was edged with small cottages set in leafy gardens, where you could hear birds and children and the kind of quiet that feels like a clean tablecloth. Sometimes I reached as far as Clapton, where old red-brick Georgian houses stood behind high palings and tall elms lent the wide road, on sunny afternoons, an air of peace that seemed borrowed from another century.

But, strangely, those narrow squalid streets nearer home had a greater hold on me.

Not the main thoroughfares with their dull deep throbbing, as of some tireless iron machine—the place where the endless human files streaming and crossing and recrossing looked like chains of flesh working on unseen wheels. What drew me were the dim, weary, lifeless streets—the dark, tortuous roots, as I imagined them, of that grim forest of brick. There was a hush there that felt less like quiet than like listening. Far-off footsteps would rise suddenly into sound and then fade, leaving the air to settle back into its own uneasy breathing. A door might bang two streets away and the noise would travel along the walls as though the houses passed it on to one another in whispers.

In those streets, silence had a taste: stale, smoky, with a hint of damp plaster. And in that silence, fear could grow fat.

One day a cry came from behind a curtained window—long, piercing, not like a street-seller’s shout but like something torn out of a throat. I stood still for an instant, then ran. Before I could get far, it came again, fiercer before it ended, and I ran faster, not heeding where I went, until I found myself in a raw unfinished street that ended in black waste land bordering the river.

The air there was different—open, wet, smelling of mud and rot and the river’s cold breath. The city’s clatter did not quite reach it; what you heard instead was a thin, restless soundscape: wind worrying at loose boards, a distant chain clinking, some far, unseen creak that might have been a rope or a conscience.

I stopped, panting, and sat on the doorstep of an empty house to recover myself. And then there came dancing down the road with a curious half-running, half-hopping step—like a water-wagtail’s—another child, a boy about my own age. He eyed me oddly and sat down beside me.

For a few minutes we watched each other. His mouth opened and shut though he said nothing, as if he were chewing words too tough to swallow. Then, edging closer, he spoke in a thick whisper, as though his mouth were full of wool.

“Wot ’appens to yer when yer dead?”

“If you’re good you go to Heaven,” I answered promptly. “If you’re bad you go to Hell.”

“Long way off, both of ’em, ain’t they?”

“Yes. Millions of miles.”

“They can’t come after yer? Can’t fetch yer back again?”

“No. Never.”

All the while his eyes stared towards the other end of the street, now darkening. He did not look away for an instant.

“Ever seen a stiff ’un—a dead ’un?”

“No.”

“I ’ave—stuck a pin into ’im. ’E never felt it. Don’t feel anythin’ when yer dead, do yer?”

As he talked he swayed and twisted, arms and legs wriggling in queer curves, pulling faces. Those gingerbread men bakers used to sell—comical, with bent limbs and grinning mouths—came into my mind so strongly that, absurdly, my stomach tightened with hunger.

“Of course not,” I said, glad to have something solemn ready. “When you are dead you’re not there, you know. Our bodies are but senseless clay.”

I was pleased with myself for remembering that line. I tried to summon the next, about food for worms, but it evaded me.

“I like you,” he announced; and, making a fist, he punched me in the chest.

It was the local token of fellowship. Gravely, feeling the sudden fierce warmth of instant friendship—children’s friendships being made like matches, with a scratch and a flare—I punched him back.

He wriggled up on the step, suddenly serious.

“Yer won’t tell?” he demanded.

I had no notion what I was not to tell, but a secret oath was obviously required of me.

“Say ‘I swear.’”

“I swear.”

This pleased me: it made him seem like a hero out of my books, only dirtier and nearer.

“Good-bye, cockey,” he said.

Then he turned back, fished in his pocket, and thrust an old knife into my hand. Without another word he ran off across the mud, that odd hopping gait carrying him towards the river.

I watched him, bewildered. He stumbled where the mud turned softer and deeper; he sank nearly to his knees, wrenched himself free, went on. I shouted, but he did not look back. Every few yards he sank again, floundered forward again, like something being slowly swallowed and refusing, with stubborn little kicks, to be eaten.

Then, still some distance from the water, he fell on his face and did not rise.

I saw his arms beating—feebler, feebler—until the oily slime closed over him. For a moment there was only a faint heaving beneath the mud, as if the earth itself were chewing. Then even that ceased.

It was late when I reached home, and fortunately my father and mother were still out. I did not tell anyone what I had seen; I had sworn not to, and the oath felt heavier than the knife in my pocket. In time the incident haunted me less and less until, as often happens, it became something I could order about in my mind—summon, dismiss, place on a shelf.

But it cured me, for a while, of my taste for those silent streets. From behind still walls I would hear again that long cry; down the narrow vistas I would see that writhing figure—like an animated gingerbread man—hopping, springing, falling.

Yet in the more crowded streets another trouble waited for me—one more tangible, and more cruel because it came with laughter.

Have you ever seen a flock of sparrows round some crumbs, and suddenly the rest fall upon one? There is a ridiculous hubbub, tiny wings and heads tossed, a babel of shrill chirps. It looks comical.

“Spiteful little imps,” people say, amused.

So good-tempered men and women would call out, laughing, when boys set upon a single boy.

“There go those young devils chivvying that poor little beggar again; ought to be ashamed of theirselves.”

But oh, the anguish of the poor little beggar.

Reduced to facts, what was it? Jeers that broke no bones; pinches, kicks, slaps; at worst a few hard knocks. Yet the dread beforehand made every street a trap. Terror hid round each corner, waiting for me like a boy with his hands in his pockets and a grin already loaded.

The half-dozen crouched over marbles—had they seen me? The boy whistling outside the print shop—would I get past without his noticing, or would he swing round and give the shrill whoop that called them from doorways as if the very bricks were breeding tormentors?

Then the shame when caught and cornered: the grinning faces that stopped to watch; the jokes of passers-by, treating it as no more than sparrows squabbling; worst of all, the rare pity, which landed like cold porridge on pride.

And afterwards, released at last, I would dart away, followed by laughter and shouted taunts. Every eye seemed turned to watch me shrink by, my whole small carcass shaking with dry sobs of bitterness and rage.

If only I could have turned and faced them! Pain I could bear; I knew it. Any number of stand-up fights would have hurt less than the hot choking misery of being hunted. For in my mind I was the comrade of a hundred heroes. Nightly I rode with Richard Coeur de Lion; I couched my lance against Sir Lancelot himself and was not wholly unsuccessful; damsels in distress looked to me as naturally as kettles look to the fire.

And yet, when the whoop went up, the joyous shout died on my pale lips, and the thing that scattered to the wind was myself, slipping into the first opening like a rabbit that knows its own burrow.

Sometimes, blood boiling, I would turn, determined to go back and at all risk defy them—prove to myself I was no coward. But before I had retraced a dozen paces the whole scene would leap up in my imagination: the laughing crowd, the halting passers-by, the spiteful little faces wherever I turned; and I would creep home instead, stealing up to my room, and cry my heart out in the dark upon my bed.

The East India Dock Road, as any self-respecting thoroughfare now, makes its living by noise. It has bells that insist upon being heard, wheels that rattle as though paid by the vibration, and a perpetual rain of feet—an industrious percussion that, if you listen long enough, will begin to dictate your thoughts for you. But in the days I speak of it was a different creature: long, bare, and oddly tongue-tied, with the docks’ wall on one side—endless brick holding its own echo—and on the other a scattering of small houses stranded among market gardens, drying grounds, and heaps of rubbish that seemed to have been tipped there as if the world had shrugged.

Only one thing, last time I went by, still held hands with that earlier road: the low, one-storeyed brick cottage at the beginning of the bridge—the old toll-house. I know it because it was there my childhood fell off me, like a cap you did not notice you had lost until the wind hit your ears, and I stood suddenly bare-headed before the world beyond.

I had been to Plaistow that afternoon, to our family dentist. Plaistow was out of the way for a dentist, which was, perhaps, its chief advantage. My mother, fastening up my knickerbockers with the air of a general sending a private into a doubtful battle, would say:

“Have the half-crown in your hand,” and then—because mothers never trust to hands—make sure the purse containing it lay deep in my pocket. “But of course if he won’t take it, why, you must bring it home again.”

I cannot say if he was kin; he felt like it, which is the better relationship. He was an old bachelor in a house set in a great garden—for Plaistow, then, had hedges and space and a look of itself—and he had the gentle, ominous cheerfulness of men who mean well while wielding metal.

I would be placed in the velvet chair of state, as if I were a small king about to be quietly assassinated, and he would unroll his case of instruments with ceremony.

“Choose,” he would say, fondly recommending the one that looked as if it had once been used to open oysters in a hurry.

I would open my mouth to argue—lo! a pair of forceps would flash from under his sleeve, swift as a magician’s trick, and before my fear had time to arrange itself into sentences, the trouble would be done.

Afterwards we would have tea together, which was his true practice. And what teas! His housekeeper, with the ripe certainty of women who can coax order out of fruit, made jams and jellies from the garden that seemed to hold summer in prison: strawberry that smelled like warm sunlight caught in glass, currant sharp as a pleasant scolding, gooseberry that made your mouth wake up. The kettle sang; the cups clinked; a spoon against a saucer gave a small, domestic bell-note that was comfort itself. Even now I think the sound of teaspoons is one of the kinder noises a civilisation makes.

We talked of my mother, and it appeared—by his account—that she had once been a little girl. Not the sort I would have expected; not solemn and correct and born with a duster in her hand, but prankish and wilful, and, if I may say so, lively company. When I repeated these tales at home, my mother would laugh, declaring she had no recollection of any such nonsense, adding severely that it was a pity he and I could not find something better to gossip about.

Yet her next question, always, would be:

“And what else did he say, if you please?” Then, if I answered about microscopes or jam, she would correct me impatiently: “No, no, I mean about me.”

When tea things were cleared away, he brought out his microscope, that round, solemn eye into which you peered and were rewarded with dragons in a drop of water. It was a peep-hole into a fairy country where monstrous beings carried on their private affairs without shame. Looking back, I cannot help thinking his love of the microscopical suited him; he was an exceptionally little man himself, yet one of the biggest-hearted I have known—compact, like a good preserve: nothing wasted, everything sweet.

On leaving, I would formally hand him my half-crown “with mamma’s compliments,” and he would formally accept it. But outside the gate, putting my hand into my pocket, I would invariably find it there again, as if it had come home on its own.

The first time I ran back, honest as only children can be when they have not yet learned the uses of dishonesty, and offered it to him again. He repudiated all knowledge.

“Must be another half-crown,” he suggested, gravely. “Such things do happen. One puts change into a pocket and overlooks it. Slippery things, half-crowns.”

Returning home on that particular day of days, I stopped on the bridge and watched the barges, lazy and broad-backed, manoeuvring between the piers as though water were thicker than it is and required persuasion. The evening was one of those hushed summer ones when even a grim city seems to soften its voice; you could hear the river’s slow talk below, and far off the muffled clop of a horse, and nearer, the soft shush of wind in weeds along the bank. It was as if the world, for once, had agreed not to shout.

And as I turned away from the river and passed through the white toll-gate, I had a sense—clear, absurd, undeniable—of leaving myself behind me on the bridge. So vivid was it that I looked back, half expecting to see myself still there, leaning over the iron parapet, a small figure looking down into sunlit water, untroubled by anything beyond the immediate glitter.

It sounds foolish, but I cannot improve upon the truth. The little chap never came back. He went away from me as a man’s body, perhaps, may one day go, leaving him only remembrance and regret. For a while I tried to play his games and dream his dreams, but the substance was gone; I was only a thin ghost pretending.

This sense—that my childhood lay behind me, that I was travelling swiftly along the strange road called growing up—troubled me for a long time, even to tears. I did not want to grow up. Could nothing stop it? I would rather have remained always as I had been, playing and imagining. The dark way frightened me. Must I go forward?

Then, slowly—so slowly that only years can do it—there came new pulses, new sensories, new throbbings rooted in the old yet different, like a new dish made from the same stock but seasoned to a sharper taste. And little Paul, the Paul of whom I have hitherto spoken, faded.

So must I let him fade from this book. But before I part with him altogether, I will call up what else I can, so that we may be quit of him and he will not come tugging at our sleeves again.

Chief among the pictures is my aunt Fan crouching over the kitchen fire. The kitchen was the true kingdom of our house: not the drawing-room with its stiff propriety, but the warm, busy region of smells and sounds—the crackle of coal, the hiss of fat, the thump of a knife on board, the comforting murmur of a kettle finding its voice. My aunt’s skirt and crinoline would be rolled up round her waist, leaving, as sacrifice to custom, only a petticoat; and up and down she swayed in rhythmic motion, her hands rubbing her knees with affectionate perseverance, as though she were polishing them for company.

Opposite her stood I, armed with paper-knife sword or mounted upon a broomstick horse, flourishing, declaiming, summoning dragons and battles out of thin air.

Sometimes I was a knight and she a wicked ogre. She would be slain, growling and swearing—my aunt did nothing feebly—and at once become a beautiful princess whom I was to secure and bear away on my prancing broomstick. So long as the princess merely held sweet converse from a high-barred window, the scene was realistic enough; but the bearing away had to remain make-believe. My aunt could not be persuaded to leave her chair, nor abandon the eternal rubbing of her knees. Even princesses, it seems, have rheumatism.

At other times, aided by the meat-chopper, I was an Indian brave; then she was Laughing Water or Singing Sunshine, and we went scalping together. In less bloodthirsty moods I was the Fairy Prince and she the Sleeping Beauty, though she was never at her best in roles that required stillness. Better when, seated in the centre of the up-turned table, I was Captain Cook and she the Cannibal Chief.

“I shall skin him and hang him in the larder till Sunday week,” my aunt would say, smacking her lips with a connoisseur’s relish, “then he’ll be just in right condition; not too tough and not too high.” She was always strong in detail.

To do her justice, her compliance was not altogether self-sacrifice. Often she suggested the game and even the theme; and in that case she invariably cast herself for what, in old theatrical parlance, would have been the heavy lead: dragons, wicked uncles, fussy necromancers, uninvited fairies. As authoress of a cookery book for use in giant-land, my aunt would have been a success. Ordinary recipes are so mean and lazy—“Boil him,” “Roast her,” “Bake ’em in a pie”—but my aunt introduced variety, garnish, and a domestic practicality that made ogre-economy almost respectable.

“I think, my dear,” she would direct, “we’ll have him stuffed with chestnuts and served on toast. And don’t forget the giblets. They make such excellent sauce.”

With regard to the diet of imprisoned maidens she advised, with the calm authority of one who had tried them all:

“Not too much fish—it spoils the flesh for roasting.”

The things she would turn people into—kings’ sons, rightful princesses—people who after a time must have forgotten what they began as! To let her have her way was a lesson in natural history, present and pre-historic. Without hesitation she would transform the loveliest damsel into a Glyptodon or a Hippocrepian. Later, when I had learned to guess at spellings, I looked these creatures up in the illustrated dictionary and felt that under no circumstances could I have loved the lady ever again. Warriors and kings she delighted in turning into plaice or prawns; haughty queens into Brussels sprouts. It was not merely wickedness; it was culinary imagination run wild.

With gusto she planned complicated slaughter, noting every necessary detail: knives to be sharpened, mops and pails of water ready for after cleaning. If she had been a writer, she would have belonged to the realistic school—blood on the floor, soot in the grate, and a sensible aside about how difficult it is to get stains out of stone.

Her death—always the close of the afternoon—was another conscientious effort. Her groans and writhings sometimes frightened me. I always welcomed the last gurgle. Not until that final sound would she let down her skirt—thus suggesting the fall of the curtain—and set briskly to work to get tea, as though ogres and princesses were but incidental to bread and butter.

Another picture: myself in a glazed-peaked cap, explaining many things as we walked through dingy streets to a yet smaller figure, curly-haired, open-eyed. Now and then she would run ahead and turn to look up admiringly into my face, as on the first day she became captive to the praise and fame of me.

I was glad of her company for more reasons than she knew. For one thing, she protected me against my baser self. Alone, I might have fled from sudden foes. With her beside me I should not have dared. Together we courted adventure; and once you are used to it, the standing hazard of attack adds a charm to exercise that older folk in well-policed districts do not understand. It is much as my dog feels when we go out. To me it is merely a walk, suggested more by contemplation of my waistband than by love of walking; to him it is an epic crowded with possibilities. The gentleman asleep with one eye open on The Chequers doorstep—friend or enemy? The cross-eyed loafer lurching against the lamp-post—greeting or battle? The next corner—ambush or empty triumph? The city is composed, for a dog, of sound: the scrape of another paw, the clink of a collar, the mutter behind a gate. One could almost pity him the modern traffic, that huge, mindless roar which drowns the smaller meanings.

But the trusting face beside me nerved me. In lonely places I would let her hold my hand, as one allows a small bird to perch—carefully, with an odd tenderness.

A second advantage of her company was that I was less trampled on, less walked over, less swept aside into doorway or gutter than when alone. She had a pretty, winsome face—if Memory is not playing me a trick—but she also had a vocabulary; and when some blind idiot, male or female, instead of walking round us attempted the customary feat of walking through us, she would use it.

“Now, then, where yer coming to, old glass-eye? We ain’t sperrits. Can’t yer see us?”

If they tried to reply, her child’s treble—so strangely out of keeping with her dainty appearance—would only rise more shrill.

“Garn! They’d run out of ’eads when they was making you. That’s only a turnip wot you’ve got stuck on top of yer!” I offer but samples.

It was useless to attempt personal chastisement. An irate lady or gentleman would sometimes be foolish enough to try. As well might a hippopotamus attempt to reprove a terrier. The only result was to provide comedy for the entire street.

On these occasions our positions reversed and I became spectator, admiring her prowess. Yet with me she was meek—almost irritatingly submissive. She found out where I lived and would often come and wait for me for hours, her little face pressed tight against the iron railings, until I came out or shook my head at her from my bedroom window. Then she would run off, and the patter of her feet dying into silence always left me a little sad, as if a small kindness had just missed its proper use.

I think I cared for her, in a way, yet she never entered my day-dreams. That means she existed for me only in the outer world, not in the inner one where I kept my true life. And I think she was unwise—though how was she to know?—in introducing me to the shop. Children and dogs—one brackets them together without meaning to—are snobbish little wretches.

If only her father had been a dealer in firewood, I could have soothed myself by imagining mistakes. It is a common occurrence, as I well knew, for children of the best families to be brought up by wood-choppers. Fairies, well-intentioned but born muddlers, were generally responsible; and such things always got put right in time for the wedding.

Or if he had been a pork butcher—which would have been almost romantic in our neighbourhood—I might have thought of him as a swineherd and found precedent for hope.

But a fishmonger—from six in the evening, a fried fishmonger! I searched history in vain. Fried fishmongers were without the pale. They belonged to the kingdom of vinegar and hot fat, and there are romances that cannot survive that smell.

So gradually our meetings grew less frequent. I knew she waited every afternoon in quiet Stainsby Road where, in semi-detached six-roomed villas, dwelt the aristocracy of Poplar; and now and then, arriving late, I saw the sad fact of tears tracing patterns down her dust-besprinkled cheeks. And with the advent of the world-illuminating Barbara—to which event I am drawing near—our meetings ceased altogether.

So began and ended my first romance. One of these days—some quiet summer afternoon, when even the air of Pigott Street vibrates with tenderness beneath Memory’s whispered sighs—I shall walk into the little grocer’s shop and boldly ask to see her. I have gone so far already as to trace her; often I have tried to catch sight of her through the glass door, but hitherto in vain. I know she is now the more or less troubled mother of numerous children. I am told she has grown stout, and it is probable her tongue has gained rather than lost in sharpness. Yet under all the clumsy unreality the world has built about her, I shall see, I know, the lithesome little maid with fond, admiring eyes.

What help they were to me I never knew until I had lost them. How hard it is to win such eyes I have learned since. If we wrote the truth in our confession books, should we not admit that the quality we most admire in others is admiration of ourselves? And is it not a wise selection? If you would have me admirable, my friend, admire me; speak your commendation without stint, that in the sunshine of your praise I may wax. For indifference makes an indifferent man, and contempt a contemptible man. Come—is it not true? Does not all that is worthy in us grow best by honour?

Chief among the remaining figures upon my childhood’s stage were the many servants of our house, the “generals,” as they were called—because, I suppose, each commanded the kitchen until driven out by the enemy. So rapid was their transit that only one or two remain distinct.

It was a neighbourhood where domestic servants were not much required. Those intending to take up the calling seriously went westward. The local ranks were recruited from the discontented and disappointed—those unappreciated at home who hoped for more discernment from strangers—or from the love-lorn, the jilted, the jealous, who took cap and apron as in earlier ages their kind would have taken the veil. Perhaps the comparative seclusion of a basement, with its steady noises—kettle, grate, knife, broom—seemed, in such moods, more fitting than the frivolity of shop or factory. With the advent of a new young man, or the recovery of the old, they would plunge again into the vain world, leaving my poor mother to begin her search anew amid the legions of the cursed.

With these women I made what comradeship I could, for I had no child friends. They were kind, most of them, though poor at make-believe. Still they would squeeze ten minutes from work to romp with me, and that, perhaps, was healthier. Less healthy was this: staggered by the amount of “book-learning” my conversation implied—for the journalistic instinct, I think, was early in me—they would listen open-mouthed and treat me as a precocious oracle. Sometimes they obtained permission to take me home with them to tea, eager that their friends should profit by me. Encouraged by admiring faces, I would hold forth, keenly enjoying the sound of my own piping.

“As good as a book, ain’t he?” was the tribute most often paid.

“As good as a play,” one old greengrocer went so far as to say.

Already I considered myself among the Immortals.

One girl, a dear wholesome creature named Janet, stayed with us for months and might have stayed years but for her addiction to strong language. She was the only and well-beloved child of the captain of the barge Nancy Jane, trading between Purfleet and Ponder’s End, and her speech was at once my terror and my delight.

“Janet!” my mother would cry, hands flying up as if to shield her ears from flying crockery, “how can you use such words?”

“What words, mum?”

“The things you have just called the gas man.”

“Him! Well, did you see what he did, mum? Walked straight into my clean kitchen without even wiping his boots, the—” And before my mother could catch the sentence by the tail and drag it out, Janet had relieved her feelings by calling him it—or rather them—again, with no notion she had done anything but give natural emotion its proper name.

Janet and I were friends, and for that reason I undertook her reformation. I did not mince matters. I told her plainly that if she persisted she would inevitably go to hell.

“Then where’s my father going?” demanded Janet.

“Does he use language?” I asked.

From Janet I gathered that any person who had enjoyed the privilege of hearing her father could never again take interest in the feeble efforts of herself.

“I am afraid, Janet,” I explained, “that if he doesn’t give it up—”

“But it’s the only way he can talk,” interrupted Janet. “He don’t mean anything by it.”

I sighed, for even then I had a weakness for mercy, but I set my face against it. “You see, Janet, people who swear do go there.”

But Janet would not believe.

“God send my dear, kind father to hell just because he can’t talk like the gentlefolks! Don’t you believe it of Him, Master Paul. He’s got more sense.”

I hope I pain no one by repeating Janet’s common sense. I remember it because often, sinking into the sloughs of childish despond, it gave me firm foothold. More than once, forced to listen to the sententious voice of immeasurable Folly explaining eternal mysteries with glib certainty, it comforted me to whisper: “I don’t believe it of Him. He’s got more sense.”

And about that period I needed all comfort I could get. As we go down the road of life the journey, demanding so much attention, becomes more important than its end; but to the child standing at the valley’s gate the terminating hills are plain, and what lies beyond is his constant wonder. I never questioned my parents directly—how strangely we all shrink, young and old, from speaking of what matters most—while they, not guessing my need, contented themselves with vague generalities, as though mist were a proper clothing for terror.

But there were foolish voices about me less reticent; and the serious literature provided for youth in those days answered questions with blunt brutality. If you did wrong, you burnt in a fiery furnace for ever and ever. If your imagination was weak, there was an illustration to help it: you could see yourself writhe and shrink and scream while cheerful devils, well organised, stoked.

I had been burnt once, badly, by live coals dropped on me in transit upon a shovel. I knew what it was to have fire cling to the skin like a cruel animal. I imagined those coals not confined to one place but pressing everywhere, and not snatched off by loving hands; no soft soap, no blue bag, no soothing household remedy—only the pain eating inward, and time doing nothing to it. For an hour, a day, a thousand years—and no nearer the end. Ten thousand, a million, and still it was for ever; and for ever it would always be for ever. I suffered insomnia then; night had too much room in it.

“Then be good,” said the foolish voices. “Never do wrong, and so avoid it.”

But it was so easy to do wrong. There were so many wrong things, and doing them felt so natural.

“Then repent,” said the voices, always ready with a door—if you could find the handle.

But how did one repent? What was repentance? Did I hate my sin, as instructed, or merely hate the idea of going to hell for it? Even my child’s sense knew the latter was not true repentance. Yet how could one know the difference?

Above all haunted me the fear of the Unforgivable Sin. What it was I could never discover. I dreaded to ask too closely lest I should learn I had already committed it. Day and night its shadow clung to me.

“Believe,” said the voices. “So only shall you be saved.”

How believe? How know you believed? Hours I would kneel in the dark, repeating in a whispered scream:

“I believe, I believe. Oh, I do believe!”

Then I would rise with white knuckles, listening to the house’s small sounds—the settling of boards, the distant clink of a spoon, the faint sigh of wind—as if any honest noise might answer me, and wondering, terrified and stubborn, whether I really did.

“Correct” was the word you could lay like a clean napkin over Doctor Florret and find it fitted him at every corner.

He was large in the approved manner—built, as it were, to preside over boys and benches; stout, but in the sense that a well-fed joint is stout: comfortable, respectable, meant to be carved without surprise. His hands were white and shaped as though they had been taught penmanship by a dancing-master. On his left finger he wore a diamond that did not so much flash as behave. His voice could give dignity to the rain. His clean-shaven face shone with benevolence—benevolence with a latch on it, so that it should not swing open upon you too far.

So with his household. His wife was his feminine echo: you looked at them together and you felt that nature, having once achieved Doctor Florret, had decided to keep the pattern and merely vary the trimming. Their furniture was solid, like school rules: made to sit upon, not to admire. Their pictures were the pictorial equivalent of “quiet colours.” Everything about him said, “This is how it is done,” and if you waited long enough, you would learn the correct thing to do by observing him do it; at which point it became suddenly obvious that anything else would have been indecent.

In practice, as I discovered at the cost of four years that had the air of being both long and short—long in the evenings, short in the results—Doctor Florret followed the methods considered correct by English schoolmen from somewhere about Edward VI., and, I should imagine, by a good many Egyptian priests before that.

Heaven knows I worked. I wanted to learn. A boy’s ambition is like yeast: it rises in every basin it is put into, and overflows in directions nobody asked it to. If I passed a man haranguing a street-corner—hatless, damp, pointing at Utopias as if they were just round the next lamp-post—I instantly became Demosthenes, then a statesman, then Prime Minister, then a figure so praised that even the Opposition (had I known of the Opposition) would have been forced into reluctant hymn-tunes about me. If a distant drum beat, it became the pulse of battle; and there, in the centre of all carnage and glory, was myself—always myself—conspicuously isolated, promoted with a speed that would have shocked the Horse Guards, returning Commander-in-Chief by sheer merit and the proper arrangement of dreams.

Vast crowds filled the streets in that agreeable roar that is half welcome and half the sound of one’s own blood when one is happy. I rode a charger—snow-white, or coal-black; the question plagued me like an unsolved sum, for I was anxious my day of triumph should not suffer from a bad colour choice. I paced, very slowly, between waves of faces, and then—because dreams are economical—walked into a lamp-post or on to the toes of some irascible old gentleman, and woke.

A drunken sailor, storming from between swing doors, could at once become the captain of my vessel. A factory chimney, belching smoke, turned into a mast. The costers’ shouts were “Ay, ay, sir!” and I, rolled together out of Columbus and Drake and Nelson, discovered new continents and defeated the French—those useful French!—died in the moment of victory, was mourned by the nation, and buried in Westminster Abbey. Alternatively, I lived and was made a Duke. I had no fixed preference; both endings were handsome.

On November the ninth certain boys, by letters from their mothers read aloud by Doctor Florret with a snort, developed severe toothaches. On November the tenth they described the Lord Mayor’s Show to us, and their voices faded as if from the far end of a corridor. The bells of Bow rang in my ears. I became a merchant prince while still young; I lent money to nobles and married their daughters; I listened to people discussing a new book and instantly became a famous author. All men praised me (reviewers, in those days, were still mythical creatures with no teeth). Poetry, fiction, history—I wrote everything, and everyone read and wondered.

Only one small thorn sat in the cushion of my greatness: penmanship was my private tragedy, and spelling a personal riddle. My fame was scratched out and rewritten in blots. More than once I considered abandoning literature in favour of something that did not require me to persuade letters to stand in line.

But whichever road led to the Elysian Fields of applause, education—dimly, stubbornly—I understood as necessary equipment. So I bent myself over books with an aching head and a heart that, at times, felt as if it had been sat upon.

I see that small bedroom still: my elbows planted on a shaky table that had one leg less than it pretended, the solitary candle making a brave show and occasionally frizzling my hair when I leaned too near. On cold nights I wore my overcoat turned up about the neck, and wrapped a blanket round my legs, looking like a refugee from my own bed. Often I put my fingers in my ears—not to shut out thought, but to shut out life: the voices and clatter from below, the domestic racket that rose like steam.

“A song, of a song, to a song—A song, O song!” came up insistently; and then I, in a small buzzing voice of my own that began to seem detached from me, repeated: “I love. Thou lovest. He, she, or it loves. I should or would love,” over and over till my head felt as if it were shrinking, and I put my hands up in alarm to see whether it was still there.

Was I more stupid than the average boy, or is a boy’s brain simply not built to carry the load our educational system packs upon it, like a dishonest porter?

“Latin and Greek,” I can hear Doctor Florret purring, with that solemn croak of Correctness, “are useful as mental gymnastics.” My dear Doctor Florret and your compatriots, could you not select your apparatus from the vast storehouse of actual knowledge, and choose something that strengthens without tearing? You, with your grown brain and your practised wit, take down your Shakespeare or Browning—your own tongue, your mother speech. Construe this passage; parse it, analyse it, divide it into joints as you would divide a tough fowl for soup. Now tell me it is sensible to set a tear-stained, ink-bespattered Tommy to do the same with AEsop or Ovid, as if his little mind were meant to lift one-hundred-pound dumb-bells.

We were not princes training for polite leisure. We were the sons of City men, minor officials, clerks, shopkeepers; our roads led through the workaday world of ledgers and counters. Yet half our time went to studies that, for our likely lives, were as much use as teaching a hungry boy the Latin names of dishes he will never taste.

How I hated them, those youth-tormenting Shades. Homer—how I wished the fishermen had put that absurd riddle to him earlier. Horace—why could not that shipwreck have succeeded? It would have, in any other case.

Then, one blessed day, a talisman came into my hands.

Hearken, ye heavy-burdened little brethren: waste not your substance upon tops and marbles, nor yet upon tuck—if you still call it tuck—but scrape and save. For near Paternoster Row dwells a magician who, for silver, will sell you a Key that opens wide the gates of Hades.

By it, Aristophanes’ Frogs became my cheerful companions. With Ulysses I wandered through Wonderland and understood what I wandered in. Doctor Florret was charmed with my sudden progress—which was real, because at last I did the sane thing: I understood first and explained afterwards. Let youth, whom the folly of age would imprison in ignorance, provide itself with Keys.

But the credit belongs to Dan.

Dan of the strong arm and soft smile; Dan the wise hater of useless labour; sharp-witted, easy-going Dan, who discovered that common sense—properly concealed—could be smuggled even into school.

He followed me a term later into the Lower Fourth, and before he had been there a week he handled Latin verse with an ease suggestive of unholy assistance. One day, in a lonely corner of Regent’s Park—after looking round to make sure no prig was within earshot—he revealed his magic.

“Don’t tell the others,” he commanded; “or it will get out, and then nobody’ll be any the better.”

“But is it right?” I asked.

“Look here, young ’un,” said Dan, with that practical morality of his, “what are you here for—what’s your father paying school fees for? For you to play a silly game, or to learn something?

“Because if it’s only a game—we boys against the masters—then let’s play by rule,” he continued. “But if we’re here to learn—well, you’ve been in the class four months and I’ve just come, and I’ll bet I know more Ovid than you do already.”

Which was true.

So I thanked him and shared his Key; and whatever Latin I remember—whatever good it may ever do me—I take it I owe to Dan.

And more than Latin, I owed him. His sound mother-wit was always at my disposal to correct my dreamy unfeasibility; he was my friend from first to last, and to be the chosen friend of Dan—shrewd judge of man and boy—seems to me no trifling feather in my cap.

He “took to” me, he said, because I was “jolly green”—“such a rummy little mug.” He would never give me a better reason, except a sweet smile and a rough tumble of my hair with his great hand. But I think I understood, in my small way. I loved him because he was big and handsome and kind; no one but a little boy knows how brutal or how kind a big boy can be.

I was still rather an effeminate little chap—nervous and shy—with a pink-and-white face and hair that no amount of wetting could persuade into straightness. I was growing too fast; the speed of it seemed to steal what strength I had. And the daily journey, with school work and home work, was perhaps too much for my years. Every morning I was up at six, out before seven, to catch the 7.15 from Poplar; then, from Chalk Farm, another couple of miles on foot. Yet I did not mind the walking, because at Chalk Farm station Dan was always waiting. In the afternoon we walked back together, and when I was tired and my back ached—as if somebody had cut a piece out of it—he would put his arm round me, for he always knew. How strong and restful it was to lean on! One walked as in an easy-chair.

Remembering how I walked thus by his side, shyly looking up into his face, thinking him so solid and good and feeling so glad he liked me, I can understand a little how a woman loves. With his arm around me, it was sweet to feel weak.

At first we were in the same class, the Lower Third. He had no business there. He was head and shoulders taller than any of us, years older. It was a disgrace that he was not in the Upper Fourth. The Doctor told him so before us all twenty times a week. Old Waterhouse—“Old Waterhouse,” because “Mister Waterhouse, M.A.” would convey nothing to my mind—liked him cordially and was honestly grieved. We, his friends, though it pleased us to have him among us, suffered in our pride of him.

The only person quite contented was Dan himself. That was his way. Other people held opinions on what was good for him; he had his own. And his own was the only opinion that influenced him. The Lower Third suited him. The Upper Fourth had no charm.

Even in the Lower Third he was always at the bottom—by preference. He selected the last seat and kept it in spite of allurements and reproaches. It was nearest the door, first out and last in. It also gave him a sense of retirement: screened a little from observation, the boy at the back becomes, with practice, almost forgotten. To Dan’s philosophical temperament its practical advantages outweighed every sentimental objection.

Only once do I remember him losing it. Generally, tiresome questions—past participles, square roots, meridians—never reached him, being snapped up on the way by arm-waving enthusiasts. The few that trickled to the back he ignored. They bored him and he never pretended they did not.

But one day, off his guard, he gave a correct answer—quite unconsciously—and found himself promoted to the front bench. I had never seen Dan out of temper before, but at that moment I am convinced that any whispered congratulation would have earned a punch.

Old Waterhouse thought he had achieved reform. “Come, Brian,” he cried, rubbing his long thin hands together, “after all you’re not such a fool as you pretend.”

“Never said I was,” muttered Dan, with one backward glance of regret towards his lost seclusion; and before the day was out he had manoeuvred himself back again.

As we went out together, Old Waterhouse passed us on the stairs. “Haven’t you any sense of shame, my boy?” he asked sorrowfully, laying a kind hand on Dan’s shoulder.

“Yes, sir,” answered Dan, with his frank smile; “plenty. It isn’t yours, that’s all.”

He was an excellent fighter. In a school of over two hundred boys, not half a dozen—Upper Sixth fellows who came in top hats with umbrellas, and who wouldn’t, out of regard for their own dignity—could have challenged him with any chance of success. Yet he fought very seldom, and then in a bored, lazy fashion, as though he were doing it purely to oblige the other boy.

One afternoon, just as we were about to enter Regent’s Park by the wicket opposite Hanover Gate, an errand boy with an empty basket, supported by two smaller boys, barred our way.

“Can’t come in here,” said Basket.

“Why not?” inquired Dan.

“’Cos if you do I shall kick you,” explained Basket simply.

Without a word Dan turned away, prepared to go on to the next opening. Basket, encouraged by this, followed us.

“Now I’m going to give you your coward’s blow,” he said, stepping in front; “will you take it quietly?” The Outer Circle is lonely on a winter afternoon.

“I’ll tell you afterwards,” said Dan, stopping short.

Basket slapped him lightly on the cheek. It could not have hurt; but the indignity—according to our code—was enormous. No boy of honour was supposed to accept it without retaliation.

“Is that all?” asked Dan.

“That’s all—for the present,” replied Basket.

“Good-bye,” said Dan, and walked on.

“Glad he didn’t insist on fighting,” Dan remarked cheerfully as we went. “I’m going to a party tonight.”

Yet on another occasion, in a street off Lisson Grove, he insisted on fighting a young rough half as big again as himself, who, brushing against him, had knocked his hat into the mud.

“I wouldn’t have said anything about his knocking it off,” Dan explained afterwards, tenderly brushing the bruised hat with his sleeve, “if he hadn’t kicked it.”

And another time—one broiling summer afternoon—three or four of us, Dan among us, were on our way to Hadley Woods. Just beyond Barnet we turned into the fields. Dan drew from his pocket an enormous pear, so juicy and fat it seemed indecently prosperous for a schoolboy.

“Where did you get that?” asked Dudley.

“From that big greengrocer’s opposite Barnet Church,” answered Dan. “Have a bit?”

“You told me you hadn’t any more money,” Dudley reproached him, taking the offered slice at the end of Dan’s pocket-knife.

“No more I had,” Dan replied.

“Then you must have had some, or you couldn’t have bought it.”

“Didn’t buy it.”

“Do you mean to say you stole it?”

“Yes.”

“You’re a thief,” Dudley denounced, wiping his mouth and throwing away a pip.

“I know it. So are you.”

“No, I’m not.”

“What’s the good of talking nonsense? You robbed an orchard only last Wednesday at Mill Hill and gave yourself the stomach-ache.”

“That isn’t stealing.”

“What is it?”

“It isn’t the same thing.”

“What’s the difference?”

Nothing could make Dan comprehend the difference. “Stealing is stealing,” he maintained, “whether you take it off a tree or out of a basket. You’re a thief, Dudley; so am I. Anybody else say a piece?”

The thermometer was at that point where morals grow slack and appetites preach louder than consciences. We all had a piece. We were all shocked at Dan, and told him so. It did not agitate him in the least.

To Dan I could speak my inmost thoughts, certain he would understand me; and sometimes he helped, and sometimes he left me deliciously confused. The yearly examination was approaching. My father and mother said nothing, but I knew how they waited for the result—my father to see what I had accomplished, my mother to see how hard I had tried. I had worked, and yet I doubted, for prizes depend less on what you know than on what you can persuade others you know; which applies to prizes beyond those of school.

“Are you going in for anything, Dan?” I asked as we crossed Primrose Hill one bright June morning. The question was absurd; I asked it because I wanted him to ask it of me.

“They’re not giving away anything I particularly want,” he murmured in his lazy drawl, looking at prizes with the practical eye of a boy who saw through gilt edges.

“You’re sweating yourself, young ’un, of course?” he asked next, as I expected.

“I mean to have a shot at the History,” I admitted. “Wish I was better at dates.”

“It’s always two-thirds dates,” Dan assured me, to my discouragement. “Old Florret thinks you can’t eat a potato till you know the date that chap Raleigh was born.”

“I’ve prayed so hard that I may win the History prize,” I told him. With Dan I never felt shy; he never laughed at me.

“You oughtn’t to have done that,” he said.

I stared. “But we’re told to pray for things we want.”

“Beastly mean way of getting ’em,” retorted Dan. “It isn’t fair to the other fellows. That won’t be your winning the prize; that’ll be your getting it through favouritism.”

“But they can pray, too,” I reminded him.

“If you all pray for it,” said Dan, “then it’ll go—not to the fellow that knows most history—but to the fellow that’s prayed the hardest. That isn’t old Florret’s idea, I’m sure.”

No argument, neither then nor later, ever brought him round on that point. Dan judged everything for himself. In his opinion Achilles was a coward, not a hero.

“He ought to have told the Trojans they couldn’t hurt any part of him except his heel, and let ’em have a fair shot at that,” he argued. “King Arthur and all the rest with their magic swords—it wasn’t playing the game. There’s no pluck in fighting if you know you’re bound to win. Beastly cads, I call them all.”

I won no prize that year. Dan, oddly enough, did—arithmetic; the only subject in the Lower Fourth that interested him.

“I like to see things coming right,” he explained.

My father shut himself up with me for half an hour and examined me himself.

“It’s very curious, Paul,” he said; “you seem to know a good deal.”

“They asked me all the things I didn’t know,” I blurted out, and laid my head upon my arm. “They seemed to do it on purpose.”

My father crossed the room and sat down beside me.

“Spud!” he said—it was long since he had used that childish nickname—“perhaps you are going to be with me, one of the unlucky ones.”

“Are you unlucky?” I asked.

“Invariably,” he answered, rumpling his hair. “I don’t know why. I try hard—I do the right thing, but it turns out wrong. It always does.”

“But I thought Mr. Hasluck was bringing us such good fortune,” I said, looking up. “We’re getting on, aren’t we?”

“I have thought so before, so often,” he replied, “and it has always ended in a— in a collapse.”

I put my arms round his neck, for I always felt towards my father as towards another boy: bigger than myself and older, but not so very much older.

“You see, when I married your mother,” he went on, “I was a rich man. She had everything she wanted.”

“But you will get it all back,” I cried.

“I try to think so,” he said. “I do think so—generally speaking. But there are times—you would not understand—they come to you.”

“But she is happy,” I persisted. “We are all happy.”

He shook his head.

“I watch her,” he said. “Women suffer more than we do. They live more in the present. I see my hopes; but she—she sees only me, and I have always been a failure. She has lost faith in me.”

I could say nothing. I understood, but dimly.

“That is why I want you to be an educated man, Paul,” he continued after a silence. “You can’t think what a help education is to a man. I don’t mean it helps you to get on in the world; I think for that it rather hampers you. But it helps you to bear adversity. To a man with a well-stored mind, life is interesting on a piece of bread and a cup of tea. I know. If it were not for you and your mother I should not trouble.”

And yet at that time, so far as I remember, our fortunes were at their brightest; and when they grew dark again he would be full of fresh hope, planning and scheming and dreaming once more. It was never acting. A worse actor never trod our stage. His occasional attempts at cheerfulness he did not feel always ended with the three of us crying in one another’s arms. It was only when things went well that experience rose up against him. Child of misfortune, he was like Antaeus: he drew strength by touching his mother earth.

Nor must it be supposed that his despondency was frequent. Generally speaking, as he would say, he was full of confidence. Already he had fixed upon our new house in Guilford Street—then still good and residential—and at the same time sufficiently central for office purposes, near Lincoln’s Inn and Gray’s Inn and Bedford Row, those pavements worn by weary footsteps of the Law’s sad courtiers.

“Poplar,” said my father, “has disappointed me. It seemed a good idea—a rapidly rising district, singularly destitute of solicitors. It ought to have turned out well, and yet somehow it hasn’t.”

“There have been a few come,” my mother reminded him.

“Of a sort,” admitted my father. “A criminal lawyer might gather something of a practice here, no doubt. But for general work, of course, you must be in a central position. Now, in Guilford Street people will come to me.”

“It should certainly be a pleasanter neighbourhood to live in,” agreed my mother.

“Later on,” said my father, “in case I want the whole house for offices, we could live ourselves in Regent’s Park. It is quite near to the Park.”

“Of course you have consulted Mr. Hasluck?” asked my mother, who was, of the two, far more practical.

“For Hasluck,” replied my father, “it will be much more convenient. He grumbles every time at the distance.”

“I have never been quite able to understand,” said my mother, “why Mr. Hasluck should have come so far out of his way. There must surely be plenty of solicitors in the City.”

“There’s nothing missing,” said my mother, turning out drawers as though the parlour-maid might be folded among the tablecloths, “so far as I can find out. Depend upon it, that’s the explanation: she has got frightened and has run away.”

“But what was there to frighten her?” said my father, arrested mid-pour, one hand holding the decanter like a trophy, the other the bottle as if it could testify.

“It was the idea of the thing,” replied my mother. “She has never been used to waiting at table. She was actually crying about it only last night.”

“But what’s to be done?” said my father. “They will be here in less than an hour.”

“There will be no dinner for them,” said my mother, with the calm of a woman looking at the abyss, “unless I put on an apron and bring it up myself.”

“Where does she live?” asked my father.

“At Ilford.”

“We must make a joke of it,” said my father, because jokes were the only thing in his armoury that didn’t require a plumber.

My mother sat down and began to cry.

It had been a trying week for her—trying in the particular way a domestic week can be, when it is asked to pretend it is a week in a grander household. A dinner party—to a real dinner, with anchovies at the start and ices at the finish (and the private terror that the ices, corrupted by our inexperienced air, would present themselves as cold custard)—was an astonishing departure from the steady, modest diet of our life. First there had been the cleaning: not the ordinary sweeping-away of crumbs, but the rearranging of the very soul of the house, as though a guest might be able to smell last year’s economy through the wallpaper. Then the purchases: little things that Need never even thinks to ask for, but which Ostentation—if you once admit her sniffing nose across your threshold—demands immediately, with a curled lip.

And then, as inevitably as a sermon after a hymn, the kitchen range took offence. It went on strike with the dignity of a labour union led by an elderly boiler with a grievance, refusing to bake another dish except under “improved conditions,” meaning, translated, weary days of plumbers clanking tools and whistling out of tune, while the kitchen filled with that peculiar metallic wet smell of disturbed pipes and damp coal-dust.

Cookery books—fat, reproachful volumes long left to sleep upright on their shelf—were hauled down, opened, argued with, insulted, and appealed to. My mother tried sauces, failed, tried again, succeeded, and wrote anxious little sums in the margins as though a béchamel were a moral expenditure. The air for days had been alive with domestic music: the knife’s quick, reassuring tap on the board; the hiss of fat when it finally consented to behave; the low bubbling of stock like talk in another room; and, above all, the tyrannical tick of the clock, which in such weeks becomes less a timepiece than a judge.

Victory had been in sight. And now—this.

Downstairs in the kitchen would be the dinner, waiting: the fish with its faint sea-sweetness; vegetables turned and trimmed until they looked like polite company; gravies reduced to the glossy point where they cling to a spoon like affection. Upstairs, around the glittering table, would be the guests, waiting for their dinner. But between the two yawned an impassable gulf. The bridge—our parlour-maid—had bolted without warning, and was probably, at that moment, on a rattling train to Ilford, leaving behind only the soundless mockery of her absence.

There was excuse for my mother’s tears.

“Isn’t it possible to get somebody else?” asked my father.

“Impossible, in the time,” said my mother. “I had been training her for the whole week. We had rehearsed it perfectly.”

“Have it in the kitchen,” suggested my aunt, folding napkins into what she insisted were ships, though they resembled nothing more nautical than damp handkerchiefs with ambitions, “and call it a picnic.”

It truly seemed the only practical solution.

A light knock came at the front door.

“It can’t be anybody yet, surely,” exclaimed my father, alarmed into motion and reaching for his coat as if a coat could repel guests.

“It’s Barbara, I expect,” said my mother. “She promised to come round and help me dress. But now, of course, I shan’t want her.” My mother could make a cloud out of sunshine if you gave her the materials.

I, however, slipped out to admit Barbara, because even then I had learned a household truth: when Barbara came in through the front door, shadows tended to depart by the window.

She ran into the room and kissed my father, my mother, and my aunt with such speed that it looked like one bright, continuous movement, like a bird’s wing brushing three branches.

At once—simply by being there—she changed the sound of the room. Before, it had been the damp thud of distress, the awkward clink of decanter and bottle, the faint sobbing that makes wallpaper seem to listen. Now there was quick breathing, the rustle of her skirts, the light, confident tap of her heels; and somehow the whole domestic disaster began to sound like a story you might later tell and laugh over.

“There’s going to be no dinner,” laughed my father. “We are going to look surprised and pretend that it was yesterday. It will be fun to see their faces.”

“There will be a very nice dinner,” said my mother, and even she smiled a little, “but it will be in the kitchen, and there’s no way of getting it upstairs.” And they explained to Barbara the calamity: the vanished parlour-maid and the stranded feast.

Barbara’s face grew grave for one instant—grave in the way a good cook looks when the sauce threatens to split. Then her eyes lit.

“I’ll get you someone,” she said.

“My dear, you don’t even know the neighbourhood,” began my mother.

But Barbara had already snatched the latchkey from its nail and flown.

With her going, the room lost its tune again, as though a violin had been carried away and the rest left scraping at their parts.

“If there were only an hotel in this beastly neighbourhood,” said my father.

“You must entertain them by yourself, Luke,” said my mother. “And I must wait—that’s all.”

“Don’t be absurd, Maggie,” cried my father, with that angry edge men use when they are afraid. “Can’t cook bring it in?”

“No one can cook a dinner and serve it, too,” answered my mother, impatiently. “Besides, she’s not presentable.”

“What about Fan?” whispered my father.

My mother merely looked at him. That look closed the topic more firmly than a lock.

“Paul?” suggested my father, turning to me as if I were a piece of spare furniture that might be used to prop up disaster.

“Thank you,” retorted my mother. “I don’t choose to have my son turned into a footman, if you do.”

“Well, hadn’t you better go and dress?” was my father’s next remark, as though he could shove the problem into a wardrobe and button it.

“It won’t take me long to put on an apron,” was my mother’s reply.

“I was looking forward to seeing you in that new frock,” said my father.

In another man you might have called it tact. In my father it was sheer happy accident—as when you drop a loaf and it lands butter-side up.

My mother confessed, speaking with the indulgence one allows one’s own foolish hopes once they are spoiled, that she too had been looking forward to seeing herself therein. Their threatened discord melted, not into agreement exactly, but into that mutual tenderness two people find in the ruins of a shared plan.

“I so wanted everything to be all right, for your sake, Luke,” said my mother. “I know you were hoping it would help on the business.”

“I was only thinking of you, Maggie, dear,” answered my father. “You are my business.”

“I know, dear,” she said, and her voice held that weary love which is like broth kept warm too long: still nourishing, but trembling at the edges. “It is hard.”

Then the key turned in the lock and we all fell quiet to listen, as if the house itself were eavesdropping.

“She’s come back alone,” said my mother. “I knew it was hopeless.”

The door opened.

“Please, ma’am,” said a new voice, bright as a struck spoon, “will I do?”

There she stood framed by the lintel: Barbara, transformed. The daintiest apron, the daintiest cap set on her golden hair; and she swept away objections as if they were crumbs with the airy, merry wilfulness that made people obey her and be grateful for it.

“You shall be footman,” she ordered, turning to me.

This time my mother only laughed—laughed, indeed, with relief so sharp it was almost a sob.

“Wait here till I come down again.”

Then, to my mother, briskly and commandingly, as though timing a dish: “Now, ma’am, are you ready?”

It was the first time I had seen my mother in evening dress. To tell the truth it shocked me. Not because she looked wrong—she looked astonishingly right—but because it was like hearing a familiar tune played on a new instrument. Her shoulders shone white, too white for the lamplight, and I felt, childishly, as though something private had been put on display. I must have shown it, because she flushed and drew her shawl over herself, murmuring about cold.

Barbara cried out that it was a sin to hide such beauty. My father, with a quick hand—so quick it suggested practice—filched the shawl away. Then he dropped on one knee as if the world had turned into a picture book where gentlemen behave as they ought, took my mother’s hand, and kissed it gravely. Barbara, standing behind my mother’s chair, insisted I do the same, declaring the Queen was receiving. So I knelt too, glancing up shyly, and caught my first glimpse of the strange philosophy of clothes: how fabric and cut can make you see, for a moment, a person you thought you already knew.

My memory lingers there, by contrast with the swift, sad days that followed—days when my mother’s eyes would flash at my father, and her voice ring cruel and hard; then, the moment he was gone, her lips would tremble and her eyes soften and fill. Days when my father would sit with his face averted, lips pressed tight, or else open them only to fling out savage words; then storm away, slamming the door, and later I would find him alone in the dark with his head bowed between his hands.

At night I lay awake hearing through the thin walls the fierce rise and fall of their voices, now high, now forced into cold whispers that sounded crueller for their quietness. In their estrangement—so new to them—both clung closer to me, though neither told me anything, and I should not have understood if they had. When my mother sobbed with her arms tight around me, I hated my father, thinking he had made her cry. When my father drew me on his knee and I looked into his kind eyes full of pain, I felt angry with my mother, remembering her bitter tongue.

It seemed as though some unseen thing had crept into the house, silent as dust, to stand always between them, so they could never look into each other’s loving eyes but only into the eyes of this evil shadow. The idea grew until I could almost feel it: a coldness passing in the air, the sense of a presence listening. It trod through our pokey rooms with no sound, yet in my mind it had a sort of acoustic signature—like the hush that falls when a cart stops outside and you know, without seeing, that someone has come to the door. It waited, alert, to thrust its grinning face between them. Beside my mother it would whisper; then it would slide across to my father and answer with his voice, but altered. Sometimes I fancied I could hear it laugh to itself, the soft, smug laugh of something that thrives on misheard words.

To this day I seem to see it behind my shoulder when I read a love tale, reading too, silently amused. I cannot pass boy and girl whispering in twilight without imagining it lurking at the edge of their half-light, ready, patient, certain.

Shall any of us escape, or must all roads lead through that valley where love turns to shadow? Is it an ordeal, sorting the weak from the strong—those who will find each other again once the darkness has gone?

Of the dinner itself, until the time of dessert, I can give no consecutive account. As footman under the direction of this enthusiastic parlour-maid, my place was no sinecure. I spent the evening in a marching rhythm between rooms: the kitchen’s rich heat and clamour, where lids clattered, plates rang, and the cook’s voice rose like a kettle; the passage where the smell of roast and hot metal mingled with furniture polish; and the dining-room with its hush of company, interrupted by silver’s bright little chimes and the occasional explosion of laughter.

There were anchovies—salty little shocks laid neatly like small, sophisticated fish on toast; soup that steamed up carrying an honest scent of bones, herbs, and patient simmering; a joint whose carved surface shone, the gravy thickened to that perfect point where it coats and does not run; vegetables that had been coaxed into tenderness rather than bullied into it; and, at last, the feared ices, which—by a miracle and the swift management of kitchen and time—remained ices and did not lapse into cold custard.

All I knew clearly was that the chief guest was a Mr. Teidelmann—or Tiedelmann; I cannot now remember which—a snuffy, mumbling old frump whose name I had seen often enough in huge letters, with a “& Co.” added, painted on dreary blank walls along Limehouse Reach. He sat at my mother’s right hand. Watching him—ugly, foolish-seeming, and profoundly deaf—I wondered how my mother could be so animated with him, shouting much and often to make herself heard, smiling, attending to his wants with an almost festive care, as though politeness were another course to be served hot.

Even old Hasluck, opposite, who though pleasant enough had no great reverence for manners, roared himself purple praising some new disinfectant of which Teidelmann appeared to be the proprietor.

“My wife swears by it!” bellowed Hasluck, leaning across the table.

“Our drains!” chimed in Mrs. Hasluck, a homely soul. “Well, you’d hardly know there was any in the house since I’ve took to using it.”

“What are they talking about?” asked Teidelmann, appealing to my mother. “What’s he say his wife does?”

“Your disinfectant,” explained my mother. “Mrs. Hasluck swears by it.”

“Who?”

“Mrs. Hasluck.”

“Does she? Delighted to hear it,” grunted the old gentleman, bored by his own product.

“Nothing like it for a sick-room,” persisted Hasluck. “Might almost call it a scent.”

“Makes one quite anxious to be ill,” remarked my aunt, aiming her comment at the general air.

“Reminds me of cocoanuts,” continued Hasluck.

Teidelmann appeared not to hear, but Hasluck could not endure the thought of flattery wasted.

“I say it reminds me of cocoanuts,” he screamed, as if cocoanuts were an accusation.

“Oh, does it?” came the reply.

“Doesn’t it you?”

“Can’t say it does,” answered Teidelmann. “As a matter of fact, don’t know much about it myself. Never use it.”

Hasluck, having at last succeeded in killing the subject, let it lie. But as I went past again, I heard him laugh, easy and unoffended.

“He’s quite right,” was Hasluck’s comment. “That’s what I am undoubtedly. Because I can’t talk about anything but shop myself, I think everybody else is the same sort of fool.”

He did himself an injustice, because on my next passage he was shouting again, only now Teidelmann sounded interested.

“Well, if you could spare the time, I’d be more obliged than I can tell you,” Hasluck was saying. “I know absolutely nothing about pictures myself, and Pearsall says you are one of the best judges in Europe.”

“He ought to know,” chuckled Teidelmann. “He’s tried often enough to palm off rubbish onto me.”

“That last purchase of yours must have been a good thing for young—” Hasluck mentioned a painter’s name that years later would have made even servants prick up their ears. “Been the making of him, I should say.”

“I gave him two thousand for the six,” replied Teidelmann, “and they’ll sell for twenty thousand.”

“But you’ll never sell them?” exclaimed my father.

“No,” grunted Teidelmann, “but my widow will.”

There came a soft, low laugh from a corner of the table I could not see, a laugh like a spoon dropped gently into a glass—musical, controlled, faintly mocking.

“It’s Anderson’s great disappointment,” followed a languid, caressing voice—her laugh translated into speech—“that he has never been able to educate me to a proper appreciation of art. He’ll pay thousands of pounds for a child in rags or a badly dressed Madonna. Such a waste of money, it appears to me.”

“But you would pay thousands for a diamond to hang upon your neck,” argued my father.

“It would enhance the beauty of my neck,” replied the voice.

“An even more absolute waste of money,” said my father, low.

And again that soft laugh, like the rustle of expensive silk.

“Who is she?” I asked Barbara.

We were sitting on the stairs, sharing a jelly. It wobbled between us in a glass dish, catching the light in trembling red, smelling faintly of fruit and sugar and that medicinal hint gelatine always keeps no matter how prettily it dresses.

“The second Mrs. Teidelmann,” whispered Barbara. “She is quite a swell. Married him for his money—I don’t like her myself, but she’s very beautiful.”

“As beautiful as you?” I asked, incredulous.

“Oh, me!” answered Barbara, and for once she sounded almost out of conceit. “I’m only a child. Nobody takes any notice of me—except other kids, like you.”

“But everybody thinks you beautiful,” I maintained.

“Who?” she asked quickly.

“Dr. Hal,” I answered.

We sat with our backs to the light so I could not see her face, only the tilt of her cap and the shine of her hair.

“What did he say?” she asked, and her voice had more content in it.

I could not remember his exact words, but the sense of them I was sure of.

“Ask him what he thinks of me, as if you wanted to know yourself,” Barbara instructed me, “and don’t forget what he says this time. I’m curious.”

It seemed a foolish command—what could he say that I had not already told her?—but Barbara’s wishes were like recipes: you did not question them; you followed them, and hoped for success.

If I am right in thinking that jealousy of Mrs. Teidelmann clouded Barbara’s sunny nature for a moment, she had no cause. No one drew more attention throughout the dinner than our sudden parlour-maid.

“Where ever did you get her from?” asked Mrs. Florret, as Barbara came down again from the kitchen stairs.

“A neat-handed Phillis,” commented Dr. Florret, with approval.

“I’ll take good care she never waits at my table,” laughed the wife of our minister, the Rev. Cottle—a broad-built, breezy-voiced woman, mother of eleven, eight of them boys.

“To tell the truth,” said my mother, “she’s only here temporarily.”

“As a matter of fact,” said my father, “we have to thank Mrs. Hasluck for her.”

“Don’t leave me out of it,” laughed Hasluck. “Can’t let the old girl take all the credit.”

Later my father, absent-minded, addressed Barbara as “My dear,” at which Mrs. Cottle shot a swift glance at my mother. Before that glance could cool, Hasluck—when no one was looking—pinched Barbara’s elbow. It would not have mattered had Barbara not, startled, let out an involuntary “augh,” like a kettle protesting. For the reputation of the house—and because the dinner was nearing its end—my mother judged it best to take the whole company into her confidence and tell them the truth.

Naturally the story raised Barbara’s admiration still higher. So when dessert arrived and Barbara, discarding the apron but wisely retaining the dainty cap that set off her hair, sat down among the guests with me—her footman—she was more than ever the centre of attention.

“It was very nice of you,” said Mrs. Cottle, completing the circle of compliments, “and, as I always tell my girls, that is better than being beautiful.”

“Kind hearts,” added Dr. Florret, summing up as if delivering a verdict, “are more than coronets.”

Dr. Florret always had the correct quotation ready. Yet from him it never irritated; it fell on the ear like the Amen in church: not an ornament, but a necessary finish.

Only my aunt could not let the moment lie.

“When I was a girl,” she said, breaking upon the pause, “I used to look into the glass and say to myself: ‘Fanny, you’ve got to be amiable,’ and I was amiable,” added my aunt, challenging contradiction with her eye. “Nobody can say that I wasn’t, for years.”

“It didn’t pay?” suggested Hasluck.

“It attracted,” replied my aunt, “no attention whatever.”

Hasluck had changed places with my mother and—after experiments—had found the correct pitch for conversation with old Teidelmann. Hasluck never wasted time on anything but business. It was in his opera box on the first night of Verdi’s Aida (I speak of days then to come) that he arranged the details of his celebrated guano deal; and even his religion, so I have been told and can well believe, he varied to suit the enterprise of the moment, once, during the protracted preliminaries of a cocoa scheme, becoming converted to Quakerism.

For most of the rest, the interest lay in a discussion between Washburn and Florret about the superior advantages of living in the East End.

As a rule, incorrect opinion found it hard to exist in Dr. Florret’s presence. As no bird, they say, can continue its song once looked at by an owl, so originality tended to fall silent under the cold stare of his disapproval. But Dr. “Fighting Hal” was no delicate warbler. Vehement and direct, indifferent to niceties, he swept through polite argument like a strong wind through a murmuring wood, carrying his friends farther than they meant to go and leaving his opponents clinging—upside down, if necessary—to their perches, angry and ruffled.

“Life!” flung out Washburn—after Dr. Florret had laid down, once again, unimpeachable rules for the conduct of all mankind. “What do you respectable folk know of life? You are not men and women, you are marionettes. You don’t move to your natural emotions implanted by God; you dance according to the latest book of etiquette. You live and love, laugh and weep and sin by rule. Only one moment do you come face to face with life; that is in the moment when you die, leaving the other puppets to be dressed in black and make believe to cry.”

It was a favourite subject of his, the artificiality of us all.

“Little doll,” he had once called me, and I had resented it.

“That’s all you are, little Paul,” he had persisted, “a good little hard-working doll, that does what it’s made to do, and thinks what it’s made to think. We are all dolls. Your father is a gallant-hearted, soft-headed little doll; your mother the sweetest and primmest of dolls. And I’m a silly, dissatisfied doll that longs to be a man, but hasn’t the pluck. We are only dolls, little Paul.”

“He’s a trifle— a trifle whimsical on some subjects,” my father had explained when I repeated it.

“There are a certain class of men,” my mother had explained, “you will meet with them more as you grow up—who talk for talking’s sake. They don’t know what they mean. And nobody else does either.”

“But what would you have?” argued Dr. Florret now. “That every man should do that which is right in his own eyes?”

“Far better than, like the old man in the fable, he should do what every other fool thinks right,” retorted Washburn. “The other day I called to see whether a patient of mine was still alive or not. His wife was washing clothes in the front room. ‘How’s your husband?’ I asked. ‘I think he’s dead,’ replied the woman. Then, without leaving off her work, ‘Jim,’ she shouted, ‘are you there?’ No answer came from the inner room. ‘He’s a goner,’ she said, wringing out a stocking.”

“But surely,” said Dr. Florret, “you don’t admire a woman for being indifferent to the death of her husband?”

“I don’t admire her for that,” replied Washburn, “and I don’t blame her. I didn’t make the world and I’m not responsible for it. What I do admire her for is not pretending a grief she didn’t feel. In Berkeley Square she’d have met me at the door with an agonised face and a handkerchief to her eyes.”

“Assume a virtue, if you have it not,” murmured Dr. Florret.

“Go on,” said Washburn. “How does it run? ‘That monster, custom, who all sense doth eat, of devil’s habit, is angel yet in this, that to the use of actions fair and good he gives a frock that aptly is put on.’ So was the lion’s skin by the ass, but it showed him only the more an ass. Here asses go about as asses, but there are lions also. I had a woman under my hands only a little while ago. I could have cured her easily. Why she got worse every day instead of better I could not understand. Then by accident learned the truth: instead of helping me she was doing all she could to kill herself. ‘I must, Doctor,’ she cried. ‘I must. I have promised. If I get well he will only leave me, and if I die now he has sworn to be good to the children.’ Here, I tell you, they live—think their thoughts, work their will, kill those they hate, die for those they love; savages if you like, but savage men and women, not bloodless dolls.”

“I prefer the dolls,” concluded Dr. Florret.

“I admit they are pretty,” answered Washburn.

“I remember,” said my father, “the first masked ball I ever went to when I was a student in Paris. It struck me just as you say, Hal; everybody was so exactly alike. I was glad to get out into the street and see faces.”

“But I thought they always unmasked at midnight,” said the second Mrs. Teidelmann in her soft, languid tones.

“I did not wait,” explained my father.
