“That was a pity,” she replied. “I should have been interested to see what they were like, underneath.”

“I might have been disappointed,” said my father. “I agree with Dr. Florret that sometimes the mask is an improvement.”

Better is little, than treasure and trouble therewith. The proverb had always sounded to me like something written by a man who had never had to decide whether to buy dripping or treacle. Yet now, when money came in regularly and the table grew ambitious, the old words turned spitefully wise.

Not only on Sundays, but on weekdays too, the joint arrived—steaming, glossy, and self-satisfied—followed by pudding, and then by another pudding, until pudding, that former festival in a basin, became as common as the salt. The air of the room was thick with good gravy and suet-sweetness; knives no longer needed to scrape the last atom from a plate as if it were a last shilling. We ate without the old arithmetic of leftovers: no planning the morrow’s hash, no covert saving of crusts for Saturday’s bread pudding, no solemn cutting of bread instead of breaking it because breakage meant waste.

And yet never now were we merry.

Something sat down with us—an unseen guest who ate nothing, drank nothing, and still managed to empty the whole room. The clatter of plates sounded too loud, then too small; conversation became a careful stirring of thin, tasteless matters, as if any real word might boil over. Even the domestic sounds seemed altered: the kettle’s song thinner, the street’s grind harsher, as though the house had lost the right to its own quiet.

It would have been easy enough to move to Guilford Street. Sometimes, in those spiritless tones in which they now spoke on every subject save the one, my mother and father would mention it—numbers, rent, “a little nearer”—and then some loosened thought would fall into the talk like grit into flour, and all at once nothing would rise. So the months limped by, and the cloud grew accustomed to our ceiling.

Then one day news came that old Teidelmann had died suddenly in his counting-house.

“You are going to her?” said my mother.

“I have been sent for,” said my father. “I must—it may mean business.”

My mother laughed—a short, bitter sound, like a spoon dropped on stone—and my father flung out of the house. During the hours he was away she locked herself into her room. Once or twice I crept to the door and heard her crying, the muffled sobs as steady as a clock. That she should grieve for old Teidelmann puzzled me; I had seen him only as an ugly little man with thick lips and a habit of looking through people as if they were panes of glass.

After that, she came oftener to our house.

Her mourning—so I thought then—made her more beautiful. Black suited her; it simplified her, as a dark sauce makes a dish look costly. It softened, or seemed to soften, the hardness of her eyes. Always she was very sweet to my mother, who beside her appeared pale and plain and stiff, like yesterday’s bread laid next to a frosted cake. To me—whatever her motive—she was kindness itself. She seldom arrived without some little gift: a book that smelled of good paper and travel, a toy chosen with uncanny accuracy, a plan for a treat that hit precisely the place my own childish wanting lived.

By instinct she understood what I desired—the stories that set my mind alight, the pleasures that actually pleased me, not the pleasures that ought to have pleased me. My mother’s plans for my future, spoken with love and certainty, chilled me with their endless walls of “Must” and “Must not,” a narrow lane with no sky. This soft-voiced lady made life seem like sunny fields and winding paths where one might follow Will and still arrive safely. Something in me went out to her—drawn by her sympathy, her swift comprehension of what I had not yet learned to say. And yet, under it, there was always a small fear, as of a knife too sharp to be played with.

One morning she looked at me with that whimsical screw of her mouth.

“Has he ever seen a pantomime?” she asked my father.

My heart leaped as if someone had lifted a lid in my chest. My father raised his eyebrows.

“What would your mother say, do you think?” he asked.

My heart sank again, heavy as dumpling dough.

“She thinks,” I replied, “that theatres are very wicked places.” It was the first time a doubt—quick as a pinprick—had entered my mind about my mother’s judgments.

Mrs. Teidelmann’s smile deepened it.

“Dear me,” she said, “then I am afraid I must be very wicked. I have always regarded a pantomime as quite a moral entertainment. All the bad people go down so very straight to—well, to the fit and proper place for them. And we could promise to leave before the Clown stole the sausages, couldn’t we, Paul?”

“Before the sausages,” I thought, was rather like saying one would leave a dinner before the pudding. But my father called my mother in, and she came—pale face, plain dark frock, hands clasping and unclasping as if she were kneading invisible dough.

“You will let him come, Mrs. Kelver,” Mrs. Teidelmann pleaded, her voice like warm milk poured over hard bread. “It’s Dick Whittington, you know—such an excellent moral.”

My mother stood silent, lips trembling. Important as the matter seemed to me, her agitation was larger than the question.

“I am very sorry,” said my mother at last. “It is very kind of you. But I would rather he did not go.”

“Just this once,” persisted Mrs. Teidelmann. “It is holiday time.”

A ray of sunlight fell into the room and lit Mrs. Teidelmann’s coaxing face, making the corner where my mother stood seem dimmer by comparison. I remember noticing it then, with a child’s exactness: how even the light seemed to take sides.

“I would rather he did not go,” my mother repeated. Her voice had turned harsh and grating, like a chair scraped the wrong way. “When he is older others must judge for him, but for the present he must be guided by me—alone.”

“I really don’t think there could be any harm, Maggie,” urged my father. “Things have changed since we were young.”

“That may be,” answered my mother, in the same harsh voice. “It is long ago since then.”

“I didn’t intend it that way,” said my father with a short laugh.

“I merely meant that I may be wrong,” said my mother. “I seem so old among you all—so out of place. I have tried to change, but I cannot.”

“We will say no more about it,” said Mrs. Teidelmann sweetly. “I merely thought it would give him pleasure; and he has worked so hard this last term, his father tells me.”

She laid her hand on my shoulder and kept it there, drawing me a little towards her as if I were already half her property.

“It was very kind of you,” said my mother. “I would do anything to give him pleasure, anything—I could. He knows that. He understands.”

I knew my mother’s hand was seeking mine, but I was angry and would not see. Without another word she left the room.

My mother said nothing more about the pantomime. But the very next afternoon she took me herself to a hall in the neighbourhood, where we saw a magic-lantern and then a conjurer. She had put on a prettier frock than she had worn for many a long day, and she tried to be bright. She laughed and talked as if laughter could be stirred up like batter if one only beat hard enough.

But I, nursing my wrongs, remained moody and sulky. At any other time such rare amusement would have overjoyed me—the lantern’s huge faces swelling on the sheet, the conjurer’s shiny apparatus laid out with a cook’s confidence before a crowd that could not see the trick. There was even a sort of culinary magic in it: empty hands producing things, like a cupboard that gives up a rabbit when you expected only flour. An open-mouthed sleeper swallowed an endless chain of rats; a live rabbit appeared from the stout old gentleman’s hat—the very last sort of hat one would choose for rabbit-breeding. The hall smelled of damp coats, orange peel, gas, and dusty boards, and the applause went off like a clapping of frying-pans.

None of it could draw my mind away from the great theatre I had built in my imagination out of boys’ boasts and gaudy posters: a place of colour and thunder, of music loud enough to drown a whole week’s grey. I had seen the joy, just for a moment, as one glimpses a roast turning in a bright kitchen and is then sent back to bread and water.

So we walked home through muddy, darkening streets. The city’s noise pressed close: wheels grinding, voices rebounding from brick, the harsh hiss of gas, and over it all the ceaseless industrial breath of London that never seemed to sleep but only to change its rhythm. It was as if the streets themselves chewed, endlessly, on iron.

That night I woke—or half woke, the way children do—and thought I saw a figure in white crouching at the foot of my bed. I must have fallen asleep again. Later—I cannot tell if it was minutes or an hour—I opened my eyes and saw it still there. Terror came on me like a cold hand and I cried out. The figure rose from its knees: my mother.

She laughed, a broken curious laugh that did not belong to ordinary things.

“It was a silly dream I had,” she explained quickly. “I must have been thinking of the conjurer we saw. I dreamt that a wicked Magician had spirited you away from me. I could not find you and was all alone in the world.”

She put her arms round me so tight it almost hurt. Her cheek against mine was hot and damp, and I could smell on her the day’s faint scent of soap and the stale, sweetish air of the hall. Thus we remained until I fell asleep again, held like something she was afraid the dark might steal.

It was towards the close of these same holidays that my mother and I called upon Mrs. Teidelmann in her great stone-built house at Clapton. She had sent a note that morning saying she suffered from terrible headaches that quite took her senses away, so she could not come out. She would be leaving England in a few days to travel. Would my mother come and see her—she wished to say good-bye.

My mother handed the letter across the breakfast-table to my father.

“Of course you will go,” said my father. “Poor girl, I wonder what the cause can be. She used to be so free from everything of the kind.”

“Do you think it well for me to go?” said my mother. “What can she have to say to me?”

“Oh, just to say good-bye,” said my father. “It would look so pointed not to go.”

The house was dull and sombre without, but one entered it as through a commonplace door into Aladdin’s cave. Old Teidelmann had collected all his life. His treasures, now scattered through a dozen galleries, were then piled here in a rich confusion that made one’s eyes ache like a child’s at a sweetshop window. Pictures filled every inch of wall, leaned against wonderful old furniture, lay unframed even across the ceilings. Statues gleamed from corners—some of them, I remember, were the only things Mrs. Teidelmann kept for herself. Carvings, embroideries, priceless china, miniatures framed in gems, illuminated missals and gorgeously bound books crowded one another like loaves on a baker’s shelf at dawn.

The ugly little thick-lipped man had surrounded himself with beauty from every age and land, as if he could buy what nature had refused him. He himself must have been the cheapest thing in his own rooms; and now he lay shrivelled in his coffin under a monument that made an unknown cemetery famous.

We were shown up to Mrs. Teidelmann’s boudoir. She lay on a sofa near the fire, asleep, dressed in a loose lace robe that fell away and showed her thin, snow-white arms. Her rich dark hair was loose about her. In sleep she looked less beautiful—harder, with a suggestion of coarseness in the face that by day did not show. My mother said we would wait; perhaps she would wake. The servant closed the door softly and left us alone.

An old French clock on the mantelpiece—Cupids holding up a heart—ticked with a muffled, soothing sound, a domestic beat that seemed to insist on calm. My mother chose a chair by the window and sat with her eyes fixed on the sleeping woman’s face. It seemed to me—though it may have been fancy born later—that a faint smile loosened for a moment the pressed lips of the sleeper, as if she tasted, even in dreams, something pleasant.

Neither my mother nor I spoke. The only sound was the hush-tick of the gilt clock, a small kindness in a room full of costly things.

Then the sleeping woman stirred. Confused murmurs came first, then clearer words. I heard her whisper my father’s name. Very low—hardly more than breathed—yet in the silence each syllable struck bright and distinct.

“Ah no, we must not. Luke, my darling.”

My mother rose so swiftly her chair creaked. But her voice, when she spoke, was curiously matter-of-fact, as if giving an order about errands.

“Go, Paul,” she said. “Wait for me downstairs.”

She opened the door noiselessly, pushed me gently out, and closed it behind me.

I waited in the strange house among the mute beauty and the servants’ quiet movements. Every sound seemed magnified: the far clink of china, the faint brush of a skirt, the echo of my own breathing. The place had its own acoustic—soft, rich, padded by carpets and curtains as if sound itself were another treasure to be arranged and controlled. It made me think, childishly, of how our own home had grown louder with silence, which is the noisiest thing of all.

It was half an hour or more before my mother came down. At once we left, letting ourselves out. All the way home she did not speak. She walked as one in a dream, eyes that saw not. Only when her hand touched the lock of our gate did she seem to return.

“You must say nothing, Paul, do you understand?” she said. “When people are delirious they use strange words that have no meaning. Do you understand, Paul; you must never breathe a word—never.”

I promised. We went in.

From that day my mother’s whole manner changed. Not another angry word escaped her lips. Never again did her eyes flash. Mrs. Teidelmann remained away three months. My father wrote to her often, of course—he managed her affairs. But my mother wrote also—though I do not think my father knew—long letters she would go away by herself to pen, always in the twilight near the window.

“Why do you choose this time, just when it’s getting dark, to write your letters?” my father would expostulate if he chanced to look in. “Let me ring for the lamp, you will strain your eyes.”

My mother would excuse herself, saying she had only a few lines to finish.

“I can think better in this light,” she would explain.

And when Mrs. Teidelmann returned, it was my mother who first called upon her—before even my father knew she was back. From then onward one might have thought them closest friends: my mother visiting often, speaking of her to all in terms of praise and liking.

So peace returned to the house. My father became tender again in word and act; my mother was thoughtful again of his wants and whims. Her voice grew soft and low, the sweet smile hovered about her lips as in the old days before this evil thing had come to dwell among us.

And yet I could not forget it, because every day my mother grew feebler, the little ways that had seemed part of her falling away. She moved more slowly; she sat more often with her hands folded as if listening to some sound at a distance that no one else could hear.

Summer came and went—that town-summer of panting days and stifling nights, when through the open window crawls hot foul air heavy with reeking odours drawn from a thousand streets: sour beer, horse sweat, soot, refuse, frying fat grown old on the fire. Lying awake, one hears the city’s fitful breathing, the restless rumble and whistle and metallic cry, as of some over-laboured beast too tired even to rest. The modern world seemed determined to fill every gap with noise, as if silence were a dangerous old language it meant to stamp out.

Dr. Hal said, “There’s nothing really the matter with her—only weakness. It is the place. Cannot you get her away from it?”

“I cannot leave myself,” said my father, “just yet; but there is no reason why you and the boy should not take a holiday. This year I can afford it, and later I might possibly join you.”

My mother consented as she did to all things now. So it came about that again we climbed—more slowly, with pauses—the steep path to the ruined tower old Jacob in his happy foolishness had built upon the headland. Again we rested upon its topmost platform, sheltered from the wind that forever worried at its crumbling walls. Again we saw the distant mountains, faint like spectres, and the silent ships that came and vanished, and below our feet the pleasant farm lands and the grave, sweet river.

We took lodgings in the village. Smaller it seemed than before, yet wonderful in its sunny calm after London’s clanging turmoil. Mrs. Fursey was there still, but quite another Mrs. Fursey: still angular, but cheery now, bent no longer on suppressing me, rather on drawing me out before admiring neighbours as one might display a cake that had surprisingly risen.

“The material was unpromising, as you know,” she would imply with her manner. “There were times when I almost despaired. But with patience—and, may I say, a natural gift that way—you see what can be accomplished!”

Anna was now a buxom wife and mother, possessed by an uncontrollable desire to fall upon and kiss me at most unexpected moments, so that I lived in constant watchfulness and chose positions that offered easy retreat. Old Chumbley still cobbled shoes in his tiny cave. The bench before him held its row of waiting footwear, and as he tapped and hammered—tap-tap, tap—one could fancy they talked in the language of leather and nails: pert little shoes piping, “Be quick, be quick, we want to be toddling.” Dapper boots sighing, “Oh, please make haste, we are waiting to dance and strut.” Stout sober boots muttering, “As soon as you can, old friend. Remember we’ve work to do.” Flat-footed old boots, rusty and limp, mumbling, “We haven’t much time, Mr. Chumbley. Just a patch, that is all—we haven’t much further to go.”

Old Joe still peddled his pack with the help of the same old jokes; and Tom Pinfold was still scratching his head over a rejected fish hanging by its tail from his expostulating hand. One might almost have believed it the same fish, preserved by stubbornness.

Grown-up folks had changed but little. Only the children had played tricks: people I had left as sucking babes now swaggering in pinafores or knickerbockers; children I had known now mincing it as men and women. Their new airs annoyed me; I preferred the village’s steady sounds—the river’s hush, the wind in the pines, the clean clack of Chumbley’s hammer—to the forced noise of “growing up.”

One afternoon, towards the close of the last week, my mother and I climbed as usual to the upper platform of old Jacob’s tower. She leaned on the parapet, eyes fixed dreamily on the distant mountains. A smile crept to her lips as if some old taste returned to her—something remembered that no one else could name.

“What are you thinking of?” I asked.

“Oh, only of things that happened over there,” she nodded towards the hills as to an old crony with whom she shared secrets, “when I was a girl.”

“You lived there, long ago, didn’t you, when you were young?” I asked, with a boy’s bluntness.

“You’re very rude,” she said; and for the first time in long while a tone of her old self rang out. “It was a very little while ago.”

Suddenly she raised her head and listened. For perhaps twenty seconds she remained so, lips parted, as though catching some far-off call. Then from the woods came a faint long-drawn “Coo-ee.”

We ran to the side of the tower that commanded the pathway from the village and waited until my father emerged from among the dark pines into sunlight.

Seeing us, he shouted again and waved his stick. From the light in his eyes, his gallant bearing, the spring of his step over the heathery turf, we knew instinctively that trouble had come upon him. He always met trouble with that look and air, as if it were a challenger and he a man who could not resist a bout. It was the old Norse blood in him, I suppose: one imagines those godless old pirates springing to their feet when the North wind, loosed like a hawk from leash, struck at the beaked prow.

We heard his quick step on the rickety stair; the next moment he was between us, breathing a little hard and laughing.

For a while he stood beside my mother without speaking, both gazing at the distant hills where, as she had said, things had happened long ago. Perhaps, “over there,” their memories met and looked upon one another with kind eyes.

“Do you remember,” said my father at last, “we climbed up here—it was the first walk we took together after coming here. We discussed our plans for the future, how we would retrieve our fortunes.”

“And the future,” answered my mother, “has a way of making plans for us instead.”

“It would seem so,” replied my father with a laugh. “I am an unlucky beggar, Maggie. I dropped all your money as well as my own down that wretched mine.”

“It was the will—it was Fate, or whatever you call it,” said my mother. “You could not help that, Luke.”

“If only that damned pump hadn’t jambed,” said my father.

“Do you remember that Mrs. Tharand?” asked my mother.

“Yes. What of her?”

“A worldly woman, I always thought her. She called on me the morning we were leaving; I don’t think you saw her. ‘I’ve been through more worries than you would think, to look at me,’ she said to me, laughing. I’ve always remembered her words: ‘and of all the troubles that come to us in this world, believe me, Mrs. Kelver, money troubles are the easiest to bear.’”

“I wish I could think so,” said my father.

“She rather irritated me at the time,” my mother continued. “I thought it one of those commonplaces with which we console ourselves for other people’s misfortunes. But now I know she spoke the truth.”

There was silence between them for a while. The wind moved about the tower and the pines answered it; the air here had room for natural sounds, not the iron-rimmed din of London. It felt as if the very quiet tried to heal.

Then my father said, in a cheery tone that did not quite cover what lay beneath:

“I’ve broken with old Hasluck.”

“I thought you would be compelled to sooner or later,” said my mother.

“Hasluck,” cried my father, with sudden vehemence, “is little better than a thief; I told him so.”

“What did he say?” asked my mother.

“Laughed, and said that was better than some people.”

My father laughed too.

I would do Noel Hasluck no injustice. He was always kind to me; not only then, but in later years, when I learned kindness was rarer than I had dreamt. And for Barbara’s sake, if for no other, I would rather speak well of him. Yet if his good-tempered, thick-skinned ghost—good-tempered and thick-skinned, for otherwise it would not be Noel’s ghost—were reading over my shoulder, I believe it would agree with me and not be offended.

Of all the men who went down into the City each day in cab or ’bus or train, he was perhaps one of the most unprincipled. Whether that is saying much or little I leave to those who know the City’s menu.

To do others—as he believed, right or wrong—as they would do him if ever he gave them half a chance, was his notion of “business,” and in most transactions he was successful.

“I play a game,” he would argue, “where cheating is the rule. Nine out of ten men round the table are sharpers like myself, and the tenth man is a fool who has no business to be there. We prey upon each other, and the cutest of us is the winner.”

“But the innocent people, lured by your fine promises,” I once ventured—“the widows and the orphans?”

“My dear lad,” he said, with a laugh, laying his fat hand on my shoulder, “I remember one of your widows writing me a pathetic letter about some shares she had taken in a Silver Company of mine. Lord knows where the mine is now—somewhere in Spain, I think. It looked as though all her savings were gone. She had an only son, and it was nearly all they possessed in the world, etc., etc.—you know the sort of thing. Well, I did what I’ve often been numskull enough to do in similar cases—wrote and offered to buy her out at par. A week later she answered, thanking me, but saying it did not matter. There had occurred a momentary rise, and she had sold out at a profit—to her own brother-in-law, as I discovered, happening to come across the transfers. You can find widows and orphans round the Monte Carlo card tables, if you like to look for them; they are no more deserving of consideration than the rest of the crowd. Besides, if it comes to that, I’m an orphan myself;” and he laughed again—one of his deep, hearty, honest laughs.

No one ever possessed a laugh more suggestive, in every cadence, of simple transparent honesty. He used to say himself it was worth thousands to him.

The eighteen months that followed—and the end came earlier than any of us had bargained for—were, as I look back, the nearest thing my father and mother ever achieved to a complete and well-seasoned happiness. Not the loud, frothy kind that spills and leaves a sticky mess, but a quiet, slow-simmered content, like a broth that has learnt its own depth by being kept on the hob through the long hours, when the house is still and the street noises thin out to an occasional cart-rattle and the far-off iron cough of the trains.

It was as though Death, that grave caller whom we slam the door on as if he were a rate-collector, had tapped very softly, and then, instead of entering, had stood on the mat with his hat in his hand and said, “Not yet. You have a little time. In a little while.” And because he did not force himself upon us, we grew less afraid of him; and because we grew less afraid, everything else that had frightened us—appearances, neighbours, bills, the silly pantomime of respectability—lost its sting.

We lived poorly again, but now with the decency of people who have stopped pretending that poverty is a moral failure. There was no more pinching and stretching to look like what we were not. My mother stopped trying to make a small joint behave like a haunch; my father ceased apologising for the state of his boots. In the kitchen, even the plainest things were handled with a kind of reverence: bread cut straight, tea made properly, the scant bit of dripping saved without shame and used with intelligence. When Amy browned onions—slowly, patiently, with the pan held just so—there would come up that deep, sweet smell that is a hymn in itself, and it would drift into the parlour and soften whatever hard thought might have been forming there.

In those days my parents laid aside, quite simply, the false and the meaningless. It is astonishing what rubbish a man can cart about in his mind until he realises he may have to leave it all at the station. They thought of little except loving each other well: comforting, explaining, forgiving; learning at last what in younger, busier years they had been too proud, too tired, or too foolish to learn. Their love grew bigger than their needs, and, like soup made with too much goodness, overflowed the pot and ran out towards other people.

Sometimes, remembering those months, it has seemed to me that we are wrong to bolt Death out of the house as if he were infection. He knows life, if anyone does; he might whisper useful things—“Only a little longer. Is it worth snapping? Is it worth being unkind?”—the way the kettle whispers when you are distracted, and if you listen you save the tea from stewing itself into bitterness.

A sort of graciousness went out from them and altered the whole air of the place. Even my Aunt Fan—who had always considered kindness a weakness and civility a form of lying—decided, for the second time in her career, to give amiability a trial. She announced this one afternoon with the abruptness with which she did everything, as if she were flinging a damp cloth at you.

“I’m a beast of an old woman,” said my aunt suddenly.

“Don’t say that, Fan,” urged my mother.

“What’s the good of saying ‘Don’t say it’ when I’ve just said it?” snapped back my aunt.

“It’s your manner,” explained my mother gently; “people sometimes think you disagreeable.”

“They’d be daft if they didn’t,” interrupted my aunt. “Of course you don’t really mean it,” continued my mother, as if reasoning with a kettle for boiling.

“Stuff and nonsense,” snorted my aunt. “Does she think I’m a fool? I like being disagreeable. I like to see ’em squirming.”

My mother laughed; it was one of those laughs that say, There you are again, and I love you for it.

“I can be agreeable,” continued my aunt, “if I choose. Nobody more so.”

“Then why not choose?” suggested my mother.

“I tried it once,” said my aunt, “and it fell flat. Nothing could have fallen flatter.”

“It may not have attracted much attention,” replied my mother, smiling, “but one should not be agreeable merely to attract attention.”

“It wasn’t only that,” returned my aunt. “It gave no satisfaction to anybody. It didn’t suit me. A disagreeable person is at their best when they are disagreeable.”

“I can hardly agree with you there,” said my mother.

“I could do it again,” communed my aunt to herself, with a note of something like vindictiveness, as if amiability were a weapon she meant to try on the enemy. “It’s easy enough. Look at the sort of fools that are agreeable.”

“I’m sure you could be if you tried,” urged my mother.

“Let ’em have it,” continued my aunt, still to herself. “That’s the way to teach ’em sense. Let ’em have it.”

And strange as it sounds, my aunt was right and my mother wrong. For when Aunt Fan turned agreeable, she did it the way she did everything: with a will, with a system, and with an alarming absence of modesty. My father was the first to notice.

“Nothing the matter with poor old Fan, is there?” he asked one evening, a day or two after my aunt had put her threat into practice. “Nothing happened, has there?”

“No,” answered my mother. “Nothing that I know of.”

“Her manner is so strange,” explained my father, frowning slightly. “So—so weird.”

My mother smiled the way one smiles at a child playing at being grown up. “Don’t say anything to her. She’s trying to be agreeable.”

My father laughed, then looked almost wistful. “I almost wish she wouldn’t,” he remarked. “We were used to it, and she was rather amusing.”

But my aunt, being a woman of resolve, persisted—and fortune, which likes a joke, sent her a new audience at precisely the right moment: James Wellington Gadley.

Properly speaking he was Wellington James, that being the order in which he had been christened in 1815, a year that must have made “Wellington” sound like a patriotic drum-beat. But as life went on—and particularly during his school career—it appears to have been borne in upon him that “Wellington” is a heavy joint for a small stomach, and he very sensibly turned it into a side-dish and ate “James” first.

He was a slightly pompous, entirely simple-minded little old gentleman, head clerk to Mr. Stillwood, the solicitor to whom my father was now assistant. Stillwood, Waterhead and Royal dated back to the Georges and was the sort of firm that had once had its fingers in every aristocratic pie, sometimes with the sugar, sometimes with the mud. In these later years its grandeur was thinning like gravy stretched too far; old Mr. Stillwood, the last survivor, declined new partners, explaining frankly that the business was a dying one, and trying to work it up again would be putting new wine into worn-out skins. Yet what clients remained were of good class, and the name still carried that respectable flavour that makes people swallow almost anything.

Mr. Gadley had entered the firm as office boy in its prime and had never noticed the slow cooling of the oven. In his mind, Stillwood, Waterhead and Royal was still the culinary centre of the legal world. Nothing delighted him more than to discuss, over and over, the strange cases in which the firm had been concerned. He had them at his tongue’s tip like sweets in a pocket. If he could find a hearer he would reargue interminably, but with a professional reticence that turned real men into Mr. Y. and Lady X., and real towns into “a certain place not a thousand miles from where we are now sitting,” as if geography itself might sue for libel.

Most people, his methods being somewhat forensic and his breath being somewhat close, discouraged him. My aunt was a never-wearied listener, particularly if the affair smelt—even faintly—of crime. When, during their very first conversation, he exclaimed:

“Now why—why, after keeping away from his wife for nearly eighteen years, never even letting her know whether he was alive or dead, why this sudden resolve to return to her? That is what I want explained to me!”

—and paused for the sympathetic yawn he had learnt to expect, my aunt replied with prompt intelligence:

“To murder her—by slow poison.”

“To murder her! But why?”

“In order to marry the other woman.”

“What other woman?”

“The woman he had just met and fallen in love with. Before that it was immaterial to him what had become of his wife. This woman had said to him: ‘Come back to me a free man or never see my face again.’”

“Dear me! Now that’s very curious.”

“Nothing of the sort,” said my aunt. “Plain common sense.”

“I mean, it’s curious because, as a matter of fact, his wife did die a little later, and he did marry again.”

“Told you so,” remarked my aunt, as if she had merely tasted the stew and knew the rest.

So every case in the Stillwood annals was reviewed, and light thrown upon it by my aunt’s cheerful faith in human depravity. It was fortunate the actors remained Mr. X. and Lady Y., for into the most innocent-seeming behaviour my aunt read the blackest intent, with the relish of a cook who suspects that even the milk has been watered.

“I think you are a little too severe,” Mr. Gadley would now and then plead.

“We’re all of us miserable sinners,” my aunt would affirm brightly; “only we don’t all get the same chances.”

An elderly maiden lady, a Miss Z., residing in “a western town once famous as the resort of fashion, but which we will not name,” my aunt was convinced had burnt down a house containing a will and forged another under which her children—should she ever marry and be blessed with such—would inherit among them at coming of age a fortune of seven hundred pounds.

The freshness of her views always fascinated Mr. Gadley.

“I have to thank you, ma’am,” he would remark on rising, “for a most delightful conversation. I may not be able to agree with your conclusions, but they afford food for reflection.”

To which my aunt would reply, “I hate talking to any one who agrees with me. It’s like taking a walk to see one’s own looking-glass. I’d rather talk to somebody who didn’t, even if he were a fool,” which, for her, was gracious.

He was a stout little gentleman with a stomach that protruded about a foot in front of him, and of this he appeared entirely unaware. It would not have mattered had it not been for his passion, when talking, to approach as close to his listener as possible. Gradually in the course of conversation his stomach, acting as a gentle battering-ram, would drive you backwards round the room. Unless you were artful, you would find yourself pinned hopelessly into a corner, while he looked surprised that, despite all his efforts, he never succeeded in getting any nearer.

His first evening at our house he was talking to my aunt from the corner of his chair. As he grew interested he drew his chair nearer and nearer until, having withdrawn inch by inch to avoid his encroachments, my aunt was sitting on the extreme edge of her own. His next move sent her to the floor.

She said nothing, which astonished me. But on the occasion of his next visit she was busy darning stockings—an unusual occupation for her, and carried on with the air of a conspirator. He approached nearer and nearer as before; but this time she sat her ground, stitching away with grim purpose, and it was he who in due course sprang back with an exclamation decidedly foreign to the subject under discussion.

Ever afterwards my aunt met him with stockings in her hand, and they talked with a safe space between their chairs.

Nothing further came of it, though his being a widower added to their intercourse that small spice of possibility which no woman is ever quite too old to relish; but that he admired her intellectually was evident. Once he even exclaimed:

“Miss Davies, you should have been a solicitor’s wife!”

—which, to his mind, was the crown of female ambition. To which my aunt replied:

“Chances are I should have been, if one had ever asked me.”

Warmed by appreciation, my aunt’s amiability took root and flourished—though, like many late-developing growths, it took on fantastic shapes. She got it into her head, not without reason, that flattery is the shortest road to being agreeable. Conscientiously she set about flattering in season and out, meaning to give pleasure; but the effect was that of sarcasm peeping through a thin napkin.

My father would tell some trifling story, an incident of the day that had amused him. At once my aunt would break into enthusiasm, holding up her hands.

“What a funny man he is! And to think it comes to him naturally without an effort. What a gift it is!”

When my mother appeared in a new bonnet—or an old one retrimmed, which happened often enough, for in these days my mother took more thought than ever for her appearance (you women who have loved will understand)—my aunt would step back in simulated amazement.

“Don’t tell me it’s a married woman with a boy getting on for fourteen. It’s a girl. A saucy, tripping girl. That’s what it is.”

I have heard of vanity growing into a hopeless disease; but I am inclined to think a dose of my aunt at about this period would have cured the most obstinate case. Also, solely for our benefit, she assumed a vivacity and sprightliness that ill suited her; and considering her age and tendency to rheumatism, must have cost her as much as making puff pastry without cold hands. From it I have retained the perhaps immoral belief that Virtue, like a good sauce, is best when it does not call attention to itself.

Now and then the old Adam—or should one say Eve—would assert itself in my aunt, and then, still thoughtful for others, she would descend into the kitchen and be disagreeable to Amy, our new servant, who never minded it.

Amy was a philosopher who reconciled herself to everything by reflecting that there are only twenty-four hours in a day. It sounds a bleak theory, but she extracted from it perpetual cheerfulness, like a cook who can make a feast out of scraps because she knows the scraps will be eaten and the washing-up will end. If my aunt clattered about above, Amy’s mind was on her saucepan; if a cart outside screamed on its ungreased wheels, Amy merely shut the back door and hummed louder. She had an instinct for protecting the house’s small soundscape, the way some people protect a garden from soot.

My mother would apologise to her for my aunt’s interference.

“Lord bless you, mum, it don’t matter. If I wasn’t listening to her something else worse might be happening. Everything’s all the same when it’s over.”

Amy had come to us as a stop-gap, explaining she was about to be married and wanted only a temporary engagement to bridge the few weeks between then and the ceremony.

“It’s rather unsatisfactory,” my mother had said. “I dislike changes.”

“I can quite understand it, mum,” Amy had replied. “I dislike ’em myself. Only I heard you was in a hurry, and I thought maybe that while you was on the lookout for somebody permanent—”

So on that understanding she came. A month later my mother asked when she thought the marriage would actually take place.

“Don’t think I’m wishing you to go,” my mother explained; “indeed I’d like you to stop. I only want to know in time to make my arrangements.”

“Oh, some time in the spring, I expect,” was Amy’s answer.

“Oh!” said my mother. “I understood it was coming off almost immediately.”

Amy appeared shocked, as if my mother had suggested eating fish with a knife.

“I must know a little bit more about him before I go as far as that,” she said.

“But I don’t understand,” my mother persisted. “You told me when you came to me you were going to be married in a few weeks.”

“Oh, that one!” Amy’s tone suggested an unfair strain on her memory. “I didn’t feel I wanted him as much as I thought I did when it come to the point.”

“You had meantime met the other one?” suggested my mother, smiling.

“Well, we can’t help our feelings, can we, mum?” admitted Amy frankly, speaking like one with experience even then. “And what I always say is—better change your mind before it’s too late afterwards.”

Amiable, sweet-faced, broad-hearted Amy—most faithful of friends, but oh, most faithless of lovers. Age has not withered nor custom staled her liking for infinite variety. Butchers, bakers, soldiers, sailors, Jacks of all trades—does the sighing procession never pass before you, Amy, pointing reproachful fingers? Still Amy is engaged. To whom at the particular moment I cannot say; but I fancy to an early one who has lately become a widower. After more exact knowledge I do not care to enquire; for to confess ignorance—implying you have treated as trivial and forgotten the most important detail of a matter vital to her—is to hurt her feelings; while to angle for information is merely to entangle oneself. To speak of Him as “Tom” when Tom has belonged for weeks to the dead and buried past, to hastily correct oneself to “Dick” when there hasn’t been a Dick for years, clearly not to know he is now Harry, annoys her even more. In my mother’s time we always referred to him as “Dearest.” It was the title Amy herself gave them all, and it avoided confusion.

“Well, and how’s Dearest?” my mother would enquire, opening the door to Amy on a Sunday evening.

“Oh, very well indeed, mum, thank you, and he sends you his respects,” or else, “Well, not so nicely as I could wish. I’m a little anxious about him, poor dear!”

“When you are married you will be able to take good care of him.”

“That’s really what he wants—some one to take care of him. It’s what they all want, the poor dears.”

“And when is it coming off?”

“In the spring, mum.” She always chose the spring when possible.

Amy was nice to all men, and to Amy all men were nice. She might have settled down with a dozen and only had occasional regrets concerning those left out in the cold. But to ask her to select only one from so many “poor dears” was to suggest a shameful waste of affection.

We had meant to keep our grim secret to ourselves; but hiding trouble from Amy was like keeping cold hands away from the fire. Very soon she knew everything there was to know, drawing it out of my mother as one draws a splinter from a child—firmly, kindly, and with no nonsense. Then she put my mother down into a chair and stood over her like a sergeant in an apron.

“Now you leave the house and everything connected with it to me, mum,” commanded Amy. “You’ve got something else to do.”

And from that day we were in Amy’s hands and had nothing else to do but praise the Lord for His goodness. Meals appeared at the right time, clean and simple, with the quiet competence that keeps a household’s heart beating. The clink of plates, the soft chopping on the board, the kettle’s steady song—these were the domestic rites by which Amy held chaos outside the door.

Barbara also found out (from Washburn, I expect), though she said nothing. She simply came often. Old Hasluck would have come himself, I am sure, had he thought he would be welcome. As it was, he always sent kind messages and presents of fruit and flowers by Barbara, and always welcomed me most heartily whenever she allowed me to see her home.

Barbara brought, as ever, sunshine with her, making trouble seem far off and shadowy. My mother tended the fire of love, but Barbara lit the cheerful lamp of laughter. Even the dreariest street sounded different when she was near; the harsh cries and iron rumblings seemed to stand back a little, as if embarrassed by her clear voice.

And with the lessening days my father seemed to grow younger, life lying lighter on him.

One summer night he and I were walking with Barbara to Poplar station, for sometimes, when he did not look tired, she would order him to fetch his hat and stick, explaining with a caress:

“I like them tall and slight and full grown. The young ones—they don’t know how to flirt! We will take the boy with us as gooseberry.”

And he, pretending to be anxious my mother did not see, would kiss Barbara’s hand and slip out quietly with her arm linked under his. It was admirable how he entered into the spirit of the thing.

That evening the last cloud faded from before the moon as we turned the corner, and even the East India Dock Road lay restful, subdued to a murmur. Somewhere a piano was being murdered behind a closed window; somewhere else a barrowman sang one line over and over like a charm against the night. The city’s great metallic throat had, for an hour, chosen to speak softly.

“I have always regarded myself,” said my father, “as a failure in life, and it has troubled me.”

I felt him pulled the slightest little bit away from me, as though Barbara, holding his other arm, had drawn him towards her with a swift pressure.

“But do you know the idea that has come to me within the last few months? That on the whole I have been successful. I am like a man,” continued my father, “who in some deep wood has been frightened, thinking he has lost his way, and suddenly coming to the end of it finds that by some lucky chance he has been guided to the right point after all. I cannot tell you what a comfort it is to me.”

“What is the right point?” asked Barbara.

“Ah, that I cannot tell you,” answered my father, with a laugh. “I only know that for me it is here where I am. All the time I thought I was wandering away from it I was drawing nearer to it. It is very wonderful. I am just where I ought to be. If I had only known I never need have worried.”

Whether it would have troubled either him or my mother very much had it been otherwise, I cannot say; for Life, so small a thing when looked at beside Death, had lost its terror for them. But I like to remember that Fortune at the last was kind to my father—prospering his adventures not to the extent his sanguine nature had dreamed, but sufficiently; so that no fear for our future marred the peaceful passing of his tender spirit.

Or should I award thanks, not to Fate, but rather to sweet Barbara? And behind her, do I not detect shameless old Hasluck, grinning good-naturedly in the background?

“Now, Uncle Luke, I want your advice. Dad’s given me this cheque as a birthday present. I don’t want to spend it. How shall I invest it?”

“My dear, why not consult your father?”

“Now, Uncle Luke, dad’s a dear, especially after dinner, but you and I know him. Giving me a present is one thing, doing business for me is another. He’d unload on me. He’d never be able to resist the temptation.”

My father would suggest, and Barbara would thank him. But a minute later she would murmur, with an air of one criticising a recipe:

“You don’t know anything about Argentinos.”

My father did not; but Barbara did—to quite a remarkable extent for a young girl.

“That child has insisted on leaving this cheque with me and I have advised her to buy Argentinos,” my father would observe after she was gone. “I am going to put a few hundreds into them myself. I hope they will turn out all right, if only for her sake. I have a presentiment somehow that they will.”

A month later Barbara would greet him with:

“Isn’t it lucky we bought Argentinos!”

“Yes; they haven’t turned out badly, have they? I had a feeling, you know, for Argentinos.”

“You’re a genius, Uncle Luke. And now we will sell out and buy Calcuttas, won’t we?”

“Sell out? But why?”

“You said so. You said, ‘We will sell out in about a month and be quite safe.’”

“My dear, I’ve no recollection of it.”

But Barbara had, and before she had done with him, so had he. And the next day Argentinos would be sold—not any too soon—and Calcuttas bought.

Could money so gained bring a blessing with it? The question would plague my father.

“It’s very much like gambling,” he would mutter uneasily to himself at each success. “Uncommonly like gambling.”

“It is for your mother,” he would impress upon me. “When she is gone, Paul, put it aside. Keep it for doing good; that may make it clean. Start your own life without any help from it.”

He need not have troubled. It went the road that all luck derived, however indirectly, from old Hasluck ever went. Yet it served good purpose on its way, like a rich stock that does not keep but nourishes while it lasts.

But the most marvellous feat ever accomplished by Barbara, to my thinking, was the carrying off of my father and mother to witness “A Voice from the Grave, or the Power of Love, New and Original Drama in five acts and thirteen tableaux.”

They had been bred in a narrow creed, both my father and my mother. That Puritan blood flowed in their veins which throughout our land has drowned much harmless joyousness; yet those who know of it only from hearsay do foolishly to speak but ill of it. If ever earnest times should come again—not how to enjoy but how to live being the question; Fate demanding of us not what we have but what we are—we may regret that they are fewer among us than formerly, those who trained themselves to despise all pleasure because in pleasure they saw the subtlest foe to principle and duty. No graceful growth, this Puritanism; its roots are in hard, stern facts. But it is strong. Its men feared and its women loved God, and if their words were harsh their hearts were tender. If they shut out sunshine, it was that their eyes might see better the glory beyond. And if their view be correct—that earth’s threescore years and ten are but preparation for eternity—who shall call them even foolish for turning away their thoughts from its allurements?

“Still, I think I should like to have a look at one, just to see what it is like,” argued my father. “One cannot judge of a thing that one knows nothing about.”

I imagine it was his first argument rather than his second that convinced my mother.

“That is true,” she answered. “I remember how shocked my poor father was when he found me one night at the bedroom window reading Sir Walter Scott by the light of the moon.”

“What about the boy?” said my father, for I had been included in the invitation.

“We will all be wicked together,” said my mother.

So an evening or two later the four of us stood at the corner of Pigott Street waiting for the ’bus, while the neighbourhood made its usual music: a distant factory throb, a whistle like an insult, the nearer slap of boots and the occasional shriek of a wheel that had never met oil in its life. The air was close, thick with summer and London and the day’s cooking escaping from other people’s windows—fried fish somewhere, cabbage somewhere, and, in a brief blessing, the warm yeasty breath of a baker’s shop shutting up.

“It is a close evening,” said my father; “let’s go the whole hog and ride outside.”

Loves of my youth—where have you gone to hide yourselves? Tubby, with hair like buttered corn and the courage to suggest we could reach the North Pole by taking the No. 9 omnibus to Baker Street; Langley, whose nose bore the permanent impression of somebody else’s knuckles; stout Shamus, all heart and no legs, who could be “downed” with a sigh and yet could not be persuaded, by any science known to man, to say he was beaten; Neal the thin; Dicky—“dicky Dick”—the fat; Ballett of the tearful eye; Beau Bunnie, lord of many neckties, who fought solemnly in black kid gloves as though a bout were a tea-party that had turned regrettable; and the rest of you, unnamed now but once as necessary to me as bread and jam—where do your creeping ghosts keep school?

Not long ago—unless time is lying to me again—I’m almost certain I saw you, Tubby. It was you, in spite of the side-whiskers and the greyness and the thinning of the once magnificent thatch. You were hurrying down Throgmorton Street, chained to a small black bag as if it were a can of soup you feared might escape. I should have stopped you; but I had a train at Liverpool Street to catch and a shave to procure on the way—two errands that, taken together, form the modern man’s idea of romance. I wonder if you recognised me. You looked hard, as one looks at a menu one suspects has been altered since last Tuesday.

And you, gallant Shamus, who once fought for half an hour to save a frog from being skinned—someone told me you are now an Income Tax assessor. I can picture you, grimly heroic, rescuing no frogs, believing nothing, taxing everything; a man with a power of disbelief unusual even among those whose business is to assume that all other men are liars. May Providence, if it has a sense of humour, assign you to districts far from mine.

So Time, Nature’s tireless handyman, bustles about the rooms of the world, sweeping away our volcanoes, smoothing down our ruins, and putting a nice respectable covering of earth over every embarrassment. Even a lover’s initials carved on beech bark get healed, as though the tree itself were ashamed to remember.

In the beginning I was not a favourite with my schoolmates, and it was then trouble first took lodgings with me and paid no rent. A grown person can wrap himself in a dozen warm delusions—one of which is that people rather like him, or would if they knew him, or are on the point of liking him as soon as their digestion improves. A child has no such overcoat. He feels the draught.

My ostracism was painfully plain, and—being made in the silent brooding pattern—I went about with it inside me as with a hot potato, afraid to drop it and unable to swallow it.

“Can you run?” asked me one day an important personage whose name I have lost, though I remember his authority with a clearness that makes up for it. He was head of the Lower Fourth: tall, beak-nosed, and possessing the air of a boy who had been born to sign documents. His father, I later learned, kept a draper’s shop in the Edgware Road, and when the shop failed the future conqueror of Europe was packed off into a clerkship in the Civil Service. But to us he was a young Duke of Wellington in short trousers, and under other circumstances might, perhaps, have become something equally disastrous.

“Yes,” I answered. In truth it was my one accomplishment. Rumour of it—school rumours travel like the smell of suet pudding—must have reached him.

“Run round the playground twice at your fastest,” he commanded. “Let me see you.”

I clenched my fists and charged off. If gratitude could be measured in perspiration, I was a model son. How thankful I was that he, the crowned head of the Lower Fourth, should speak to me publicly—the outcast!—I could only show by running as though pursued by the Devil and the French.

When I drew up before him I was panting hard, but I could see he approved.

“Why don’t the fellows like you?” he asked bluntly.

If only I could have stepped out of my shyness and spoken the hunger that was eating me! If only I could have cried: O Lord of the Lower Fourth, you upon whom success—success, that steaming, irresistible thing, like a pie just taken from the oven—has fallen without your so much as ordering it! Tell me the secret recipe. Is it Tommy’s swagger? Then I will swagger too, though my heart shakes like jelly. But why, when Tommy struts, do they admire it—and when I do, do they crow like roosters and sidle after me like pouter pigeons? Is it Dicky’s playfulness—Dicky who steals caps, runs away with balls, springs on backs, and is rewarded with laughter—while I, doing no more and being if anything lighter, am repaid with a bloody nose and a widening ring of dislike?

Should I fling my arms about their necks as Billy does? They wrench themselves from me as from a wet apron. Should I be indifferent—walk apart, practising carelessness? No friendly arm hooks itself through mine. Should I win them by kind offices? Oh, if I could. I would do their sums (I was good at sums), write their impositions, take their punishments, scrub their boots with devotion—if only they would pay me in the small coin of love and the larger coin of admiration.

But all I could find to say—sulkily, like a boy pretending not to bleed—was: “They do like me, some of them.”

“Don’t tell lies,” he answered. “You know they don’t—none of them.” And I hung my head.

“I’ll tell you what I’ll do,” he continued, in the grand manner. “I’ll give you a chance. We’re starting hare and hounds next Saturday; you can be a hare. You needn’t tell anybody. Just turn up and I’ll see to it. Mind, you’ll have to run like the devil.”

He walked away without waiting for my answer, leaving me to meet Joy running towards me with outstretched hands—Joy, who always arrives breathless, without asking whether there’s room.

I obeyed orders and gave no hint in school of the glory preparing for me. But the moment I reached home I burst in like a kettle boiling over.

“I’m to be a hare because I run so fast! Anybody can be a hound, but there’s only two hares, and they all want me. And can I have a jersey? We begin next Saturday. He saw me run. I ran twice round the playground. He said I was splendid! Of course it’s a great honour to be a hare. We start from Hampstead Heath. And may I have a pair of shoes?”

The jersey and the shoes my mother and I bought that very day, because the fear was upon me that unless we hurried the last blue-and-white striped jersey in London would be snapped up by some other aspiring hero and I should be forced to run in something plain and therefore doomed.

That evening, before bed, I dressed myself in full costume and admired myself in the glass, as a cook admires a cake before it collapses. From then till the end of the week I practised leaping over chairs; my way of descending stairs became perilous, circuitous, and—if I may say so—innovative. My mother watched me with the look with which one watches a saucepan one suspects is about to boil over.

“The credit of the Lower Fourth is at stake,” I explained, with the solemnity of a statesman. “Banisters and legs are of small account compared with fame and honour.”

My father nodded and supported me with manly argument; my mother supported herself by adding another line to her prayers.

Saturday came. We were to meet at half-past two by the turnpike gate outside the Spaniards. I took my lunch and ate it in Regent’s Park—what it was I no longer remember, but I recall the damp taste of anticipation, which is always the chief ingredient in a boy’s meal. Then I took the ’bus to the Heath.

One by one the others came up. Beyond glances none took any notice of me. I wore my ordinary clothes over my jersey. I knew they thought I’d come merely to watch them start, and I hugged to myself the surprise I meant to serve up—hot, with myself as the dish.

He came at last, our leader—one of the last, because commanders arrive when it suits them. I sidled behind him and waited while he organised and constructed, as though building a railway.

“But we’ve only got one hare,” cried one of them. “We ought to have two, you know, in case one gets blown.”

“We’ve got two,” answered the Duke. “Think I don’t know what I’m about? Young Kelver’s going to be the other one.”

Silence fell—thick, sudden silence, like flour thrown over a mess.

“Oh, I say, we don’t want him,” broke in a voice at last. “He’s a muff.”

“He can run,” explained the Duke.

“Let him run home,” said another voice; and laughter came out, sharp and eager, like the snap of a biscuit.

“You’ll run home in a minute yourself,” threatened the Duke, “if I have any of your cheek. Who’s captain here—you or me? Now, young ’un, are you ready?”

I had begun unbuttoning my jacket. My hands stopped and fell to my side as if they’d been told to. The heat in my chest turned instantly to cold.

“I don’t want to come,” I said, “if they don’t want me.”

“He’ll get his feet wet,” suggested the first boy. “Don’t spoil him—he’s his mother’s pet.”

“Are you coming or are you not?” shouted the Duke. He could not endure hesitation; authoritative people prefer even disaster, provided it is prompt.

But the tears were rising and would not go back. I turned my face away, without speaking.

“All right, stop then,” cried the Duke. “Here, Keefe, you take the bag and be off. It’ll be dark before we start.”

My substitute snatched at the chance as one snatches at a new bun. Away went the hares, while I, keeping my face hid, moved slowly off.

“Cry-baby!” shouted a sharp-eyed youngster.

“Let him alone,” growled the Duke; and I went on until I found the cedars.

A few minutes later I heard them start, whooping. Their sound went across the Heath and seemed to press me down into the wet earth. How could I go home and confess—confess the shame that I had been offered a place and refused it because it came with contempt? My father would be waiting with questions; my mother with hot water and blankets, as though I’d been out in a storm of triumph. What explanation could I give that did not betray the miserable secret—that I was unwanted?

It was a chill, dismal afternoon. The Heath lay empty; a thin rain began, the sort of rain that insinuates itself into everything as gossip does. I slipped off my shirt and jacket and rolled them under my arm. Then I trotted off alone—hare and hounds combined into one small carcass—to chase myself sadly by myself.

I see it still: that pathetically ridiculous little figure, jogging doggedly over the dank fields. Mile after mile it runs, the little idiot—jumping, sometimes falling into muddy ditches as though anxious to season itself; scrambling through dripping hedges; swarming over tarry fence and slimy paling. On it pants—through Bishop’s Wood, by tangled Churchyard Bottom, where now the railway shrieks; down sloppy lanes bordering Muswell Hill, where now stand rows of prim villas, looking as though they have never smelt anything more pungent than respectability.

At intervals it stops to dab its eyes with a dingy rag of a handkerchief, to rearrange the bundle under its arm; its chief anxiety to keep out of sight of chance wanderers, to dodge farmhouses, to dart across high roads when nobody is looking. And so, tear-smeared and mud-bespattered, up the long rise of darkening Crouch End Lane—where to-night the electric light will blaze from a hundred shops—and dead beat into the Seven Sisters Road station, there to tear off its soaked jersey; and then home to Poplar with a shameless account of the jolly afternoon it has spent, of the admiration and praise it has won.

You poor little brat. Popularity? It is a shadow. Turn your eyes toward it and it will run before you. Turn your back and walk toward the living sun and it will follow—so they say, and I, who have tried both, can only tell you the proverb tastes better than the fact. Why, then, do you look at me with that quizzical grin, as if you already know you will spend years proving the proverb wrong?

When one takes service with Deceit, one signs a contract one may not break without penalty. Perhaps those lonely runs were good for my lungs. But oh, they were dreary. I persuaded myself—by a sort of logic that grows easily in the mind of a boy—that truth was merely a matter of words; that so long as I had actually gone over the ground I described, I was not lying. To further satisfy my conscience, I bought a big satchel and scattered torn-up paper as I ran, making myself, in a reduced and melancholy way, my own hare.

“And they never catch you?” my mother would ask.

“Oh, no, never,” I answered. “They never even get within sight of me.”

“Be careful, dear,” she would advise. “Don’t overstrain yourself.” But I could see she was proud—proud as a woman is when she thinks her child has been invited into the world.

After a while imagination came to my help. Often I could hear behind me the sound of pursuing feet, catch through gaps in the trees a sight of a merry host upon my trail, and I would redouble my speed. The rain would patter on leaves with that soft, busy whisper that makes even loneliness feel occupied, while far off some cart would rattle along a road like an argument that will not end. In those days the earth still had room for quiet; now the shriek of rails and the clatter of building have moved in like vulgar neighbours who keep a piano.

But for Dan my loneliness would have been unbearable. His friendship was there for me to creep to like shade on a scorching day. To this day you can always know Dan’s politics: they are those of the party out of power. You can always know the cause he will champion: the unpopular one. The man he will defend: the man who is down.

“You are such an un-understandable chap,” complained a fellow-clubman to him once in my hearing. “I sometimes ask myself if you have any opinions at all.”

“I hate a crowd,” was Dan’s only confession of faith.

He never claimed anything from me in return for his affection. He was there—solid, quiet, not asking me to perform. When I returned to him for comfort, baffled in attempts to win others, he gave it without even troubling himself to add advice. When childish success came to me at last and I needed him less, he was neither hurt nor surprised. Other people—what they thought, what they did—even when it concerned himself—never seemed to trouble him. He loved to bestow; response bored him. He was like a fountain: content to give, unable to receive.

My popularity came to me unexpectedly, after I had given up hoping—surprising me and, in a way, annoying me. Gradually it dawned that my company was being sought.

“Come along, Kelver,” the spokesman of one group would say; “we’re going part of your way home. You can walk with us.”

Perhaps I would go with them; more often, before we reached the gate, the delight of my society would be claimed by a rival troop.

“He’s coming with us this afternoon. He promised.”

“No, he didn’t.”

“Yes, he did.”

“Well, he ain’t, anyhow. See?”

“Oh, isn’t he? Who says he isn’t?”

“I do.”

“Punch his head, Dick!”

“Yes, you do, Jimmy Blake, and I’ll punch yours. Come, Kelver.”

I might have been some Queen of Beauty offered as a prize for knightly combat. Indeed, more than once the argument ended in the simplest manner: a fight, and I was carried off, half laughing and half ashamed, by the victorious party.

For a while it was a mystery to me, until I asked Norval—George Grampian—whom we called “Norval” because we were witty. Norval, who had taken particular delight in teasing me, had abruptly become one of my greatest admirers. He was in the second eleven and, after Dan, the best fighter in the lower school. If I could understand Norval, all would be plain.

One afternoon he bounded on me in the cloakroom and slipped his arm into mine, clamouring for my company to Camden Town. I stopped and put it bluntly.

“Why should I walk home with you? Why do you want me?”

“Because we like you.”

“But why do you like me?”

“Why!” He stared, as though I’d asked why jam sticks to fingers. “Because you’re such a funny chap. You say such funny things.”

It struck me like a slap. I had meant to win popularity on a ladder of heroic qualities. In the school books I read, Leonard or Marmaduke won love and admiration by integrity, nobility, goodness, intellect—perhaps with a little bowling or jumping thrown in by the way. Not one of them had ever said anything funny, either by accident or design. And if a Marmaduke existed among us in the Lower Fifth, we called him Marmalade; the school books never mention that.

“Don’t be disagreeable, Kelver,” Norval complained. “Come with us and we’ll let you into the team as an extra. I’ll teach you batting.”

So I was to be their Fool. I, dreamer of knightly dreams, aspirant to laurels, had won not wonder but laughter. I had prayed for popularity and it had been granted—in this form. Had the gods, who once played tricks on Midas, grown no kinder?

Had my vanity been less I might have flung their gift back at them. But my thirst for approbation was too keen. I had to choose: cut capers and be followed, or walk in dignity and be ignored. I chose the capers.

As time wore on I strove to cut them quicker, quainter. I thought out funny stories, prepared ingenuous impromptus, twisted every idea into odd expression. And I had my reward. Before long my company was desired by all the school.

But I was never content. I would rather have been captain of their football club—aye, even deputy vice—than the king of their laughter. I would have given all my meed of chuckles for stuttering Jerry’s one round of applause when, in our match against Highbury, he knocked up his century and won us the victory by three.

Till the end I never quite abandoned hope of exchanging my vine leaves for laurels. I rose an hour earlier to practise throwing at broomsticks set up in waste places. Later, when stilts came temporarily into fashion, I wearied body and mind for weeks trying to acquire skill upon them. That fat Tubby could out-distance me saddened my life for months.

There was a Sixth Form boy—Wakeham, if I remember rightly—who envied me my gift of amusing. He was at the age when the other sex becomes important and the desire had come to him to be regarded as a star of wit among the social circles of Gospel Oak. Need I say that by nature he was ponderously dull?

One afternoon I happened to be the centre of a small group in the playground. I had been holding forth and they had been laughing. Whether I had said anything truly entertaining I cannot tell. It made little difference; they had got into the habit of laughing when I talked. Sometimes I would say something serious on purpose; they laughed just the same, as though seriousness were my best joke.

Wakeham was among them, watching me with the fixed stare boys reserve for conjurers when they’re trying to find the hidden rabbit. Later he slipped his arm through mine and drew me into an empty corner.

“I say, Kelver,” he broke out the moment we were beyond hearing, “you really are funny!”

It gave me no pleasure. If he’d told me he admired my bowling I might not have believed him, but I should have loved him for it.

“So are you,” I answered savagely. “Only you don’t know it.”

“No, I’m not,” he said. “Wish I was. I say, Kelver”—he glanced round to make sure no one was near—“do you think you could teach me to be funny?”

I was about to reply with conviction that it could not be done, when an idea occurred.

Wakeham was famous for one thing. He could insert two fingers in his mouth and produce a whistle capable of confusing dogs a quarter of a mile off and causing people nearby to jump from six to eighteen inches into the air—an accomplishment like a bad smell: powerful, immediate, and seldom welcomed.

I envied him it as keenly as he envied me my supposed wit. I could not see the use of it; it generally called forth irritation rather than affection. Once he performed close to a purple-faced old gentleman’s ear; the old gentleman promptly cuffed his head, and for so doing was commended by the whole street as a public benefactor. Drivers flicked at him with whips. Even youth, from whom sympathy might have been expected, was moved to throw whatever came handy. My own social circle regarded the whistle as vulgar; Wakeham dared not perform it within hearing of his own classmates. That any human being should desire to acquire it seems incomprehensible.

And yet for weeks in secret I had wrestled to produce it. Why? Because it drew attention; because someone else could do it and I could not; and because I had no natural aptitude for it—and therefore my soul wanted it all the more. Had a boy appeared who could walk on his hands with his heels in the air, I should probably have broken my neck trying to copy and excel. I make no apologies for the brat; I present him merely as a study for the amusement of a world of wiser boys—and men.

I struck a bargain with Wakeham. I would teach him to be funny; he would teach me the costermonger’s whistle.

Each of us strove conscientiously to impart knowledge. Neither succeeded.

Wakeham tried hard to be funny. I tried hard to whistle. He did all I told him; I followed his instructions implicitly. The result was the feeblest of wit and the feeblest of whistles.

“Do you think anybody would laugh at that?” Wakeham would enquire pathetically at the end of his supremest effort.

And honestly I had to confess: “I don’t think any living being would.”

“How far off do you think anyone could hear that?” I would demand anxiously, on recovering breath enough to speak at all.

“Well,” Wakeham would reply kindly, not wishing to discourage me, “it would depend on whether you knew it was coming.”

We abandoned the scheme by mutual consent at about the end of a fortnight.

“I suppose it’s something you’ve got to have inside you,” I suggested by way of consolation.

“I don’t think the roof of your mouth can be quite the right shape for it,” concluded Wakeham.

And thus ended the only formal education I ever received in the arts of humour and noise.

My father chose, with a kind of dry punctuality that would have amused him had he been in a condition to be amused, to die on the morning of his birthday. We had been expecting him to go on—quietly ebbing, as he had been for weeks—so that when the end came it arrived like a stranger who has been in the room all the while, only one has not happened to turn one’s head.

“I have left him sleeping,” my mother whispered, gliding back into the little parlour in her dressing-gown as softly as if the boards themselves were sick and must not be jarred. “Washburn gave him a draught last night. We won’t disturb him.”

So we breakfasted in low voices. The house was one of those small, thin-walled places that hold sound as badly as they hold heat; a spoon against a cup seems to travel through lath and plaster with the insistence of a drum. The kettle hissed, the tea smelt of tannin and comfort, toast browned unevenly over the fire—its scorched edge giving off that sharp, homely perfume that, to this day, can make me think of narrow rooms and brave economies. We spoke as if words had weight.

Afterwards my mother crept upstairs; I followed, feeling as though my boots ought to have been taken off at the foot of the stairs like muddy shoes at a church door. She opened the bedroom door by a fraction and stood listening—her ear turned, not toward the keyhole, but toward the silence itself, as if silence were a jar and she might detect by its ring whether it had cracked.

The blinds were down; the room lay in a dusk that smelt faintly of medicine and the cooled sweetness of last night’s gruel. Outside, life had begun its daily street-chant with the sturdy indifference of London. The first costermonger went past—by the sound of it a girl, shrill and bright as a tin whistle—crying, “Watercreases, fine fresh watercreases with your breakfast-a’penny a bundle watercreases,” and farther off a hoarse boy wailed “Mee-ilk-mee-ilk-oi,” stretching the syllables as though he were pulling them on a rope.

Inch by inch my mother opened the door wider and we went in. He lay on his back, the lips slightly parted, as if he had been about to answer someone and had forgotten the rest of the sentence. His eyes were closed; his face, freed at last from effort, looked oddly young—too young for the tired lines I had grown used to. I had never seen death before. I could not make the thing fit the sight. All I could take in, at first, was that he was still there, and yet not there; like a clock that keeps its place on the shelf while its ticking has vanished.

It came to me only by slow degrees, as cold comes into a room when the fire is out and one does not notice until one’s hands begin to ache: he had gone away from us. For days—weeks—I would fancy I heard his step behind me in the street, his voice calling, see his face in a crowd and turn with a quick, foolish happiness, only to stand bewildered because it had melted into other faces. But at that first moment, curiously, I felt no pain at all. Pain, when it arrived, took its own time, and did not trouble to be punctual.

To my mother it was not an ending, merely a brief separation—an absence like a man gone on a journey whose boots still stand by the door. Fear and doubt had never been allowed to lodge in her faith; she seemed to carry it as naturally as she carried her keys. He was waiting for her. In God’s good time they would meet again. What need, then, of wailing? Without him the days would be slower, the house a little dull when the good man was away; but that was the worst of it. She spoke of him always—of his dear ways, of his little follies and oddities that had made us laugh—not with tears, but with a smile that was almost domestic in its calm, as though she were keeping his memory warm like bread in a cloth.

We stayed on in the old house though always, in talk, on the verge of moving. Year by year the great brick monster had crept closer, chewing up whatever was green or kindly. Field and garden, tree and cottage; the time-mellowed house that suggested story; the hedgerow that hid ugliness; the last scraps that reminded one there had once been sunshine in the neighbourhood—one by one the monster scrunched them between his ugly teeth. This east end of London was a world apart, a ghetto of the poor forever spreading, dreariness laid upon dreariness like soot on soot; endless rows of reeking cells where London herded her slaves.

Often on a misty afternoon, when we knew that beyond this city of the dead there must be a countryside where life was stirring in sunlight, we would go “house-hunting” in pleasant suburbs—some of them already being swallowed in their turn. We talked ourselves into hope at Highgate, then still a tiny town reached by coach from leafy Holloway; at Hampstead, with its ancient red-brick houses and wind-blown heath where you could look past woods and farms and see, far off, London’s domes and spires like grey bubbles on the horizon; at Wood Green, among pastures where smock-coated labourers discussed their politics and ale under wide-spreading elms; at Hornsey, then a village made of an ivy-covered church and one grass-bordered way. Again and again we found “the very thing for us,” praised its light, imagined curtains and furniture, judged its stairs by the needs of future years—and still we delayed.

“We must think it over,” my mother would say. “There is no hurry; for some reasons I shall be sorry to leave Poplar.”

“For what reasons, mother?”

“Oh—no particular reason, Paul. Only we have lived there so long, you know. It will be a wrench leaving the old house.”

Even to the making of a man goes the instinct of the clinging vine. We throw out our tendrils and fasten on whatever wall is nearest—castle-keep or pig-sty—and then rain and sunshine tie us tighter. Dying Scott hurrying home from Italy, anxious to breathe once more the damp mists of barren hills; an old woman I knew carried to the sea for her lungs, pining instead for her “home”—a slum attic—until the alley’s reek smelt to her like belonging, its riot like the voices of her own. Who shall understand such things save He who fashioned us?
