So the old house held us to its dismal bosom; and I did not leave it until, within its homely but unlovely arms, first my aunt and later my mother had died, and I had said good-bye to Amy, crying amid littered emptiness.

My aunt died as she had lived: grumbling—making a last practical protest against the injustice of existence, as if death were another bill improperly made out.

“You will be glad to get rid of me, all of you!” she said, dropping, for the first and last time I can recall, into the retort direct. “And I can’t say I shall be very sorry to go myself. It hasn’t been my idea of life.”

That was a couple of weeks before the end. I don’t suppose she knew how near it was—perhaps if she had, she might have contrived a little sentiment; though it is hard to imagine my aunt with sentiment without some grumble attached, like a pin.

“Don’t be foolish,” said my mother. “You’re not going to die!”

“What’s the use of talking like an idiot,” snapped my aunt. “I’ve got to do it some time. Why not now, when everything’s all ready for it. It isn’t as if I was enjoying myself.”

“I am sure we do all we can for you,” said my mother.

“I know you do,” my aunt answered. “I’m a burden to you. I always have been.”

“Not a burden,” corrected my mother, as gently as one corrects a child’s bad grammar.

“What does the woman call it then,” my aunt retorted. “Does she reckon I’ve been a sunbeam in the house? I’ve been a trial to everybody. That’s what I was born for; it’s my métier.”

My mother put her arms about the poor old soul and kissed her.

“We should miss you very much,” she said.

“I’m sure I hope they all will!” my aunt replied. “It’s the only thing I’ve got to leave ’em worth having.”

My mother laughed; the laugh did not mock, it warmed.

“Maybe it’s been a good thing for you, Maggie,” grumbled my aunt. “If it wasn’t for cantankerous, disagreeable people like me, gentle, patient people like you wouldn’t get any practice. Perhaps, after all, I’ve been a blessing to you in disguise.”

I cannot honestly say we ever wished her back; but we did miss her—missed the odd angles of her, the corner ways that gave one something to bump against and therefore something to laugh at. It takes all sorts to make a world; and I dare say if only the perfect folk were left, existence would become unbearably smooth.

Amy, I believe, genuinely regretted her.

“One never knows what’s good for one till one’s lost it,” sighed Amy.

“I’m glad to think you liked her,” said my mother.

“You see, mum,” Amy explained, “I was one of a large family; and a bit of a row now and again cheers one up, I always think. I’ll be losing the power of my tongue if something doesn’t come along soon.”

“Well, you are going to be married in a few weeks now,” my mother reminded her.

But Amy remained despondent. “They’re poor things, the men, at a few words—the best of them,” she replied. “As likely as not just when you’re getting interested you turn round to find that they’ve put on their hat and gone out.”

After my aunt’s death my mother and I were very much alone. Barbara had gone abroad “to finish her education”—to learn, as old Hasluck phrased it, “the tricks of the Nobs’ trade.” I had left school and entered Mr. Stillwood’s office without salary—the arrangement being that I should study for the law.

“You are in luck’s way, my boy, in luck’s way,” old Mr. Gadley assured me. “To have commenced your career in the office of Stillwood, Waterhead and Royal will be a passport anywhere. It will stamp you, my boy.”

Mr. Stillwood himself was an extremely old and feeble gentleman—so old and feeble it seemed strange he had not long since retired to a country chair and a warm rug.

“I am always meaning to,” he explained to me one day soon after I began. “When your poor father came to me he told me very frankly the sad fact—that he had only a few more years to live. ‘Mr. Kelver,’ I answered him, ‘do not let that trouble you, so far as I am concerned. There are one or two matters in the office I should like to see cleared up, and in these you can help me. When they are completed I shall retire!’ Yet, you see, I linger on. I am like the old hackney-coach horse Mr. Weller—or is it Mr. Jingle?—tells us of; if the shafts were drawn away I should probably collapse. So I jog on, I jog on.”

He had married late in life a common woman much younger than himself; and she had brought him a horde of needy, greedy relatives that clattered about his quiet old age like knives in a tin dish. No doubt, as refuge from her noisy neighbourhood, the daily peace of Lombard Street was welcome to him.

We saw her occasionally. She was one of those blustering “managing” women who think making a disturbance is the same thing as setting things right. Ridiculously ashamed of her origin, she tried to hide it under what her friends assured her was “the air of a duchess”—but which resembled, in truth, rather the Sunday manners of an elderly barmaid. Old Gadley alone did not fear her; he kept her, instead, in continual fear of him, speaking with a candour that must have made her feel as though her past were always being held up to the light.

He had known her in the days she wanted buried in oblivion, and he resented, as a personal insult, her entry into the old established aristocratic firm of Stillwood & Co.

Her history was peculiar. Mr. Stillwood, when he was a blasé man about town verging on forty, had first seen her—a fair-haired, ethereal-looking child in spite of her dirt—playing in the gutter. To his lasting self-reproach it was young Gadley, accompanying his employer home from Westminster, who had drawn his attention to the girl by boxing her ears for having slapped his face with a convenient sprat as he passed. Stillwood, in a moment’s impulse, had dragged the unwilling child by the hand to her father’s “place of business”—a small coal shed in the Horseferry Road—and there made an arrangement that amounted practically to purchasing her. She was sent abroad to school; the coal shed was closed. Ten years later she returned a big handsome young woman; he married her; and learned at leisure the truth of the old saying: what’s bred in the bone will come out in the flesh—scrub it, paint it, dress it in satin, it will still insist on being itself.

Her constant complaint against her husband was that he was “only a solicitor,” a profession she considered vulgar; and nothing “riled” old Gadley more than listening to her.

“It’s not fair to the gals,” I once heard her say in a voice that could have been heard in Whitechapel had the wind been favourable. I was in the next room, and the door was not quite closed—a precaution quite wasted in her case. “What real gentleman, I should like to know, is going to marry the daughter of a City attorney? As I told him years ago, he ought to have retired and gone into the House.”

“The very thing your poor father used to talk of doing whenever things were going a bit queer in the retail coal and potato business,” grunted old Gadley.

Mrs. Stillwood called him a “low beast” in her most aristocratic tones and swept out.

Old Stillwood, for his part, never professed much affection for the law.

“I am not at all sure, Kelver,” he said to me once, “that you have done wisely in choosing the law. It makes one regard humanity morally as the medical profession regards it physically—as universally unsound. You suspect everybody of being a rogue. When people are behaving themselves, we lawyers hear nothing of them. All we hear of is roguery, trickery and hypocrisy. It deteriorates the character, Kelver. We live in a perpetual atmosphere of transgression. I sometimes fancy it may be infectious.”

“It does not seem to have infected you, sir,” I replied; for the firm of Stillwood, Waterhead and Royal was held in legal circles as the synonym for a rectitude almost old-fashioned.

“I hope not, Kelver, I hope not,” he answered; and then, with that mild smile of his that could make doubt itself seem a harmless hobby, added, “And yet, do you know, I sometimes suspect myself—wonder if I may not perhaps be a scamp without realising it. A rogue, you know, Kelver, can always explain himself into an honest man to his own satisfaction. A scamp is never a scamp to himself.”

His words alarmed me for a moment; acting on Gadley’s advice I had persuaded my mother to put all her small capital into Mr. Stillwood’s hands for reinvestment—an arrangement that had substantially increased our little income. But looking into the old man’s kindly eyes, my fear slipped away like steam from a cooled kettle.

Laughing, he laid his hand on my shoulder. “One person always be suspicious of, Kelver—yourself. Nobody can do you so much harm as yourself.”

Of Washburn we saw more and more. We both called him “Hal” now. With his gentle, masterful hands he had somehow slipped my mother’s shyness off her shoulders without her noticing the garment go; he established himself as one of the family. My mother regarded him as she might some absurdly bearded boy entrusted to her care; I looked up to him as to some wonderful elder brother who knew the trick of living.

“You rest me, Mrs. Kelver,” he would say, lighting his pipe and sinking into the deep leather chair that always waited for him in our parlour. Smoke would rise and hang, softening the shabby little room, turning its poverty almost into atmosphere. “Your even voice, your soft eyes, your quiet hands—they soothe me.”

“It is good for a man,” he would say, looking from one to the other of us through the hanging smoke, “to test his wisdom by two things: the face of a good woman, and the ear of a child—I beg your pardon, Paul—of a young man. A good woman’s face is the white sunlight. Under the gas-lamps who shall tell diamond from paste? Bring it into the sunlight: does it stand that test? Then it is good. And the children! they are the waiting earth on which we fling our store. Is it chaff and dust or living seed? Wait and watch. I shower my thoughts over our Paul, Mrs. Kelver. They seem to me brilliant, deep, original. The young beggar swallows them, forgets them. They were rubbish. Then I say something that dwells with him, that grows. Ah, that was alive, that was a seed. The waiting earth—it can make use only of what is true.”

“You should marry, Hal,” my mother would say. It was her panacea for the male sex, offered as calmly as tea for a headache.

“I would, Mrs. Kelver,” he answered once, “I would to-morrow if I could marry half a dozen women. I should make an ideal husband for half a dozen wives. One I should neglect for five days and be a burden to on the sixth.”

From anyone else my mother would have received the jest as an insult. But Hal’s smile coated anything; one could swallow it without choking.

“I am not one man, Mrs. Kelver,” he went on. “I am half a dozen. If I were to marry one wife she would be married to six husbands. It is too many for any woman to manage.”

“Have you never fallen in love?” asked my mother.

“Three of me have, but on each occasion the other five of me out-voted him.”

“You’re sure six would be sufficient?” my mother queried, smiling.

“Just the right number, Mrs. Kelver. There is one of me must worship, adore a woman madly, abjectly; grovel before her like the Troubadour before his Queen of Song—eat her slipper, drink the water she has washed in, scourge himself before her window, die for a kiss of her glove flung down with a laugh. She must be scornful, contemptuous, cruel. There is another I would cherish—a tender, yielding creature—one whose face would light at my coming, cloud at my going; one to whom I should be a god. There is a third I, a child of Pan—an ugly little beast, Mrs. Kelver; horns on head and hoofs on feet, leering through the wood, seeking its fit mate. And a fourth would wed a wholesome, homely wench, deep of bosom, broad of hip; fit mother of a sturdy brood. A fifth could only be content with a true friend—a comrade wise and witty, a sharer and understander of all joys and thoughts and feelings. And a last, Mrs. Kelver, yearns for a woman pure and sweet, clothed in love and crowned with holiness. Shouldn’t we be a handful, Mrs. Kelver, for any one woman in an eight-roomed house?”

But my mother would not be discouraged.

“You will find the woman one day, Hal, who will be all of them to you—all of them that are worth having, that is. And your eight-roomed house will be a kingdom!”

“A man is many, and a woman but one,” answered Hal.

“That is what men say who are too blind to see more than one side of a woman,” retorted my mother, a little sharply; for the honour and credit of her sex was dear to her. I have noticed this: the flag of Womanhood is upheld by true women, flouted only by the false; for a judge in petticoats is ever but a witness in a wig.

Hal laid aside his pipe and leant forward, eyes bright with mischief and inquiry. “Now tell us, Mrs. Kelver, for our guidance—we two young bachelors—what must the lover of a young girl be?”

Always serious on love, my mother answered gravely, as if she were measuring ingredients for something one could ruin by carelessness.

“She asks for the whole of a man, Hal—not merely for a sixth, nor any other part of him. She is a child asking for a lover to whom she can look up, who will teach her, guide her, protect her. She is a queen demanding homage, and yet he is her king whom it is her joy to serve. She asks to be his partner, his fellow-worker, his playmate—and at the same time she loves to think of him as her child, her big baby she must take care of. Whatever he has to give she has also to respond with. You need not marry six wives, Hal; you will find your six in one.”

“‘As the water to the vessel, woman shapes herself to man;’ an old heathen said that three thousand years ago,” Hal replied, “and others have repeated him. That is what you mean.”

“I don’t like that way of putting it,” said my mother. “I mean that, as you say of man, so in every true woman is contained all women. But to know her completely you must love her with all love.”

Sometimes they talked of religion; my mother’s faith was no dead ornament to be kept from air lest it crumble.

One evening Hal burst out, fresh from some bruising contact with misery: “Who are we that we should live? The spider is less cruel; the very pig less greedy, gluttonous and foul; the tiger less tigerish; our cousin ape less monkeyish. What are we but savages, clothed and ashamed—nine-tenths of us?”

“But Sodom and Gomorrah,” my mother reminded him, “would have been spared for the sake of ten just men.”

“Much more sensible to have hurried the ten men out,” growled Hal, “leaving the remainder to be buried with all their abominations under their own ashes.”

“And we shall be purified,” my mother continued; “the evil in us washed away.”

“Why have made us ill merely to mend us? If the Almighty were so anxious for our company, why not have made us decent in the beginning?”

“It is His way,” my mother answered. “The precious stone lies hid in clay. He has His purpose.”

“Is the stone so very precious?”

“Would He have taken so much pains to fashion it if it were not? You see it all around you, Hal, in your daily practice—heroism, self-sacrifice, love stronger than death. Can you think He will waste it, He who uses again even the dead leaf?”

“Shall the new leaf remember the new flower?”

“Yes, if it ever knew it. Shall memory be the only thing to die?”

Often, of an evening, I accompanied Hal on his rounds. Among the savage tribe he served and ruled he was regarded as medicine-man and priest combined, tyrant and slave at once—working early and late, yet bullying them unmercifully, enforcing his commands with tongue and, when needful, with fist; helper, counsellor, ruler literally of thousands. He could have made barely enough to live upon; but few men could have enjoyed more sense of power. I think that power held him there.

“Nature laid me by and forgot me for a couple of thousand years,” was his own explanation of himself. “Born in my proper period, I should have climbed to chieftainship upon uplifted shields. I might have been an Attila, an Alaric. Among the civilised one can only climb by crawling, and I am too impatient to crawl. Here I am king at once by force of brain and muscle.” So in Poplar he remained—poor in fees, rich in honour.

Justice was a passion with him. The oppressors of the poor knew him; feared him. Injustice once proved before him, vengeance followed sure. If the law would not help, he did not hesitate to use lawlessness, of which he could always command a satisfactory supply. Bumble might have the Board of Guardians; Shylock the legal support for his pound of flesh; but sooner or later the dark night brought punishment—ducking in dock basin or canal, a “Brutal Assault Upon a Respected Resident” (according to the local papers), the “miscreants” always escaping; for Hal was an admirable organiser.

One night he decided a child must go at once into the Infirmary.

“It ain’t no use my taking her now,” the mother explained, shifting the small bundle in her arms with that weary competence poverty teaches. “I’ll only get bullyragged for disturbing ’em. My old man was carried there three months ago when he broke his leg, but they wouldn’t take him in till the morning.”

“Oho! oho! oho!” sang Hal, snatching the child up as if it were no heavier than a loaf. He put on his hat with a flourish. “You follow me; we’ll have some sport. Tally ho! tally ho!” And off we went, Hal heading the procession through the streets, shouting a rollicking song. The baby stared at him open-mouthed, the way infants stare at thunder.

At the Workhouse gate Hal halted us like an impatient general.

“Now ring,” he cried to the mother. “Ring modestly, as becomes the poor ringing at the gate of Charity.”

The bell tinkled faintly—small sound, large irony.

“Ring again!” Hal commanded, stepping back into the shadow, his eyes gleaming like a boy’s before a planned practical joke.

At last the wicket opened.

“Oh, if you please, sir—my baby—” began the mother.

“Blast your baby!” returned a husky voice. “What d’ye mean by coming here this time o’ night?”

“Please, sir, I’m afraid it’s dying, and the Doctor—”

The man was no sentimentalist, and to do him justice, made no hypocritical pretence of being one. He consigned baby, mother, doctor and their urgency to Hell, and would have shut the wicket—but for the point of Hal’s stick.

“Open the gate!” roared Hal, his voice filling the night as if it were another street-vendor’s cry—only this one sold terror. “Open it quick, you blackguard! You gross vat-load of potato spirit, you—”

The Romans, born rulers, held that an officer must speak the language of the ranks. Hal believed it too. His vocabulary, when he chose, was so perfectly comprehensible to his people that it had the authority of scripture. In the time it took the trembling porter to fumble at bolts, Hal had painted him, in words, such a portrait of himself—seen from all angles—that the man must have felt as if he had been stripped and held up by the heels.

“I didn’t know as it was you, Doctor,” the porter explained, suddenly humble as a dog that has discovered whose boot it sniffed.

“No, you thought you had only to deal with some helpless creature you could bully. Stir your fat carcass, you ugly cur! I’m in a hurry.”

Inside, the House Surgeon was away. An attendant or two lounged about, which was singularly unfortunate for them; for Hal, being there, took it upon himself to go round the ward setting crooked things straight. He had the air of a man who has been given a kitchen and noticed the cook is lazy: the knives are seized, the fires poked up, orders barked, and before you can say “by your leave” the place is being run properly—at least for that night and under threat of explosion.

A busy and alarming time they had of it. Hal’s presence altered the very sound of the building: doors shut more briskly, footsteps hurried, voices dropped from sulky drawl to obedient answer. He moved among them like a storm with a medical diploma.

Not until a couple of hours later did he fling himself forth again into the street, having enjoyed himself greatly, and leaving behind him an institution that had, for once, been made to remember—by voice and will and the sharp crack of truth—that it existed for the living.

“Room to let for a single gentleman.” Sometimes, when my thoughts are wandering and my feet are looking for trouble, I find myself before that sentence as though it were a familiar smell—damp stair-carpet, yesterday’s grease, and the faint despair of paraffin. I have even knocked, on pretence of enquiring, though the house knows me as a tongue knows an old sore.

The door opens—always after a pause sufficiently long to allow one to repent—upon the “slavey.” In the morning she is a catalogue of calamities: hair like teased hemp, mouth ajar as if the air itself were astonishing, arms vanished beneath an apron which appears to have seen service as a dish-cloth, a duster, and perhaps, in emergencies, a small tent. In the afternoon she makes an effort at smartness: a cap and apron, both of them dirty with the neatness of established dirt. Her hands are scored red, as if she had been peeling potatoes all her life with the wrong end of a knife.

How well I know her, and how unchanged she remains—never growing an inch, never acquiring a new expression. It costs me an effort not to say, “So sorry—forgot my key,” and slip past her, taking the stairs two at a time: narrow, grudging stairs—carpeted as far as the first floor, bare and honest about their misery beyond.

Instead I put on the voice of the respectable enquirer and ask, “Oh—what rooms have you to let?”

She scuttles to the top of the kitchen stairs and, leaning over the banisters with the solemnity of a town crier announcing plague, calls: “A gentleman to see the rooms!”

Up comes the landlady: elderly, harassed, stout with the sort of stoutness produced by perpetual stair-climbing and perpetual indignation. She is genteel in black, as though black could make virtue of everything. She crushes past the little drudge—who collapses into the wall like an apology—and regards me with the eye of one appraising a joint: not to admire it, but to calculate what may be got for it.

“I have a very nice room on the first floor,” she says, “and one behind on the third.”

I explain, with that helpless lie which is the lodger’s etiquette, that I am looking for a young friend. We squeeze past the hat-and-umbrella stand; there is precisely room if one keeps close to the wall and breathes shallowly. The first-floor room is, by comparison, an imposing apartment—its pretensions are like a Sunday hat worn on a weekday body. A marble-topped sideboard, three feet by two, stands like an altar to respectability; its doors will remain closed if you wedge a wad of paper between them, which suggests they are anxious to meet, but cannot manage it unaided. A green table-cloth—green with that particular green which has spent too long inhaling gas—matches the curtains; together they contrive to say, “We are a suite,” with the air of people who have only met that morning.

The lamp is “perfectly safe,” provided it stands dead centre of the table and is never moved, as though it were a wild creature that will bite if startled. On the mantelpiece, a paper fire-stove ornament casts over the place the oddest air of chastity—as if the room has taken a vow to be respectable even while it sins. Above it hangs a fly-blown mirror, and between the once gilt frame and the glass are invitation cards—yes, actual cards—stuck there like pressed flowers to prove that even bed-sitting-room life may, at times, be visited by society. One or two remain, faintly yellow with age, hinting at dances long finished and people long disillusioned.

Opposite is the inevitable oleograph, the kind a Cheap Jack sells by auction on a Saturday night in the Pimlico Road and warrants “hand-made,” which it is—by machinery. It is, as usual, a Swiss landscape; there is more “body” in Switzerland than anywhere else, apparently. You can always rely on a foaming torrent, a mountain with a snowy cap, a dilapidated mill, and—if the artist is feeling generous—a maiden and a cow. They look as though they were put in to reassure the purchaser that something is alive.

Then there is the easy chair—if ever a word deserved prosecution for libel. It is stuffed with innumerable coils of steel wire, each with a “business end” sharpened by experience, and upholstered in glossy horsehair that seems to grin. There is one way of sitting upon it, and one only: on the extreme edge, feet planted as though awaiting an explosion. Attempt to recline and the chair gently, firmly, slides you out as if saying, “Excuse me—you are very heavy—and the floor is far more suitable for you. Thank you.”

The bed is behind the door; the washstand behind the bed. Sit facing the window and, with discipline, you can pretend you sleep nowhere. But when two friends call, you suddenly become grateful for the bed: experienced visitors, indeed, make straight for it, declining the easy chair with a firmness born of puncture.

“And this room is?” I ask.

“Eight shillings a week, sir—with attendance, of course.”

“Any extras?”

“The lamp, sir, is eighteenpence a week; and the kitchen fire, if the gentleman wishes to dine at home, two shillings.”

“And fire?”

“Sixpence a scuttle, sir, I charge for coals.”

“It’s rather a small scuttle.”

She bridles. “The usual size, I think, sir.” No doubt there is a pattern—“The Lodging-House Keeper’s Special”—made to suggest coal while supplying mainly hope.

Since I am already committed to the comedy, I agree that we may as well see the other room—third floor back. She opens the door for me and remains, tactfully, on the landing: being stout, she does not wish to dwarf the apartment by comparison.

Here the bed cannot be ignored. It dominates the room like a relative who has come to stay. It is the life and soul of the place, and declines to efface itself for anyone. Its only rival is the washstand: straw-coloured wood, glaring white basin and jug, and other appurtenances that speak of cold water and colder mornings. It stands in its corner with the defiance of usefulness: “I know I’m small,” it seems to say, “but I matter; and I won’t be overlooked.”

The remaining furniture comprises two chairs—no hypocrisy about them; they do not pretend to be easy, and it would be indecent to accuse them of it—a light-painted chest of drawers under the window, with white china handles, upon which sits a tiny looking-glass as if apologising for its own smallness; and, occupying nearly all the remaining space (after allowing, by generosity, three square feet for the tenant when he arrives), an attenuated four-legged table that has the air of having been made by a man who disliked tables.

The sole ornament is above the fireplace: a funeral card framed in beer corks. It hangs there like the ancient Egyptian corpse introduced into banquets to remind guests that pleasure is brief; or perhaps it is meant, in dark moments, to console the lodger with the reflection that after all, he might be worse off.

“This room,” she tells me, “is three-and-six a week—attendance included; lamp eighteenpence; kitchen fire a shilling.”

“But why should kitchen fire for the first floor be two shillings, and for this only one?” I ask, for one must ask something, if only to hear the philosophy of lodging-houses.

“Well, as a rule, sir, the first floor wants more cooking done.”

Quite right, my dear lady. I was forgetting the great truth of civilisation: the higher you go, the less the kitchen fire is expected to be troubled with you.

The gentleman in the third floor back does not “want” cooking. His breakfast, madam, is what you call plain—the sort of plain that would make a prison dietary look ornamental. His lunch he takes out. You may see him between twelve and two, walking round the quiet square, up and down the narrow street that leads nowhere in particular and is, in those hours, abandoned by anyone with a purpose. He carries a paper bag, and at intervals—when he is sure nobody is looking—his mouth vanishes into it.

From studying the neighbourhood one can guess what the bag contains. Saveloys are plentiful hereabouts and only twopence each; they have a brave, reddish look, as though they were meant to comfort the poor with colour. There are pie-shops where meat pies are twopence and fruit pies a penny. The woman behind the counter—who handles pastry as a violinist handles a bow—uses a broad flat knife, and with one twist lifts the little dainty clean from its tin dish. Considering the thinness of the crust, it is a marvel she never breaks one; perhaps the pastry is already too broken to protest. There is roley-poley pudding, sweet, heavy, wonderfully satisfying—especially cold—at a penny a slice. Pease pudding, awkward to eat from a bag yet comforting on a raw day, turns up too, yellow and steadfast.

At tea the third-floor-back gentleman takes two eggs, or a haddock—fourpenny size; perhaps, on rare occasions, a chop or steak. You fry it for him, madam, though he always urges how much he would prefer it grilled; fried in your one frying-pan, its flavour becomes, as it were, confused—everything tastes faintly of everything else. But perhaps that is mercy: shutting his eyes and trusting only to smell and taste, he can imagine variety. He can begin with herrings, pass on to liver and bacon, open his eyes a moment to observe he has arrived at the joint, then close them again and end with a distinct suggestion of toasted cheese. Thus he avoids monotony by an effort of the imagination and a frying-pan of catholic sympathies.

For dinner he goes out again. Perhaps he is not hungry—late meals are a mistake; or perhaps, putting his hand in his pocket and making calculations beneath a lamp-post, appetite comes to him as a consequence. There are places cheerful with the sound of frizzling fat—how that sound carries into the street, a little orchestra of spitting and sizzling!—where fried plaice, brown and odorous, may be had for three halfpence, and a handful of sliced potatoes for a penny; where, for fourpence, succulent stewed eels may be discussed, vinegar ad lib.; or for sevenpence—these are red-letter evenings—half a sheep’s head may be indulged in, which is a supper fit for any king who happened to be hungry and not too particular about his company.

I tell the landlady I will discuss the matter with my young friend when he arrives.

“Certainly, sir,” she says; she is used to what she calls the “wandering Christian.” I ease my conscience by slipping a shilling into the slavey’s astonished, lukewarm hand, and pass out again into the long, dreary street—now echoing, perhaps, to the sad cry of “Muffins!” a sound that always seems as much lament as trade.

Or sometimes, of an evening, with the lamp lit and the remnants of the meat tea cleared away, and the flickering firelight cosying the dingy rooms into a sort of makeshift homeliness, I go a-visiting. There is no need to ring, no need to mount the stairs. Through the thin, transparent walls I can see you plainly, old friends—fashions altered a little, that is all. We wore bell-shaped trousers—eight-and-six to measure, seven-and-six if from stock—and fastened our neckties with a horseshoe pin in dashing style. In waistcoats, I think, we had the advantage; ours were gayer, braver. Our cuffs and collars were of paper: sixpence-halfpenny the dozen, three-halfpence the pair. On Sunday they were white and glistening; on Monday less aggressively pure; on Tuesday morning decidedly dappled. But on Tuesday evening, when with natty cane or umbrella neatly rolled in a patent-leather case we took our promenade down Oxford Street—fashionable hour, nine to ten—we could shoot our arms and cock our chins with the best.

Your india-rubber linen has its merits. Storm does not wither it; it braves the heat and turmoil of the day. The passing of a sponge and your “Dicky” is itself again. We had to use bread-crumbs and so sacrifice the glaze. Yet I still suspect that for the first few hours our paper dazzled more honestly.

For the rest I see little change in you, old friends. I wave you greeting from the misty street. God rest you, gallant gentlemen—lonely, friendless, despised—making the best of joyless lives; keeping yourselves genteel on twelve, fifteen, or eighteen (ah, but you are plutocrats!) shillings a week; saving something even of that, maybe, to help the old mother in the country, so proud of her “gentleman” son who has book learning and is “something in the City.” May nothing dismay you. Bullied and badgered and baited from nine to six though you may be, from then till bedtime you are rorty young dogs.

The half-guinea topper—“as worn by the Prince of Wales” (ah, how many a meal has it not cost!)—warmed before the fire, brushed and polished and coaxed, shines resplendent. The second pair of trousers is drawn from beneath the bed; in the gaslight, with a well-marked crease, it will pass for new. A pleasant evening to you! May your cheap necktie make all the impression your soul can desire. May your penny cigar be mistaken for Havana. May the barmaid charm your simple heart by calling you “Baby.” May some sweet shop-girl throw a kindly glance, inviting you to walk with her; may she snigger at your humour; may other dogs cast envious looks at you; and may no harm come of it.

You dreamers of dreams—you who, while your companions play and sleep, toil upward in the night! You have read Mr. Smiles’ “Self-Help,” Longfellow’s “Psalm of Life,” and so strengthened, attack with confidence “French Without a Master,” “Bookkeeping in Six Lessons.” With a sigh you turn aside from the alluring streets, from bright bewitching eyes, into the stuffy air of Birkbeck Institutions and Polytechnic Schools. May success compensate you for youth devoid of pleasure. May the partner’s chair you see in visions be yours before the end. May you live one day in Clapham in a twelve-roomed house.

And, after all, we have our moments, have we not? The Saturday night at the play. The hours of waiting are short: we converse with kindred souls of British Drama, its past and future—we have our views. We dream of Florence This and Kate That; in a little while we shall see her. Ah, could she but know how we loved her! Her photo stands on our mantelpiece, transforming the dismal little room into a shrine. The poem we have so often commenced—when finished, we will post it to her. At least she will acknowledge receipt; we can kiss the paper her hand has rested on.

The great doors groan, then quiver. The wild thrill! Now push for all you are worth: charge, wriggle, squirm. It is an epitome of life. We are through—collarless, panting, pummelled from head to toe: but what of that? Upward, still upward; then downward with leaps at risk of our necks, from bench to bench through the gloom. We have gained the front row. Would we exchange sensations with the stallite strolling languidly to his seat? The extravagant dinner once a week! We banquet à la Français in Soho for one-and-six, including wine. Does Tortoni ever give his customers a repast they enjoy more? I trow not.

My first lodging was an attic in a square the other side of Blackfriars Bridge. The rent, if I remember rightly, was three shillings a week with cooking, half-a-crown without. I purchased a methylated-spirit stove with kettle and frying-pan, and took it without. The blue flame had a smell like a chemist’s conscience; it made tea with a flavour faintly medicinal, and eggs that suggested they had been introduced to spirit for moral improvement. Still, it was my own fire, and that counted for much.

Old Hasluck would have helped me willingly; there were others too. But a boy’s pride held me back. I would make my way alone, win my place in the world by myself. To Hal, knowing he would understand, I confided the truth.

“Had your mother lived,” he said, “I should have had something to say on the subject. Of course, I knew what had happened, but as it is—well, you need not be afraid; I shan’t offer you help. Indeed, I should refuse it were you to ask. Put your Carlyle in your pocket: he is not all voices, but he is the best maker of men I know. The great thing to learn of life is not to be afraid of it.”

“Look me up now and then,” he added, “and we’ll talk about the stars, the future of Socialism, and the Woman Question—anything you like except about yourself and your twopenny-halfpenny affairs.”

From another it would have sounded brutal. From him it was comradeship—his way of keeping me from the soft indignities of charity. We shook hands and parted for longer than either of us then expected. A few weeks later the Franco-German War broke out, and Hal, with the love of adventure always strong in him, volunteered his services and was accepted. It was years before we met again.

In those days, on the door-post of a house in Farringdon Street not far from the Circus, there was a small brass plate announcing that the “Ludgate News Rooms” occupied the third and fourth floors, and that admission was one penny. Every morning a seedy company crowded there: clerks, shopmen, superior artisans, travellers, warehousemen—all of us out of work. Most were young; mingled with us was a sprinkling of older men, and these were always the saddest and most silent of that whispering army of the down-at-heel.

Roughly we divided ourselves into two groups: newcomers—cheery, confident—flitting from paper to paper with pleasant anticipation, selecting an advertisement as one chooses a dainty from a rich menu, and replying as though conferring a favour:

“Dear Sir,—in reply to your advertisement in to-day’s Standard, I shall be pleased to accept the post vacant in your office. I am of good appearance and address. I am an excellent—”

It was marvellous what we could all do on paper. French—written and spoken fluently, à la Ahn. German—slighter, but sufficient for commerce. Bookkeeping, arithmetic, geometry—we played with them. Love of work—a passion. Moral character—fit to adorn a Free Kirk elder.

“I could call on you to-morrow or Friday between eleven and one, or on Saturday any time up till two. Salary required, two guineas a week. An early answer will oblige. Yours truly.”

The old stagers did not buzz. Hour after hour they wrote steadily, methodically, with day by day less hope and heavier fears:

“Sir,—Your advt. in to-day’s D. T. I am—” of such and such an age. Qualifications shorter, set forth with modesty, aimed at verisimilitude. “If you decide to engage me I will endeavour to give you every satisfaction. Any time you like to appoint I will call on you. I should not ask a high salary to start with. Yours obediently.”

Dozens of the first letter, hundreds of the second, I wrote with painful care: pen carefully chosen, the one-inch margin down the left first portioned off with dots, as though respectability could be ruled in. To three or four I received curt replies instructing me to call.

But the shyness that had stood in my way at school returned upon me—I know not why—hampering me at every turn. A shy child grown-up people can forgive; a shy young man is taken for a fool. I gave the impression of being awkward, stupid, sulky. The more I struggled to be at ease, to assert myself, the worse I became; my attempts at confidence ended—so I could see—in something like rudeness.

“Well, I’ve got to see one or two others. We will write and let you know,” concluded each interview; and that was the end of it.

My few pounds, guarded as I tried to guard them, dwindled rapidly. Looking back, it is easy enough to see early struggles as humorous: one knows the story ended happily. But at the time there is no way of telling whether one’s biography will turn out comedy or tragedy. There were moments I felt sure it would be tragedy. Sometimes, when one is well, it is not unpleasant to contemplate with pathetic sympathy one’s own death-bed: one thinks of friends and relations who at last will understand and regret, be sorry they had not behaved better. But for me there was no one to regret. I felt very small, very helpless. The world was big. I feared it might walk over me, trample me down, never seeing me. I seemed unable to attract its attention.

One morning at the Reading Room I found waiting for me another of the usual notes: “Will Mr. P. Kelver call at the above address to-morrow morning between ten-thirty and eleven.” It was headed: “Lott and Co., Indian Commission Agents, Aldersgate Street.”

Without much hope I went back to my lodgings, changed, donned my silk hat, took my one pair of gloves, drew the silk case over my holey umbrella; and thus equipped for combat with Fate, made my way to Aldersgate Street.

For a quarter of an hour—being too soon—I walked up and down the pavement outside the house, gazing at the second-floor windows where, according to the door-plate, the offices of Lott & Co. were. I could not recall their advertisement nor my reply. The firm, judging by the mean look of the entrance, was not flourishing. I wondered what salary they would offer. For a moment I dreamed of a Cheeryble Brother asking kindly if I could do with thirty shillings a week as a beginning; then I remembered my usual luck and considered whether it was worth climbing the stairs and going through that ordeal merely to be told again, in another room, how little I was.

A fine rain began. I did not want to unroll my umbrella, yet I felt nervous for my hat. It was five minutes to the half hour. Listlessly I crossed the road and mounted the bare stairs to the second floor. Two doors faced me; one was marked “Private.” I tapped at the other. No answer. I tapped again. A sound came, unintelligible. I knocked louder.

This time a reply reached me, shrill and plaintive, full of pathetic entreaty:

“Oh, do come in.”

I turned the handle and entered. It was a small room, dimly lit by a dirty window, the bottom half rendered opaque by tissue paper pasted to the panes. The place suggested a village shop rather than an office. Pots of jam, jars of pickles, bottles of wine, biscuit tins, parcels of drapery, boxes of candles, bars of soap, boots, packets of stationery, boxes of cigars, tinned provisions, guns, cartridges—things sufficient to furnish a desert island—littered every corner. One could imagine being shipwrecked here and living, if not happily, at least variously.

At a small desk under the window sat a youth with a remarkably small body and a remarkably large head; so disproportionate were the two I should scarcely have been surprised had he taken it off and set it beside him for convenience. Half in the room and half out, I paused.

“Is this Lott & Co.?” I asked.

“No,” he answered. “It’s a room.” One eye was fixed on me—dull and glassy; it never blinked, never wavered. With the help of the other he continued writing.

“I mean,” I explained, coming fully in, “are these the offices of Lott & Co.?”

“It’s one of them,” he said. “The back one. If you’re really anxious for a job, you can shut the door.”

I shut it and announced, with as much dignity as a young man can summon while standing among jam and cartridges, that I was Mr. Kelver—Mr. Paul Kelver—and had an appointment.

“Minikin’s my name,” he returned. “Sylvanus Minikin. You don’t happen, by any chance, to know what you’ve come for, I suppose?”

Seeing his body, my impulse was to pick my way among the goods and pull his ears for him. But his grave, massive face suggested that, for all I knew, he might be head clerk or prophet.

“I have called to see Mr. Lott,” I replied, producing the letter and leaning across a sewing-machine to hand it to him.

He read it, then suddenly took from its socket the eye with which he had been regarding me, and polished it on his pocket-handkerchief. Having satisfied himself, he handed my letter back and replaced the glass eye as if it were a coin he preferred not to lose.

“Want my advice?” he asked.

It seemed to me advice was precisely what I needed, so I said yes.

“Hook it,” said Mr. Minikin.

“Why?” I asked. “Isn’t he a good employer?”

He turned back to his work. “If employment’s what you want,” he answered, “you’ll get it. Best employer in London. He’ll keep you going twenty-four hours a day, and then offer you overtime at half salary.”

“I must get something to do,” I confessed.

“Sit down then,” said Mr. Minikin. “Rest while you can.”

I sat. There was only the chair I took and the one he occupied.

“Apart from his being a bit of a driver,” I asked, “what sort of a man is he? Is he pleasant?”

“Never saw him put out but once,” answered Minikin.

That sounded, on the whole, hopeful. “When was that?” I asked.

“All the time I’ve known him.”

My spirits sank lower. Had I been left alone with Minikin a minute longer, I might have followed his counsel and “hooked it” before Mr. Lott arrived. But at that moment I heard the other door open and someone enter the private office. Then a bell rang, and Minikin disappeared, leaving the communicating door ajar. The conversation that came through was as follows:

“Why isn’t Mr. Skeat here?”

“Because he hasn’t come.”

“Where are the letters?”

“Under your nose.”

“How dare you answer me like that?”

“Well, it’s the truth. They are under your nose.”

“Did you give Thorneycroft’s man my message?”

“Yes.”

“What did he answer?”

“Said you were a liar.”

“Oh, he did, did he! What did you reply?”

“Asked him to tell me something I didn’t know.”

“Thought that clever, didn’t you?”

“Not bad.”

Whatever faults Mr. Lott might have, he possessed, I concluded, the virtue of self-control.

“Anybody been here?”

“Yes.”

“Who?”

“Mr. Kelver—Mr. Paul Kelver.”

“Kelver, Kelver. Who’s Kelver?”

“Know what he is—a fool.”

“What do you mean?”

All things pass—even the private martyrdoms of shy young men who have sentenced themselves to their own company and then complain of the cooking. When winter came, I took to work; there is no stew so thick as labour, and it will swallow a great deal of self-pity if you keep it on the boil. My “apartment,” with its grudging little fire and two candles that behaved as though wax were an expensive virtue, grew almost homelike. I bought paper, pens, and ink, as a man lays in flour, salt, and fat when he means to bake miracles. Great or small, what more can a writer do? He is the saucepan; whether any spirit consents to simmer inside him is another matter.

London—seasoned with a million voices, full of grave and gay, its romances and mysteries, its pathos and its humour—lay ready to my hand like a market that never closes. It asked only the right kind of listening and the right kind of noticing. Yet, in the manner of most beginners, I never thought to make my stories out of what I actually knew. I wrote instead of cottage maidens and bucolic yeomen; I laid my scenes in windmills, among mountains, or in moated granges—anything, in short, that did not require me to describe the smell of paraffin on a stair, or the particular creak a banister makes when it has been worried by five lodgers and a landlady with short breath.

A trail of gentle melancholy lay over my efforts like a dusting of icing sugar on a cake that has no cake beneath. Sentiment was more popular then than it is now, and I followed fashion as a cook follows a recipe when he has not yet dared to taste. Generally speaking, to be a heroine of mine was fatal. However cheerfully her hair might curl—curly hair, I still maintain, is the hallmark of vitality—and however many indications of robust health I crammed into chapter one (“dancing eyes,” “colour that came and went,” “ringing laughter,” “fawn-like agility”), she was tolerably certain, poor girl, to be served up cold by chapter ten. Snowdrops and early primroses (my botany I had whipped up out of a useful little book, “Our Garden Favourites, Illustrated”) sprouted everywhere as though forced under glass; and if the story happened to be near the coast, the sea-breezes would select that particular churchyard—irreverently—as their favourite playground. Years later a white-haired man would come leading little children by the hand, and he would tell them the tale all over again, which must have been a singularly gloomy form of nursery entertainment, like putting wormwood in the jam.

Now and then, for variety, I would kill the gentleman instead. It was of no consequence, so he himself declared in his last soliloquy; it was “better so.” He never got snowdrops; his grave, marked by a rude cross, lay, as a rule, in some exceptionally unfrequented portion of the African veldt or amid burning sands. For these endings I had to make a pilgrimage to the British Museum Reading Room, as one visits a spice-merchant when one has run out of all flavours at home.

Dismal little fledgelings! Again and again I shoved them out of the nest; again and again they fluttered back, soiled, crumpled, singed at the corners by my own despair. Yet one person had admired them, cried over them—myself. It is astonishing what tears a man can spare when he has no audience and no handkerchief.

I tried every method. Sometimes I sent them out with a curt business note of the take-it-or-leave-it order, as though I were delivering a joint to a customer who had already paid. At other times I attached pathetic appeals for consideration, like parsley over a plate that needs meat. Sometimes I gave them a price—five guineas—and requested the cheque should be crossed; at other times, in a burst of entrepreneurial genius, I offered my “first contribution” for nothing, the sample packet, sent gratis, one trial surely sufficient. Now I wrote sarcastically and enclosed, besides the stamped envelope for return, a brutally penned rejection note I had composed on the editor’s behalf—thus saving him time, an act for which he showed no gratitude. Or I wrote frankly, explaining that I was a beginner and asking to be told my faults—if any.

Not one found a resting place for its feet. A month, a week, a couple of days, they would remain away from me and then return, like dogs that have been kicked but still believe in the hearth. I never lost a single one. I wished I had. It would have varied the monotony.

I hated the poor little slavey who would burst into the room with the morning’s post, holding out my envelopes between apron-hidden thumb and finger as though they were soiled rags. Her chronic sniff I translated into contempt; and if, flying down the stairs at the postman’s knock, I snatched the letters from his hands, it seemed to me he smiled—as if he had heard the same song of rejection in ten thousand keys. Tearing open the envelopes, I would curse the manuscripts, abuse them, fling them into the fire; but before they were more than scorched I would rescue them, smooth them, reread them, like a cook pulling a fallen cutlet out of the ashes and insisting it is still “quite all right.” The editor himself could never have seen them; it was impossible. Some jealous underling had done this thing. I had sent them to the wrong paper. They had arrived at an inopportune moment. Their triumph would come. Rewriting the first and last sheets—my favourite sort of stirring—I would send them out again with fresh hope.

Meanwhile, believing that the would-be happy warrior must shine in camp as well as in the field, I sought to fit myself for the social side of life. Smoking and drinking were the twin sins I found most difficulty in acquiring. I do not claim mental superiority so much as confess bodily frailty. The spirit was always willing; the flesh, like underdone pastry, refused to rise to the occasion.

At school, fired by emulation, I had occasionally essayed a cigarette. The result had been distinctly unsatisfactory. After two or three attempts I abandoned further endeavour for the time being, excusing myself with a sanctimonious air that smoking was bad for growing boys; attempting to delude myself before sturdier contemporaries by striking a pose of moral disapproval; yet in my heart knowing myself a young hypocrite, dressing physical cowardice in the robes of virtue—a form of seasoning human nature is fond of sprinkling over its failures.

So, too, I had tasted the red wine that stood year in, year out, decanted on our sideboard, like a family relic no one dared admit was useless. The true inwardness of St. Paul’s prescription became plain to me; those who drink “under doctor’s orders,” regarding liquor purely as medicine, seemed reasonable. I had noticed grown men making wry faces over my mother’s claret and concluded that a taste for strong drink was an accomplishment less easily acquired than is generally supposed. The lack of it in a young man could be no disgrace—so I postponed effort, and called postponement prudence.

But now, a gentleman at large, my education could no longer be delayed. To the artist in particular training—severe training—was considered indispensable. Fashion has shifted a little since; but a quarter of a century ago a genius who did not smoke and drink (and more than was good for him) would have been dismissed without further evidence as an impostor. About the genius I was hopeful, but never certain. The smoking and drinking, at least, could be achieved by perseverance. I set to work methodically, conscientiously, as though learning a trade.

Smoking, experience taught me, was best practised on Saturday nights, Sunday offering the opportunity to walk off the consequences in the open air, under a sky that at least did not reek of other men’s breath. Patience and determination were crowned with success: I learned to smoke a cigarette to all appearance as though I were enjoying it. Lesser men might have stopped there—outward success, inner nausea; but attainment of the highest has always been with me a motive force. The cigarette conquered, I next attacked the cigar.

My first cigar I remember well; most men do, though some remember it with pride and others with pallor. It was at a smoking concert in the Islington Drill Hall, to which Minikin had invited me. Not feeling sure whether my growing dizziness was due solely to the cigar or in part to the hot, overcrowded room (air there was not, only a heated mixture of smoke and humanity), I made my excuses and slipped out. I found myself in a small courtyard, divided from a neighbouring garden by a low wall. The cause of my trouble was clearly the cigar. Inclination urged me to remove it from my mouth and see how far I could throw it—an experiment I felt would have a moral value. Conscience urged perseverance.

It occurred to me that if I climbed onto the wall and could walk along it from end to end, there would be no excuse for my not heeding the counsels of perfection. If, on the contrary, the wall proved not wide enough for my steps, then Providence itself would have pronounced against the cigar, and I might drop it without shame and make my way home to bed. I attained the wall with some difficulty and began my ordeal. Two yards further I found myself lying across it, my legs hanging down one side, my head overhanging the other—like a man trying to straddle two worlds, neither of which wants him. The position proving suitable to my requirements, I maintained it, and in that dumb pause the city’s noises came to me oddly sorted: a cart rumbling in the street like far-off thunder; a sudden burst of laughter from the hall; a dog barking at nothing in particular; and over all the constant London hum, as if the town itself were one vast kettle never quite taken off the boil.

Inclination seized its chance and urged me then and there to take a solemn vow never to smoke again. I am proud to write that through that hour of temptation I remained firm; strengthening myself by whispering: “Never despair. What others can do, so can you. Is not all victory won through suffering?” Thus did I cling to my cigar as though it were a moral principle, and to my wall as though it were a career.

A liking for drink I found, if possible, even more difficult to achieve. Spirits I almost despaired of. Once, confusing bottles, I drank some hair oil in mistake for whisky and found it decidedly less nauseous—an observation which I mention for the benefit of moralists who maintain that everything in life is arranged for our improvement. Twice a week I forced myself to swallow a glass of beer, standing over myself insisting on draining it to the bitter dregs. Afterwards, as reward and as antidote, I treated myself to chocolates to take the taste out of my mouth; comforting myself that it was for my good, that there would come a day when I should really like it, and be grateful to myself for my severity. I was training my palate as one trains a dog: by punishment and bribes.

In other and more sensible directions I sought to progress. Gradually I was overcoming my shyness. It was a slow process. I found the best plan was not to mind being shy, to accept it as part of my temperament, and with others laugh at it. The coldness of an indifferent world does harden a too sensitive skin. The gradual rubbings of existence rounded off my many corners. I became possible to my fellow creatures, and they to me; and I began to take pleasure in their company, even when it arrived accompanied by boots on stairs and opinions at full volume.

By directing me to a particular house in Nelson Square, Fate did me a kindness. It was a leaky-roofed menagerie, and I flatter myself it was an interesting one. We were thrown together not by affinity but by rent, which is the great match-maker of London. Our landlady, Mrs. Peedles, slept in the basement with the slavey and had been an actress in Charles Keane’s company at the old Princess’s. There, it is true, she had played only insignificant parts; but London, she explained, was even then a poor judge of art, hampered by prejudices. Besides, an actor-manager is always hampered by a wife—we understood. But previously, in the Provinces, there had been a career of glory: Juliet, Amy Robsart, Mrs. Haller in “The Stranger”—almost the entire roll of the “Legitimates.” Should we show any sign of doubt, proof was produced: handbills a yard long, rich in exclamation marks, as if punctuation could make genius undeniable—“On Tuesday Evening! By Special Desire!!! Blessington’s Theatre! In the Meadow, adjoining the Falcon Arms!”—“On Saturday! Under the Patronage of Col. Sir William and the Officers of the 74th!!!! In the Corn Exchange!” Maybe she would convince us further by running through a passage or two—the sleep-walking scene from “Macbeth,” or Ophelia’s entrance in the fourth act? It would be no trouble; her memory was excellent. We assured her hurriedly of our perfect faith, as a man agrees with a cook who has the carving-knife in her hand.

Listening to her, it was difficult—as she herself would frankly admit—to imagine her the once “arch Miss Lucretia Barry;” looking at her, to remember there had been an evening when she had been “the cynosure of every eye.” One had to fortify oneself with perusal of underlined extracts from ancient journals, much thumbed and creased, which she thoughtfully lent for the purpose. Since those days Fate had woven round her a mantle of depression. She was now a faded, watery-eyed little woman, prone on the slightest provocation to sit down suddenly on the nearest chair and at once commence a history of her troubles. Quite unconscious of this failing, it was an idea of hers that she was an exceptionally cheerful person.

“But there, fretting’s no good. We must grin and bear things in this world,” she would conclude, wiping her eyes on her apron. “It’s better to laugh than to cry, I always say.” And to prove it was no idle sentiment, she would laugh there and then upon the spot, the sound a little thin—like a spoon clinking in an empty cup.

Much stair-climbing had bestowed upon her a shortness of breath which no amount of panting in her resting moments was able to make good. Her life, when you listened to it, was a concert of effort: footfalls, wheezes, doors, bells. In that house bells were more than furniture; they were the reigning dynasty. They ruled Mrs. Peedles absolutely. She lived listening for them. Conversation with her was merely filling in time while waiting for interruptions; and even while she spoke her head would cock slightly, as if one ear belonged to you and the other to the house.

Once our second-floor front—a kindly young man—attempted to improve her breathing.

“You don’t know ’ow to breathe,” he explained. “You don’t swallow it, you only gargle with it. Take a good draught and shut your mouth; don’t be frightened of it; don’t let it out again till it’s done something: that’s what it’s ’ere for.”

He stood over her with his handkerchief pressed against her mouth to assist the process, as though air were a medicine to be administered by force. It was of no use.

“There don’t seem any room for it inside me,” she explained, and this, in a sense, was true: there was too much past packed into her.

A bottle of whisky fell into my hands that Christmas-time, a present from a commercial traveller in the way of business. Not liking whisky myself, it was no sacrifice to reserve it for the occasional comfort of Mrs. Peedles when, breathless, hands to her side, she would sink upon the chair nearest my door. Her poor washed-out face would lighten at the suggestion, as if I had offered her not drink but a brief return to the old footlights.

“Ah, well,” she would reply, “I don’t mind if I do. It’s a poor heart that never rejoices.”

Then, her tongue unloosed, she would sit there and tell me stories of my predecessors—young men lodgers who, like myself, had taken her bed-sitting-room—and of the woes and misfortunes that had overtaken them. I gathered that a more unlucky house could scarcely have been selected. A former tenant of my room, of whom I strangely reminded her, had written poetry on my very table. He was now in Portland doing five years for forgery. Mrs. Peedles appeared to regard the two accomplishments as merely different expressions of the same art, as if rhymes naturally led to cheques. Another of “her young men,” as she affectionately called us, had been of studious ambition; his career up to a point seemed brilliant.

“What he mightn’t have been,” according to Mrs. Peedles, there was practically no saying; what he happened to be at the moment of conversation was an inmate of the Hanwell lunatic asylum.

“I’ve always noticed it,” Mrs. Peedles would explain; “it’s always the most deserving, those that try hardest, to whom trouble comes. I’m sure I don’t know why.”

I was glad, on the whole, when that bottle of whisky was finished. A second might have driven me to suicide. Even pity can be over-fed.

There was no Mr. Peedles—at least not for Mrs. Peedles, though as an individual he continued to exist somewhere in London, like a bad smell that has found another room. He had been “general utility” at the Princess’s—a fine figure of a man in his day, I was given to understand, but easily led away, especially by minxes. Mrs. Peedles spoke bitterly of general utilities as people of not much use.

For working days she had one dress and one cap, both black and void of ostentation; but on Sundays and holidays she would appear metamorphosed. She had preserved the bulk of her stage wardrobe, even to paste-decked shoes and tinsel jewellery. Shapeless in classic garb as Hermia, or bulgy in brocade and velvet as Lady Teazle, she would receive her few visitors on Sunday evenings—discarded puppets like herself—who talked of gayer nights before their wires had been cut; or, her glory hidden under a mackintosh, she would pay her few calls. Perhaps it was the unusual excitement that brought colour into her furrowed cheeks and straightened and darkened her eyebrows, at other times so unobtrusive. Be that as it may, the change was remarkable; only the thin grey hair and the work-worn hands remained for identification. Nor was the transformation merely surface. Mrs. Peedles hung on her hook behind the kitchen door, dingy, limp, discarded; out of the wardrobe with the silks and satins was lifted down and put on—like an undergarment—Miss Lucretia Barry, somewhat aged, somewhat withered, but still distinctly “arch.”

In the room next to mine lived a law-writer and his wife. They were very old and miserably poor. It was none of their doing. Despite copy-book maxims, there is such a thing as ill-luck—persistent, monotonous—that gradually wears away the power of resistance as water wears down a stone. I learned their history; it was hopelessly simple, hopelessly uninstructive. He had been a schoolmaster, she a pupil-teacher. They married young and for a while the world smiled. Then came illness, attacking them both—not a melodrama, no moral to be extracted, merely bad drains leading to typhoid fever. They started again, saddled by debt; after years of effort they cleared themselves, only to fall once more, this time in helping a friend. Nor was it folly: the man had helped them in their trouble; they could hardly do otherwise without becoming ungrateful. And so on—commonplace, trivial, the sort of story that newspapers never print because it has no villain except circumstance.

Now, listless, patient, hard-working, they had reached an animal-like indifference to their fate. They were content so long as they could obtain the bare necessities; passive when these were not forthcoming. Their interest in life had narrowed to the one luxury of the poor—an occasional glass of beer or spirits. Often days would go by without his getting any work; then they would more or less starve. Law documents are generally given out to such men in the evening, to be returned finished next morning. Waking in the night, I would hear through the thin wooden partition the even scratching of his pen—steady, tireless, like a small insect in a cupboard. London’s great roar softened at night, but that sound remained: labour without audience, words without ownership.

Thus, cheek by jowl, we worked: I on my side of the screen, he on his—youth and age, hope and realisation. The house itself seemed to keep time: the scrape of his pen; the occasional distant bell; the creak of a stair; the drip of a tap in the kitchen like a metronome for poverty. Sound was never merely sound in Nelson Square; it was climate. It pressed upon the nerves, it sweetened or soured the temper, it could make courage feel like a thing borrowed and due to be returned.

Out of that old man my fears fashioned a vision of the future. Past his door I would slink on tiptoe, dread meeting him on the stairs. Had he not once said to himself, “The world’s mine oyster”? Had not the voices of night proclaimed him king also? Might I not be but an idle dreamer, mistaking desire for power? Would not the world prove stronger than I? At such times I saw my life laid out before me like a cheap weekly bill of fare: the clerkship at thirty shillings a week, rising by slow instalments to one hundred and fifty a year; the four-roomed house at Brixton; the girl-wife, pretty perhaps, but soon sinking into the slatternly woman; the squalling children; the endless scraping and eking, the thinning of all flavours. How could I, unaided, expect to raise myself from the ruck? Was not that the more likely picture?

Our second-floor front was a young fellow in the commercial line. Jarman was Young London personified—blatant yet kind-hearted; aggressively self-assertive, generous to a fault; cunning, yet at the same time frank; shrewd, cheery, and full of pluck. “Never say die” was his motto, and anything less dead would be difficult to imagine. All day long he was noisy; all night long he snored. He woke with a start, bathed like a porpoise, sang while dressing, roared for his boots, whistled during breakfast. His entrance and exit were always accompanied by an orchestration of banging doors and shouted directions concerning his meals as he plunged up and down the stairs, the clatter of brooms and pails flying before him. His departure left behind it the suggestion that the house was now to let; it came almost as a shock to meet a human being on the landing afterward, as though the building had been emptied by a gale.

Sometimes he would march into my room carrying his own supper-tray arranged for two. At first I resented the familiarity; I would hint at my desire to be alone, explain that I was busy.

“You fire away, Shakespeare Redivivus,” he would reply. “Don’t delay the tragedy. Why should London wait? I’ll keep quiet.”

But Jarman’s notion of keeping quiet was to retire into a corner and enact a tragedy of his own in a hoarse whisper, with gesture large enough to be seen from the Strand.

“Ah, ah!” I would hear him muttering. “I ’ave killed ’er good old father; I ’ave falsely accused ’er young man of all the crimes that I ’ave myself committed; I ’ave robbed ’er of ’er ancestral estates. Yet she loves me not! It is streeange!”

Then, changing his bass to a shrill falsetto: “It is a cold and dismal night: the snow falls fast. I will leave me ’at and umbrella be’ind the door and go out for a walk with the chee-ild. Aha! who is this? ’E also ’as forgotten ’is umbrella. Ah, now I know ’im in the pitch dark by ’is cigarette! Villain, murderer, silly josser! it is you!”

Then, with lightning change of voice and gesture: “Mary, I love yer!” “Sir Jasper Murgatroyd, let me avail myself of this opportunity to tell you what I think of you—” “No, no; the ’ouses close in ’alf an hour; there is not tee-ime. Fly with me instead!” “Never! Un’and me!” “’Ear me! Ah, what ’ave I done? I ’ave slipped upon a piece of orange peel and broke me ’ead! If you will kindly ask them to turn off the snow and give me a little moonlight, I will confess all.”

After such an entertainment, it was (much to Jarman’s surprise) impossible to catch again the thread of my own work. My tragedy, such as it was, curdled in the pot. I would abandon literature and listen instead to Jarman’s talk, which was always interesting. It was generally about himself, it is true, but on that account it was no less attractive. His love affairs—numerous as public-houses—formed his chief topic. There was no reserve about him; his life contained no secret chambers. What he “told her straight,” what she “up and said to him” was for any one within earshot. So far his search after the ideal had met with ill success.

“Girls,” he would say, “they’re all alike, till you know ’em. So long as they’re trying to palm themselves off on yer, they’ll persuade you there isn’t such another article in all the market. When they’ve got yer order—ah, then yer find out what they’re really made of. And you take it from me, ’Omer Junior, most of ’em are put together cheap. Bah! it sickens me sometimes to read the way you paper-stainers talk about ’em—angels, goddesses, fairies! They’ve just been getting at yer. You’re giving ’em just the price they’re asking without examining the article. Girls ain’t a special make, like what you seem to think ’em. We’re all turned out of the same old slop shop.”

“Not that I say, mind yer,” he would add, generous even in cynicism, “that there are none of the right sort. They’re to be ’ad—real good ’uns. All I say is, taking ’em at their own valuation ain’t the way to do business with ’em.”

What he was on the lookout for—by his own description—was a really first-class article, not something from which the paint would come off almost before you got it home.

“They’re to be found,” he would affirm, cheerfully as a man whistling against London’s din, “but you’ve got to look for ’em. They’re not the sort that advertises.”

Sunlight came slanting in through my attic window and landed, with a sort of insolent cheerfulness, on the wreckage of my respectability. I sat up and listened.

The streets were already in full throat—carts, cries, the hard iron scuffle of London getting on with itself without the least regard for my private catastrophe. I stretched for my watch. No watch. I turned my head, which appeared to have been replaced in the night by something larger, heavier, and—judging by the throbbing—partly made of mallet.

My clothes lay about as if they had tried to escape and been overtaken. One boot stood upright on the chair by the fireplace, like a moral lesson. The other had gone into hiding, as though ashamed to be associated with me.

A peal of church chimes came through the open air—clean, orderly sound, the sort of sound that makes you feel you ought to be clean and orderly too. I counted. Eleven.

I sprang out of bed, and sat down at once on the floor.

The room was still politely turning, not the reckless whirl of last night’s nonsense, but a weary rotation, as if it had danced all night and now, out of sheer habit, could not quite remember how to be still. I crawled to the washstand and, after two false dives, got my head into the basin. Cold water has a way of talking to you plainly.

Then, by degrees, I drew on my trousers, reached the easy-chair, and began to reconstruct the universe from the only point available: the present, which was disgusting, and moving backward toward the cause, which I suspected would be worse.

I felt very ill. Something had disagreed with me.

“That cigar,” I whispered, weakly and with a strong desire to be unjust to an innocent object. “I ought never to have ventured upon it. And that little room with all those people. And I have been working hard. I must take more exercise.”

It gave me a momentary satisfaction—like a man finding a coin in the street while his house burns—to discover that, cowardly though I might be, I was not wholly to be taken in by my own excuses.

“Nonsense,” I told myself abruptly. “Don’t be an idiot. You were drunk.”

“Not drunk,” I pleaded; “don’t say drunk. It’s such a coarse word. Some people can’t stand sweet champagne. It affected my liver. Do make it a question of liver.”

“Drunk,” I persisted with the brutality of a better self that has no pity. “Hopelessly, vulgarly drunk—drunk as any ’Arry after a Bank Holiday.”

“It is the first time,” I murmured.

“It was your first opportunity,” I answered.

“Never again,” I promised.

“The stock phrase,” I replied.

“How old are you?”

“Nineteen.”

“So you haven’t even the excuse of youth. How do you know it won’t grow upon you—that, having thus begun your downward career, you won’t sink and sink, and end as a confirmed sot?”

My big head fell into my hands. Temperance tales read on pious Sunday afternoons marched back upon me in grim procession. I saw myself, with an imagination that was never moderate in any direction, hurrying toward a drunkard’s grave: now struggling heroically, now yielding weakly, the craving thickening like fog. In the mist I saw my father’s white face, my mother’s sad eyes; Barbara’s mouth, lovely enough to scorn one to death; Hal’s tremendous contempt, which would have made weakness feel not only ashamed but badly mannered.

“It shall be never again!” I cried aloud. “By God, it shall!” (At nineteen one swears as if heaven itself were a witness in a police-court.) “I will leave this house at once,” I continued, addressing my room as though it had lured me to ruin; “I will get away from its unwholesome atmosphere. I will wipe it out of my mind, and all connected with it. I will make a fresh start. I will—”

Something, which had been lurking like a debt at the back of my brain, stepped forward and sat down in the middle of my resolution: the flabby, smiling figure of Miss Rosina Sellars.

“What is she doing here?” I demanded of myself. “What right has she—”

“The right of your affianced bride,” my other half supplied, with a grin that I would have struck if it had possessed a face.

“Did I really go as far as that?”

“We will not go into details,” I answered; “I do not wish to dwell upon them. That was the result.”

“I was not quite myself,” I protested. “I didn’t know what I was doing.”

“As a rule, we don’t when we do foolish things. But we abide by the consequences all the same. Unfortunately it happened in the presence of witnesses, and she is not the sort of lady to be easily got rid of. You will marry her and settle down with her in two small rooms. Her people will be your people. You will come to know them better before many days are passed. Among them she is regarded as ‘the lady,’ from which you may judge of them. A nice commencement of your career, is it not? A nice mess you’ve made of it!”

“What am I to do?” I asked.

“Upon my word, I don’t know,” I replied.

I passed a wretched day. I would not face Mrs. Peedles, nor even the slavey, as if the very sight of another human being would make my disgrace sound louder. I kept to my room with the door locked, and toward dusk—feeling a shade less as if my head were being used as a drum—I slipped out and ate a meal that was intended to be penitential: tea without sugar and a kippered herring at a coffee-house near by. The kipper—hot, sharp, honest, all salt and smoke—was the only thing that seemed to have any moral position in the world. A gentleman sat opposite and ordered hot buttered toast. The smell of it—warm, rich, irresponsible—made me feel that mankind was not taking my downfall seriously enough, and I fled.

At eight Minikin called round from the office to ask what had happened. I confessed, appealing to shame to make it seem smaller than it was.

“Thought as much,” he said. “Seems to have been an A1 from the look of you.”

“I’m glad it’s happened, now it’s over,” I assured him. “It will be a lesson I shall never forget.”

“I know,” said Minikin. “Nothing like a fair and square drunk for making you feel real good; better than a sermon.”

In my trouble I wanted advice. Minikin, though younger than I, had the calm, worldly air of a person who had been born with his sleeves rolled up.

“That’s not the worst,” I admitted. “What do you think I’ve done?”

“Killed a policeman?” suggested Minikin.

“Got myself engaged.”

“No one like you quiet fellows for going it when you do begin,” he observed. “Nice girl?”

“I don’t know,” I said. “I only know I don’t want her. How can I get out of it?”

Minikin took out his left eye and began polishing it on his handkerchief—a habit he had when thought was required, as other men rub their chins. He seemed to consult the glass orb for counsel.

“Take-her-own-part sort of a girl?”

I intimated that he had drawn Miss Rosina Sellars to the life.

“Know how much you’re earning?”

“She knows I live up here in this attic and do my own cooking,” I answered.

Minikin glanced round the room. “Must be fond of you.”

“She thinks I’m clever,” I explained, “and that I shall make my way.”

“And she’s willing to wait?”

I nodded.

“Well, I should let her wait,” said Minikin, replacing his eye. “There’s plenty of time before you.”

“But she’s a barmaid,” I groaned, “and she’ll expect me to walk out with her, to take her out on Sundays, to go and see her friends. I can’t do it. Besides, she’s right: I mean to get on. Then she’ll stick to me. It’s awful.”

“How did it happen?” asked Minikin.

“I don’t know. I didn’t know I’d done it till it was over.”

“Anybody present?”

“Half-a-dozen,” I said, as if that number were a sentence.

The door opened and Jarman walked in; he never knocked anywhere, as if doors were a superstition. Instead of his usual noisy greeting, he crossed in silence and shook my hand with the solemnity of a man offering condolences over a funeral.

“Friend of yours?” he asked, indicating Minikin.

I introduced them.

“Proud to meet you,” said Jarman.

“Glad to hear it,” said Minikin. “Don’t look as if you’d got much else to be stuck up about.”

“Don’t mind him,” I told Jarman. “He was born like it.”
