“Wonderful gift,” replied Jarman. “D’you know what I should do if I ’ad it? ’Ire myself out to break up evening parties. Ever thought of it seriously?”

Minikin said he would consider the idea.

“You’d make your fortune going round the suburbs,” Jarman assured him. Then he turned his attention back to my ruins. “Pity you weren’t ’ere last night,” he added. “Might ’ave saved our young friend a deal of trouble. Has ’e told you the news?”

I said Minikin knew everything.

“Now you’ve got a good, steady eye,” said Jarman to Minikin, who, in his manner, had fixed him with the polished orb; “’ow d’you think ’e is looking?”

“As well as can be expected under the circumstances,” returned Minikin.

“Does ’e know the circumstances?” asked Jarman. “Has ’e seen the girl?”

I confessed I had not yet enjoyed that privilege.

“Then ’e don’t know the worst,” said Jarman. “A hundred and sixty pounds of ’er, and still growing! Bit of a load for ’im, ain’t it?”

“Some of ’em do have luck,” said Minikin.

Jarman leaned forward and examined Minikin’s new acquaintance with interest.

“That’s a fine ’ead of yours,” he remarked. “All your own? No offence,” he added, without giving Minikin time to retaliate. “I was merely thinking there must be room for a lot of sense in it. Now, what do you, as a practical man, advise ’im: dose of poison, or Waterloo Bridge and a brick?”

“I suppose there’s no doubt,” I put in, “that we are actually engaged?”

“Not a blooming shadow,” said Jarman cheerfully, “so far as she’s concerned.”

“I shall tell her plainly,” I said, clutching at honesty as if it were a life-buoy, “that I was drunk.”

“And ’ow are you going to convince ’er of it?” asked Jarman. “You think your telling ’er you loved ’er proves it. So it would to anybody else—but not to ’er. Besides, if every girl is going to give up ’er catch just because the fellow ’adn’t all ’is wits about ’im at the time—well, what do you think?” He appealed to Minikin.

Minikin agreed that if such a principle prevailed, girls might as well shut up shop.

Jarman, having “got even” with Minikin, became almost cordial and drew his chair nearer, engaging him in a confidential argument from which I was excluded as completely as if I had been a teapot.

“You see,” explained Jarman, “this ain’t an ordinary case. This chap’s going to be the future Poet Laureate. Now when the Prince of Wales invites ’im to dine at Marlborough ’ouse, ’e don’t want to go there tacked on to a girl that carries aitches with ’er in a bag, and don’t know which end of the spoon out of which to drink ’er soup.”

“It makes a difference,” agreed Minikin.

“What we’ve got to do,” said Jarman, “is to get ’im out of it. And upon my sivvy, blessed if I see ’ow to do it!”

“She fancies him?” asked Minikin.

“What she fancies,” said Jarman, “is that nature intended ’er to be a lady. And it’s no use pointing out the mistake, because she ain’t got sense enough to see it.”

“No good talking straight to her,” suggested Minikin—“telling her it can never be?”

“That’s our difficulty,” replied Jarman; “it can be. This chap—” (I listened as a prisoner listens to counsel, interested and helpless) “don’t know enough to come in out of the rain. ’E’s just the sort of chap this sort of thing does ’appen to.”

“But he don’t want her,” urged Minikin. “He says he don’t want her.”

“Yes, to you and me,” said Jarman. “And of course ’e don’t. I’m not saying ’e’s a natural-born idiot. But let ’er come along and do a snivel—tell ’im ’e’s breaking ’er ’eart, and appeal to ’im to be’ave as a gentleman—and what do you think will be the result?”

Minikin admitted the problem had points.

“Of course, if ’twas you or me, we’d just tell ’er to put ’erself away somewhere where the moth couldn’t get at ’er and wait till we sent round for ’er, and there’d be an end. But with ’im it’s different.”

“He is a bit of a soft,” said Minikin.

“’Tain’t ’is fault,” explained Jarman; “’twas the way ’e was brought up. ’E fancies girls are like in plays—going about saying ‘Un’and me!’ ‘Let me pass!’ Maybe some of ’em are. But this ain’t one of ’em.”

“How did it happen?” asked Minikin again.

“’Ow does it ’appen nine times out of ten?” returned Jarman. “’E was a bit misty, and she was wide awake. ’E gets a bit spoony and—well, you know.”

“Artful things, girls,” said Minikin.

“Can’t blame ’em,” Jarman replied, magnanimous as a judge. “It’s their business. Got to dispose of themselves somehow. Oughtn’t to be binding without a written order dated the next morning; that’d make it all right.”

“Couldn’t prove a prior engagement?” Minikin suggested.

“She’d want to see the girl first,” said Jarman. “Only natural.”

“Couldn’t get a girl?” urged Minikin.

“Who could you trust?” asked Jarman, cautious. “Besides, there ain’t time. She’s letting ’im rest to-day; to-morrow evening she’ll be down on ’im.”

“Don’t see anything for it,” Minikin decided, “but for him to do a bunk.”

“Not a bad idea,” mused Jarman. “Only where’s ’e to bunk to?”

“Needn’t go far,” said Minikin.

“She’d find ’im and follow ’im,” said Jarman. “She can look after ’erself, mind you. Don’t go doing ’er any injustice.”

“He could change his name,” suggested Minikin.

“’Ow could ’e get a crib? No character, no references.”

Then Jarman sprang up as if the ceiling had whispered to him.

“I’ve got it—the stage!”

“Can he act?” asked Minikin.

“Can do anything,” retorted Jarman, “that don’t want too much sense. That’s ’is sanctuary, the stage. No questions asked, no character wanted. Lord! why didn’t I think of it before?”

“Wants a bit of getting on to, doesn’t it?” said Minikin.

“Depends where you want to get,” replied Jarman. For the first time he turned directly to me. “Can you sing?” he demanded.

“A little,” I said. “Not in public.”

“Sing something now,” ordered Jarman. “Let’s ’ear you. Wait a minute!”

He was out of the room before I could protest. I heard him pause on the landing and knock at the door of the fair Rosina’s room. In a moment he returned.

“It’s all right,” he said. “She’s not in yet. Now sing for all you’re worth. Remember, it’s for life and freedom.”

I sang “Sally in Our Alley,” but without much of the gentlemanly tremor that song requires. With every mention of the lady’s name, my imagination obligingly supplied Miss Sellars, abundant and undeniable, and the romance died in my throat. Still, Jarman was satisfied.

“It ain’t what I call a grand opera voice,” he remarked, “but it ought to do all right for a chorus where economy is the chief point. Now, I’ll tell you what to do. You go to-morrow straight to the O’Kelly, and put the whole thing before ’im. ’E’s a good sort. ’E’ll touch you up a bit, maybe give you introductions. Lucky for you, this is just the right time. There’s one or two things coming on, and if Fate ain’t dead against you, you’ll lose your amorita—or whatever it’s called—and not find ’er again till it’s too late.”

That evening I was not in a mood to believe in rescue of any sort. Still, I thanked them, promised to consider it when my head was less like a crime, and they rose to go.

Minikin went down alone. Jarman, having reached the door, returned and whispered with the handle in his hand:

“Consols are down a bit this week. If you want a little of the ready to carry you through, don’t go selling out. I can manage a few pounds. Suck a couple of lemons and you’ll be all right in the morning. So long.”

I sucked the lemons—an acid penance, but effective—and next morning, finding the advice sound, went to the office as usual. Lott & Co., in consideration of my agreeing to a deduction of two shillings from the week’s salary, overlooked my appearance with the indulgence shown to unavoidable disasters.

I had meant to act upon Jarman’s plan at once and go that evening to see the O’Kelly at his Hampstead address of respectability. I had even posted a note. Before leaving the office, however, I received his reply: he would be out that evening; would I come the following Friday instead?

Disappointed, I returned to my lodgings in a depressed state of mind. Jarman’s scheme, which in daylight had looked hopeful—almost romantic—now seemed shadowy and impossible. As I passed the open door of the first-floor parlour, its emptiness struck cold. The disappearance of the Lady ’Ortensia left the house not merely quieter but hollow. (It is astonishing how much companionship can be provided by a person one disapproves of, if only she will talk to you.)

Unable to work, the old ache of loneliness returned. I sat in the dark, listening to the scratching pen of my neighbour, the old law-writer. There are pens that write as if they were gnawing their way through time; his did that, and the sound always brought with it a grey despair, as if all the language in the world had been reduced to clauses, sub-clauses, and the steady murder of joy.

After all, I thought, was not the sympathy of the Lady ’Ortensia—stimulated for personal purposes though it might be—better than nothing? At least it had been a living sound in the house, a human voice among the mechanical scrape and street-roar. Now I belonged to nobody; and the mind, when it is frightened, will cling even to a rope that is frayed.

In this mood I heard a slight tap at my door. The slavey stood in the dim passage, holding out a note. I took it, returned, and lighted my candle.

The envelope was pink and scented, as if it had been stored in a drawer with an ambitious bar of soap. It was addressed, in handwriting not so bad as I had expected, to “Paul Kelver, Esquire.” I opened it and read:

“Dr mr. Paul— I herd as how you was took hill hafter the party. I feer you are not strong. You must not work so hard or you will be hill and then I shall be very cros with you. I hop you are well now. If so I am going for a wark and you may come with me if you are good. With much love. From your affechonat ROSIE.”

In spite of the spelling, a curious tingling stole over me as I read my first love-letter. My eyes grew a little foolish. A mist swam up—half sentiment, half the after-effects of champagne. Through it, glorified and softened like a portrait in a cheap frame, I saw my betrothed’s face, pasty yet inviting, and her large white arms stretched toward me as if they were an argument.

A sudden hot haste seized me. I dressed with trembling hands—anxiety to act before thought could catch me. Then I descended and knocked, nervously, at the door of the second-floor back.

“Who’s that?” came Miss Sellars’ sharp voice.

“It is I—Paul.”

“Oh, wait a minute, dear.” Her tone sweetened at once. There followed scurrying footsteps, the rustle of clothes, the bang of drawers, a moment’s dead silence, and then:

“You can come in now, dear.”

I entered. The room was small and untidy, smelling of smoky lamp and stale finery. But at first I saw only Miss Sellars, arms lifted above her head, pinning her hat on straw-coloured hair.

And with the sight of her in the flesh my mood changed as suddenly as if some hidden string had snapped. While waiting outside I had been possessed by an almost uncontrollable desire to turn the handle and rush in. Now—had I obeyed impulse—I should have run out.

It was not that she was unpleasant-looking. It was the air of commonness that lay about her like a fog that no scent could drive off. That finikin fastidiousness of mine which could spoil my appetite for a good chop if the waitress had dirty finger-nails now fastened itself with cruel accuracy on Miss Sellars’ red rough hands and the one or two warts that grew there. I could not look away, any more than one can stop listening to an annoying noise once one has noticed it.

“You’re a very naughty boy,” said Miss Sellars, finishing her hat. “Why didn’t you come in and see me in the dinner-h_our? I’ve a great mind not to kiss you.”

The powder she had dabbed on in haste was plainly visible. Her round soft arms were hidden beneath ill-fitting sleeves of some harsh, crepey stuff that made my teeth ache merely to imagine it against the skin. I wished her resolve about the kiss had been firm. Instead she relented, offered her flowery cheek, and I saluted it gingerly. The taste reminded me of pale dough-cakes sold at school by the porter’s wife—thin, sweet, and satisfying in a way one later regrets.

At the top of the kitchen stairs Miss Sellars paused and called down shrilly to Mrs. Peedles, who appeared in course of time, panting as if she had been chased by her own curiosity.

“Oh, me and Mr. Kelver are going out for a short walk, Mrs. Peedles. I shan’t want any supper. Good night.”

“Oh, good night, my dear,” replied Mrs. Peedles. “Hope you’ll enjoy yourselves. Is Mr. Kelver there?”

“He’s round the corner,” I heard Miss Sellars explain in a lower voice; and there followed a snigger.

“He’s a bit shy, ain’t he?” Mrs. Peedles whispered.

“I’ve had enough of the other sort,” Miss Sellars answered, also low.

“Ah well; it’s the shy ones that come out the strongest after a bit—leastways, that’s been my experience.”

“He’ll do all right. So long.”

Miss Sellars, buttoning a burst glove, rejoined me with the air of a person who has announced a public event and expects the world to take note.

“I suppose you’ve never had a sweetheart before?” she asked, as we turned into the Blackfriars Road.

I admitted this was my first experience.

“I can’t a-bear a flirty man,” explained Miss Sellars. “That’s why I took to you from the beginning. You was so quiet.”

I began to wish Nature had endowed me with a temperament like a street-organ.

“Anybody could see you was a gentleman,” she continued. “Heaps and heaps of hoffers I’ve had—_h_undreds, you might almost say. But what I’ve always told ’em is, ‘I like you very much indeed as a friend, but I’m not going to marry any one but a gentleman.’ Don’t you think I was right?”

I murmured that it was only what I should have expected from her.

“You may take my harm, if you like,” suggested Miss Sellars, as we crossed St. George’s Circus; and linked together, we proceeded along the Kennington Park Road.

Happily there was little need for me to talk. Miss Sellars supplied almost all the conversation, and every word of it concerned herself, as though she were giving me a guided tour of Miss Sellars.

I learned that her instincts since childhood had been towards gentility. This, she assured me, was hardly surprising, considering that her family—on her mother’s side, at all events—were connected distinctly with “the _h_ighest in the land.” _Mesalliances_, however, were common; and one, particularly flagrant, her “Mar” had committed—she had married, of all things, a waiter. Miss Sellars stopped in the act of crossing Newington Butts to shudder at the recollection, and was nearly run down by a tram, which seemed to me a practical comment on the hazards of dwelling too much on one’s ancestry in the middle of a public road.

Mr. and Mrs. Sellars, it appeared, had not “hit it off.” Could one wonder? Mrs. Sellars with an uncle on the Stock Exchange, and Mr. Sellars with one on Peckham Rye. His chief calling seemed to have been “three shies a penny.” Mrs. Sellars was now, however, happily dead; and if no other good came from the catastrophe, it had fixed Miss Sellars’ determination to take warning by her mother’s error and avoid any further connection with the lowly born. She, with my help, would lift the family back to its proper social position—like a saucepan restored to the top shelf, away from the grease.

“It used to be a joke against me,” she explained, “even when I was quite a child. I never could tolerate anything low. Why, one day when I was only seven years old, what do you think happened?”

I confessed my inability to guess.

“Well, I’ll tell you,” said Miss Sellars; “it’ll just show you. Uncle Joseph—that was father’s uncle, you understand?”

“Don’t be nervous,” said the O’Kelly, with that air of brisk tenderness which, in men of genius, often masquerades as impatience. “And don’t try to do too much. You’ve a very fair voice, but it’s not powerful. Keep cool and open your mouth.”

It was eleven o’clock in the morning, and the air outside the stage-door smelt of wet stone, stale paint, and that peculiar theatrical draught which seems to whistle from one century into the next. We stood at the mouth of a narrow court that funneled sound as neatly as a kitchen funnel funnels broth: every cart-wheel in the street became a drum, every distant hammer a cymbal. The O’Kelly, who heard the world as other men taste it, winced once at an iron-shod hoof striking the cobbles.

“For a fortnight past,” as I say now, “the O’Kelly had been coaching me,” and if you think that means singing in comfort, you don’t know the domestic arrangements of a man married to virtue with a conscience.

Mrs. O’Kelly—thin, acid, and morally trimmed to a point—held a conscientious objection to all music not of a sacred character. To win her husband from at least one of his sinful tendencies, the piano had been sold off like contraband, and its place filled by an American organ of exceptionally lugubrious tone. It did not so much play hymns as pronounce sentence on them. Its notes did not ring; they exhaled—like boiled cabbage left too long, only without the consolation of nourishment.

With this instrument we had had to make shift. The O’Kelly—veritable musical genius—coaxed from it an accompaniment to “Sally in Our Alley” that was less misleading than might reasonably have been expected from an object apparently designed to accompany funerals of people one hadn’t liked. Still, it did nothing to lighten our labours. My rendering of the famous ballad acquired, in consequence, a dolefulness not intended by the composer; the sort of dolefulness you get when you salt your custard by accident, and must go on eating it because there is no more milk in the house and your aunt is watching.

“Lighter, lighter. Not so soulful,” the O’Kelly would plead, while the organ groaned beneath his hands like a beast remembering its sins.

Once we were nearly caught. Mrs. O’Kelly returned from a district visitors’ committee meeting earlier than was expected. Hastily I was hidden in a small conservatory adjoining the first-floor landing; crouching behind flower-pots, breathing geranium and damp earth, I listened—heart thumping like a kitchen pestle—to the severe cross-examination of the O’Kelly.

“William, do not prevaricate. It was not a hymn.”

“Me dear, so much depends upon the time. Let me give ye an example of what I mean.”

“William, pray in my presence not to play tricks with sacred melodies. If you have no respect for religion, please remember that I have. Besides, why should you be playing hymns in any time at ten o’clock in the morning? It is not like you, William, and I do not credit your explanation. And you were singing. I distinctly heard the word ‘Sally’ as I opened the door.”

“Salvation, me dear,” corrected the O’Kelly, with the innocent boldness of a man seasoning a lie with confidence.

“Your enunciation, William, is not usually so much at fault.”

“A little hoarseness, me dear.”

“Your voice did not sound hoarse. Perhaps it will be better if we do not pursue the subject further.”

With this the O’Kelly appeared to agree—wisely, as a cook agrees with a hostess who insists that boiling improves everything.

“A lady a little difficult to get on with when ye’re feeling well and strong,” he would explain afterward; “but if ye happen to be ill, one of the kindest, most devoted of women. When I was down with typhoid three years ago, a tenderer nurse no man could have had. I shall never forget it. And so she would be again to-morrow, if there was anything serious the matter with me.”

I murmured the well-known quotation.

“Mrs. O’Kelly to a T,” concurred the O’Kelly. “I sometimes wonder if Lady Scott may not have been the same sort of woman.”

“The unfortunate part of it is,” he continued, “that I’m such a healthy beggar; it don’t give her a chance. If I were only a chronic invalid, now, there’s nothing that woman would not do to make me happy. As it is—” He struck a chord; the American organ responded as though a tomb had sighed. We resumed.

But to return to that morning at the stage-door.

“Meet me at the Cheshire Cheese at one o’clock,” said the O’Kelly, shaking hands as though sealing a bargain for a fine side of beef. “If ye don’t get on here, we’ll try something else; but I’ve spoken to Hodgson, and I think ye will. Good luck to ye!”

He went his way, and I mine. In a glass box just behind the door a curved-nose, round-eyed little man demanded my business. He looked like an angry bird in a cage—a starling with grievances.

I showed him my letter.

“Up the passage, across the stage, along the corridor, first floor, second door on the right,” he snapped, all in one breath, as if afraid a pause might let in humanity. The window shut with a click like the lid of a biscuit tin.

I proceeded up the passage. It surprised me to discover I was not in the least excited at the thought of my first introduction to “behind the scenes.” Perhaps the reason was that my mind, like a cook’s at a banquet, was occupied not with glory but with the dreadful practicalities: Would my voice rise when called? Would my mouth go dry? Would my stomach, that treacherous assistant, decide to make its own remarks at the wrong time?

I recall my father asking a young soldier home from the Crimea what sensations he had at the commencement of his first charge.

“Well,” replied the young fellow, “I was worrying all the time, remembering I had rushed out leaving the beer tap running in the canteen, and I could not forget it.”

So far as the stage I found my way in safety. Pausing for a moment and glancing round, my impression was not so much disillusionment as the realization of my worst forebodings. In that instant, all glamour fell from me as skin peels from a scalded tomato. The theatre—tawdry decorations, childish make-belief littered about—showed itself a painted thing of shreds and patches: the grown child’s doll’s-house. One could smell the paste behind the grandeur; hear, beneath the applause imagined, the honest squeak of boards and the clatter of unseen props. The sonic world here was all wrong: too many hard surfaces, too many sharp echoes. Even silence sounded manufactured, like canned soup.

The Drama may improve us, elevate us, interest and teach us. I am sure it does; long may it flourish. But so likewise does the dressing and undressing of dolls, the opening of the front of the house, the tenderly putting of them away to bed in rooms they completely fill, train our little dears to the duties and joys of motherhood. Toys! what wise child despises them? Art, fiction, the musical glasses—are they not preparing us for the time, however distant, when we shall at last be grown up?

In a maze of ways beyond the stage I lost myself, but at length, guided by voices—those penetrating, domesticated stage voices that seem to sit on your ear like a parrot—I came to a large room furnished barely with many chairs and worn settees. Some twenty to thirty ladies and gentlemen were already seated.

They were of varying ages, sizes and appearance, but all alike in having about them that impossible-to-define but impossible-to-mistake suggestion of theatricality. The men were chiefly remarkable for having no hair on their faces, but a good deal upon their heads; the ladies, one and all, were blessed with remarkably pink and white complexions and exceptionally bright eyes, as though they had been basted frequently with youth. The conversation, carried on in subdued but penetrating voices, was chiefly of “him” and “her.” Everybody appeared to be on affectionate terms with everybody else: “My dear,” “My love,” “Old girl,” “Old chappie,” Christian names—when any name at all was needed. I hesitated with the door in my hand, fearing I had stumbled upon a family gathering; but as nobody seemed disconcerted, I ventured to take a vacant seat next to an extremely small and boyish-looking gentleman and asked him if this was the room where I, an applicant for a place in the chorus of the forthcoming comic opera, ought to be waiting.

He had large, fishy eyes, with which he looked me up and down. For such a length of time he remained thus regarding me in silence that a massive gentleman sitting near, who had overheard, took it upon himself to reply in the affirmative, adding that from what he knew of Butterworth we should all of us be waiting here a damned sight longer than any gentleman should keep other ladies and gentlemen waiting for no reason at all.

“I think it exceedingly bad form,” observed the fishy-eyed gentleman in deep contralto tones, “for any gentleman to take it upon himself to reply to a remark addressed to quite another gentleman.”

“I beg your pardon,” retorted the large gentleman. “I thought you were asleep.”

“I think it very ill manners,” remarked the small gentleman, slow and impressive, “for any gentleman to tell another gentleman, who happens to be wide awake, that he thought he was asleep.”

“Sir,” returned the massive gentleman—umbrellas, like gravies, improve some men’s dignity—assuming a Johnsonian attitude with the aid of his, “I decline to alter my manners to suit your taste.”

“If you are satisfied with them,” replied the small gentleman, “I cannot help it. But I think you are making a mistake.”

A bright little woman at the other end asked, “Does anybody know what the opera is about?”

“Does anybody ever know what a comic opera is about?” replied another lady, whose face suggested experience of many plots and few explanations.

“I once asked the author,” observed a weary-looking gentleman in a corner. “His reply was: ‘Well, if you had asked me at the beginning of the rehearsals I might have been able to tell you, but damned if I could now!’”

“It wouldn’t surprise me,” said a good-looking gentleman in a velvet coat, “if there occurred somewhere a drinking chorus for male voices.”

“Possibly, if we are good,” added a thin lady with golden hair, “the heroine will confide to us her love troubles, which will interest us and excite us.”

The door at the far end opened and a name was called. An elderly lady rose and went out.

“Poor old Gertie!” said the thin lady sympathetically. “I’m told she really had a voice once.”

“When poor young Bond first came to London,” began the massive gentleman on my left, “I remember his telling me he applied to Lord Barrymore’s ‘tiger’—Alexander Lee, I mean, of course—then running the Strand Theatre, for a place in the chorus. Lee heard him sing two lines, and then jumped up. ‘Thanks, that’ll do; good morning,’ says Lee. Bond knew he’d got a good voice, so he asked Lee what was wrong. ‘What’s wrong?’ shouts Lee. ‘Do you think I hire a chorus to show up my principals?’”

“Having regard to the company present,” commented the fishy-eyed gentleman, “I consider that anecdote distinctly lacking in tact.”

The feeling of the room seemed to be with the fishy-eyed young man. The massive gentleman snorted as though he had swallowed pepper.

For the next half hour the door continued to open and close, devouring, ogre-fashion, some dainty human morsel each time—now chorus gentleman, now chorus lady. Conversation among our thinning ranks became fitful; an anxiety grew that made even the stage voices pause, as if listening to their own hearts.

At length a voice called, “Mr. Horace Moncrieff.”

In common with the rest, I glanced round languidly to see what sort of man Mr. Horace Moncrieff might be. The door was pushed open further; the voice’s owner—pale-faced, drooping moustache—put his head in and repeated impatiently the invitation to the apparently coy Moncrieff.

It suddenly occurred to me I was Mr. Horace Moncrieff.

“So glad you’ve found yourself,” said the pale-faced young man as I joined him. “Please don’t lose yourself again; we’re rather pressed for time.”

He led me across a deserted refreshment bar—one of the saddest of sights. A bar without people is like a table without bread: it tells you, not that hunger has ceased, but that something has gone wrong with the world.

We entered a room beyond. A melancholy-looking gentleman sat at the piano. Beside him stood a tall, handsome man reading rapidly from a bundle of letters. A big, burly, bored-looking gentleman was making desperate efforts to be amused at the staccato conversation of a sharp-faced, restless-eyed gentleman who never looked at the person to whom he spoke, but always at something else, as if the present were beneath his attention.

“Moncrieff?” enquired the tall, handsome man—Mr. Hodgson, the manager, as I later learned—without raising his eyes.

The pale-faced gentleman responded for me.

“Fire away,” said Mr. Hodgson.

“What is it?” asked the melancholy pianist, wearily, as though I had offered him not a song but a bill.

“‘Sally in Our Alley,’” I replied.

“What are you?” interrupted Mr. Hodgson. He still had not looked at me.

“A tenor,” I said. “Not a full tenor,” I added, remembering the O’Kelly’s instructions, as a cook remembers to warn you the dish is “not a full curry.”

“Utterly impossible to fill a tenor,” remarked the restless-eyed gentleman, looking at me but speaking to the burly man. “Ever tried?”

Everybody laughed except the melancholy pianist. Mr. Hodgson contributed a laugh without looking up from his letters. Throughout, the restless-eyed gentleman continued his humorous observations, and everybody produced the same short, sharp, mechanical laugh—like knives being sharpened in unison—except always the accompanist. The restless-eyed gentleman, I discovered, was the leading low comedian.

“Go on,” said the melancholy pianist, and began.

After the first verse Mr. Hodgson remarked, “Tell me when he’s going to begin.”

“He has a fair voice,” said my accompanist. “He’s evidently nervous.”

“There is a prejudice throughout theatrical audiences,” observed Mr. Hodgson, “in favour of a voice they can hear. That is all I am trying to impress upon him.”

So I sang the second verse in what I imagined to be the voice of a trumpet. It came out to my own ears as something closer to a kitchen funnel being tested for leaks. The burly gentleman—translator of the French libretto, though he preferred “author of the English version”—admitted he had distinctly detected a sound. The comedian suggested an announcement from the stage requesting strict silence during my part of the performance.

The sickness of fear stole over me. My voice, disappointed at the effect it had produced, retired sulkily into my boots and refused to emerge, like a child hiding under the table at Sunday dinner.

“Your voice is all right—very good,” whispered the musical conductor kindly. “They want to hear the best you can do, that’s all.”

At this my voice ran up my legs and out of my mouth, in a hurry, as if it had been promised something hot and savoury at the top.

“Thirty shillings a week, half salary for rehearsals. If that’s all right, Mr. Catchpole will give you your agreement. If not, very much obliged. Good morning,” said Mr. Hodgson, still absorbed in his correspondence.

With the pale-faced young man I retired to a desk in the corner. A few seconds sufficed for the completion of business. Leaving, I sought to catch the eye of my melancholy friend at the piano, but he appeared too sunk in dejection to notice. The restless-eyed comedian, looking at the author of the English version and addressing me as Boanerges, wished me good morning; everybody laughed; and, informed of the way out by Mr. Catchpole, I left.

The first “call” was for the following Monday at two o’clock.

I found the theatre then full of life and bustle—noise not as nature makes it (wind in trees, water over stones), but as industry makes it: feet thudding, voices cutting, a bell ringing with the insistence of a factory. The principals, just finished with their own rehearsal, talked together in a group. We of the chorus filled the centre of the stage. I noticed the lady called Gertie, and the thin lady with the golden hair. The massive gentleman and the fishy-eyed young man were again in close proximity; so long as I knew them they always were, possessed apparently of a sympathetic antipathy. The fishy-eyed young man was explaining the age at which he thought decayed chorus singers ought, in justice to themselves and the public, to retire; the massive gentleman replied with the age and size at which he thought parcels of boys ought to be learning manners across their mother’s knee.

Mr. Hodgson stood close to the footlights reading letters exactly as I had left him four days ago. My melancholy friend, the musical director, armed now with a violin and supported by about a dozen other musicians, occupied the orchestra. The adapter and the stage manager—a Frenchman whom it was good policy to mistake for a born Englishman—sat deep in confabulation at a small table under a temporary gas jet.

A quarter of an hour passed; then the stage manager, becoming suddenly in a hurry, rang a small bell furiously.

“Clear, please; all clear!” shouted a small boy with an important air suggestive of a fox terrier; and, following the others, I retreated to the wings.

The comedian and the leading lady—whom I knew well from the front, but whom I should never have recognised—severed themselves from their companions and joined Mr. Hodgson by the footlights. As a preliminary we were sorted out, according to our sizes, into loving couples.

“Ah,” said the stage manager, casting an admiring gaze upon the fishy-eyed young man—height perhaps a little over five feet two—“I have the very girl for you—a beauty!” Darting into the group of ladies, he returned with quite the biggest specimen, a lady of magnificent proportions; with the air of the virtuous uncle in melodrama he bestowed her upon the fishy-eyed young man. To the massive gentleman was given a sharp-faced little lady who at a distance appeared quite girlish. I found myself mated to the thin lady with the golden hair.

Complete at last, we took our places in the approved semi-circle, and the attenuated orchestra struck up the opening chorus.

My music—sent me by post—I had gone over with the O’Kelly, and about the notes I felt confident; but for the rest I was ill at ease, as a man is ill at ease who has been told to carve without being told which end of the bird is which.

“I am afraid,” said the thin lady, “I must ask you to put your arm round my waist. It’s very shocking, I know, but you see our salary depends upon it. Do you think you could manage it?”

I glanced into her face. A whimsical expression of fun replied to me and drove away my shyness. I carried out her instructions to the best of my ability, and discovered that there are embraces performed not out of passion but out of payroll—an odd kind of choreography, like kneading dough because the recipe says so.

The indefatigable stage manager ran in and out among us while we sang, driving this couple back a foot or so, that other forward, herding this group closer, making space through another—suggesting a sheep-dog at work, only with more profanity and less wool.

“Very good, very good indeed,” commented Mr. Hodgson at the conclusion. “We will go over it once more, and this time in tune.”

“And we will make love,” added the stage manager, “not like marionettes, but like ladies and gentlemen all alive.” Seizing the lady nearest him, he explained by object lesson how the real peasant invariably behaves under the grand passion: standing gracefully with hands clasped upon heart, head inclined at an angle of forty-five, countenance eloquent with tender adoration.

“If he expects,” remarked the massive gentleman _sotto voce_ to an experienced-looking young lady, “a performance of Romeo thrown in, I, for one, shall want an extra ten shillings a week.”

Casting the lady aside and seizing upon a gentleman, the stage manager then showed us how a village maiden should receive affectionate advances: one shoulder a trifle higher than the other, body from the waist upward gently waggling, roguish expression in the left eye.

“Ah, he’s a bit new to it,” replied the experienced young lady. “He’ll get over all that.”

Again we started.

Whether others attempted to follow directions I cannot say; my attention was centred upon the fishy-eyed young man, who did—implicitly. Soon it became apparent the whole chorus was watching him to the utter neglect of our own business. Mr. Hodgson even looked up from his letters. The orchestra played out of time. The author of the English version and the leading lady exchanged glances. Three people only did not appear to be enjoying themselves: the chief comedian, the stage manager, and the fishy-eyed young gentleman himself, who pursued his labours methodically and conscientiously, making love as though it were a duty on a list to be ticked off before tea.

There was a whispered confabulation between the leading low comedian, Mr. Hodgson and the stage manager. As a result the music ceased and the fishy-eyed young gentleman was requested to explain what he was doing.

“Only making love,” replied the fishy-eyed young gentleman.

“You were playing the fool, sir,” retorted the comedian severely.

“That is a very unkind remark,” replied the fishy-eyed young gentleman, evidently hurt, “to make to a gentleman who is doing his best.”

Mr. Hodgson behind his letters was laughing. “Poor fellow,” he murmured; “I suppose he can’t help it. Go on.”

“We are not producing a pantomime, you know,” urged our comedian.

“I want to give him a chance, poor devil,” explained Mr. Hodgson in a lower voice. “Only support of a widowed mother.”

The comedian appeared inclined to argue; but at this point Mr. Hodgson’s correspondence became absorbing again, and the fate of art was defeated by the post.

For the chorus the second act was a busy one. We opened as soldiers and vivandières, every warrior thus possessing his own private travelling bar—like a picnic that had taken a wrong turning into patriotism. The stage manager explained by example how a soldier behaves, first under stress of patriotic emotion and secondly under stress of cheap cognac, the difference being subtle: patriotism displaying itself by slaps upon the chest, cheap cognac by slaps upon the forehead.

A little later we were conspirators; the stage manager, with the help of a tablecloth, showed us how to conspire, which is to say how to hunch and whisper as though seasoning a stew with treason. Next we were a mob led by the sentimental baritone; the stage manager ruffled his hair and expounded how a mob led by a sentimental baritone would naturally behave. The act wound up with a fight. The stage manager, minus his coat, demonstrated how to fight and die, the dying being painful and dusty, necessitating much rolling about on the stage until one felt like a cutlet breaded in sawdust.

The fishy-eyed young gentleman throughout the whole of it was again the centre of attraction. Whether he solemnly slapped his chest and sang about glory, or solemnly patted his head and sang about grapes, was immaterial. He was the soldier for us. What the plot was about did not matter so long as he was in it. Who led the mob one did not care; one’s desire was to see him lead. How others fought and died was no moment; to see him slaughtered was sufficient. Whether his unconsciousness was assumed or natural I cannot say; in either case it was admirable. An earnest young man, over-anxious, if anything, to do his duty by his employers—that was the extent of the charge that could be brought against him.

Our chief comedian frowned and fumed; our stage manager despaired. Mr. Hodgson and the author of the English version, on the contrary, appeared kindly disposed. In addition to the widowed mother, Mr. Hodgson invented for him five younger brothers and sisters utterly destitute but for his earnings. To deprive so exemplary a son and brother of livelihood was not in Mr. Hodgson’s heart. Our chief comedian dissociated himself from all uncharitable feelings—would subscribe towards the young man’s subsistence out of his own pocket—his only concern being the success of the opera. The author of the English version was convinced the young man would not accept charity; had known him for years—was a most sensitive creature.

The rehearsal proceeded.

In the last act it became necessary for me to kiss the thin lady.

“I am very sorry,” said the thin lady, “but duty is duty. It has to be done.”

Again I followed directions.

The thin lady was good enough to congratulate me on my performance.

Dan and I made a compact over supper, the sort of compact a man makes most sincerely when there is gravy in it. The beef had been coaxed into tenderness by Dan’s patient spooning, and the onions—sliced thin as theatre excuses—had gone down in that slow, golden sigh they make when they surrender to butter. With the kettle murmuring on the hob like an elderly aunt who has opinions but does not insist on them, we exchanged our histories between mouthfuls, as men do when the food is good and the future still feels edible.

Dan’s past, it turned out, had been as various as the contents of a boarding-house teapot.

Leaving school earlier than I, he had gone to Cambridge; then—two years later—his father had died of an old wound from the Indian Mutiny that had never forgiven him.

“You mightn’t imagine that would upset me,” Dan said, laying down his fork with the care of a man who respects cutlery, “but it hit me hard. At Cambridge I discovered I was, by temperament, a scholar. At school they made learning into a sort of compulsory march-past—like being herded through a picture gallery by Cook’s, where the goal is not to look at anything, only to be able to say you have been there. At college I chose my own subjects, and studied them in my own way. I fed on knowledge, instead of being crammed with it like a Strassburg goose.”

He had returned to London and taken a position in a bank through the chairmanship of an old friend of his father’s. The advantage, he said, was time for study; the disadvantage was the City’s peculiar way of grinding sound into the mind—pens scratching like a thousand trapped beetles, coins clinking with the cheerfulness of chains, the constant clatter of urgency. After his mother’s death, he shook the dust of it off and joined a small fit-up theatrical company.

For eighteen months he lived among grease-paint and draughty halls; played everything from Romeo to Paul Pry; helped paint scenery; helped bill-post—an art, he assured me, in which the paste-laden poster possesses a natural malice and will, if treated without reverence, rebound upon your own head and remain there, sticking to your hair like a public opinion you do not deserve.

Wearying of the stage proper, he joined a circus; became “Signor Ricardo, the daring bare-back rider,” and also one of the “Brothers Roscius” in a trapeze act. Then, inclined again toward respectability—perhaps because he had begun to value his bones—he waited three months at Ostend, and from that descended (or ascended; social geography is uncertain) to footman.

“One never knows,” Dan observed. “I may end up a society novelist. Inside knowledge of the aristocracy will give me an advantage over competitors who only know them from the outside of their gates.”

Other callings followed like courses at a dinner too generous to be wise: he tramped through Ireland with a fiddle; through Scotland with a lecture on Palestine aided by dissolving views; he was a billiard-marker; then a schoolmaster. For the last three months he had been a journalist—dramatic and musical critic to a Sunday paper. Often I had dreamed of such a thing for myself, imagining it to be a life composed chiefly of free stalls and superior adjectives.

“How did you get it?” I asked.

Dan laughed, and poured the coffee, which came out dark and aromatic, with that slight bitterness that tastes like determination.

“The idea occurred to me one afternoon,” he said, “sauntering down the Strand and wondering what I should do next. I was on my beam ends with only a few shillings. Luck, however, has always seemed to take an interest in my education. I went into the first newspaper office I saw, went upstairs to the first floor, and opened the first door without knocking. I passed through a small empty room into a larger one, littered with books and papers. It was getting dark. A gentleman of extremely youthful figure was running round and round cursing, because he had upset the ink, couldn’t find the matches, and had broken the bell-pull. In the gloom, assuming him to be the office boy, I thought it would be amusing to mistake him for the editor. As it happened, he was the editor.

“I lit the gas for him and found him another ink-pot. He was slim and had the voice and manner of a schoolboy, though I dare say he was five or six-and-twenty. He had the job because his aunt owned the paper. He asked if he knew me. Before I could explain he did not, he went on talking, oppressed by a general sense of injury.

“‘People come into this office,’ he said, ‘as if it’s a shelter from the rain—people I don’t know from Adam. And that damned fool downstairs lets them march straight up—anybody: men with articles on safety valves, people who’ve only come to raise a row. Half my work I do on the stairs.’

“I suggested he should insist on strangers writing their business on a slip of paper. He thought it genius.

“‘For the last three-quarters of an hour,’ he said, ‘I’ve been trying to finish one column, and four times I’ve been interrupted.’

“At that moment someone knocked.

“‘I won’t see him!’ he cried. ‘I don’t care who it is. Send him away! Send everybody away!’

“I went to the door. An elderly gentleman tried to sweep past. I barred the way, closed the editorial door behind me, told him the editor was not to be seen, and handed him paper to write his business. For half an hour I stayed in the ante-room, turning away ten or a dozen people. Their affairs cannot have been important, or I should have heard of them later.

“The last was a tired-looking man smoking a cigarette. I asked his name.

“He looked at me, then said, ‘Idiot!’

“I remained firm, refused to let him pass.

“‘It’s awkward,’ he said. ‘Couldn’t you make an exception for the sub-editor on press night?’

“I told him it was against instructions.

“‘Oh, all right,’ he said. ‘I only wanted to know who’s going to the Royalty to-night. It’s seven already.’

“Then the thought came. If a sub-editor doesn’t know who’s being sent to the theatre, it means the dramatic critic’s post is vacant for some reason.

“‘That’ll be all right,’ I told him. ‘I shall be in time.’

“He asked if I’d arranged with the Guv’nor.

“‘Just waiting to see him again for a moment,’ I replied. ‘Have you got the ticket?’

“‘Haven’t seen it.’

“‘About a column?’ I suggested.

“‘Three-quarters,’ he corrected, and went.

“The instant he was gone, I slipped downstairs, met a printer’s boy coming up, and asked the name of the sub—tall fellow, black moustache, looks as if sleep is a rumour.

“‘Penton,’ said the boy.

“I went back in. The editor was still furious at the universe.

“‘What is it now?’ he snapped.

“‘I only want the ticket for the Royalty Theatre,’ I said. ‘Penton says you’ve got it.’

“‘Don’t know where it is,’ he growled.

“I found it on his desk after a little hunting.

“‘Who’s going?’ he asked.

“‘I am,’ I said. And I went.

“They’ve never discovered to this day that I appointed myself. Penton thinks I’m some relation of the proprietress, and everyone treats me with respect. Mrs. Wallace thinks I’m Penton’s discovery—she trusts his judgment; and with her I get on. The paper isn’t thriving; the salary’s small but enough. Journalism suits me; I shall likely stick to it.”

“You’ve been something of a rolling stone,” I remarked.

“From the stone’s view,” Dan answered, “I never understood the advantage of moss. I’d rather stay visible, movable. But now—what are your plans? Your opera—thanks to those gentlemen, the gods have christened ‘Goggles’—will run through the winter. Are you getting any salary?”

“Thirty shillings a week,” I told him, “and full salary for matinees.”

“Say two pounds,” said Dan. “With my three, we could set up our own establishment. I have an idea—original. Shall we work it out together?”

I told him with fervour that nothing could please me more—partly because I meant it, and partly because he had just produced a dish of devilled kidneys whose peppery steam rose like a hymn.

“There are four delightful rooms in Queen’s Square,” he said. “Charming furniture. A fine sitting-room in front, two bedrooms and a kitchen behind. The last tenant was a Polish Revolutionary. Three months ago he was foolish enough to go back to Russia, and he’s now living rent free. The landlord—Deleglise, an engraver—is an original. He occupies the rest of the house. He says I can have the rooms for whatever I like to offer. I suggest thirty shillings a week. Under ordinary circumstances they’re worth three or four pounds. But he’ll only let them on condition we do for ourselves. He hates petticoats—especially elderly petticoats. He has one servant, an old Frenchwoman—she was his mother’s housekeeper—and he and she do the housework together, mostly quarrelling over it. No other domestic female will he permit inside. Not even an occasional charwoman for us. But it’s a beautiful old Georgian house—Adams mantelpieces, stone staircase, oak-panelled rooms—and our portion would be the entire second floor: no pianos, no landlady. He’s a widower with one child—a girl of about fourteen, perhaps older. What do you say? I’m a fair cook. Will you be house-and-parlour-maid?”

A week later we were installed.

For nearly two years we lived there, and we did not perish; which, considering the prejudice that men left to themselves can do little more than put themselves to bed—roughly—should be regarded as a triumph of civilisation. The house itself had a handsome hush: old wood that swallowed footfalls, a staircase that gave out a dignified creak like a cello clearing its throat, and rooms panelled so thickly they seemed to remember every conversation that had ever been held within them. Outside, London’s iron rattle and cab-grind came and went like weather; inside, the old place took the edge off it. Deleglise’s hatred of “petticoats” may have been eccentric, but he had, by accident, protected us from a good deal of shrill domestic warfare. Modern life, even then, seemed bent on making a loud advertisement of itself; Queen’s Square, for all its proximity to the city’s clangour, still held pockets of older sound—sparrows, the knock of a milkman’s can, the human voice unamplified and therefore worth listening to.

Dan had not boasted idly. His plain cooking was excellent; his coffee—properly ground, properly timed, boiled only as much as it ought, and never bullied—was a solace. His omelettes were as neat as folded letters, and his devilled kidneys had that honest sting that makes you feel grateful to still possess a tongue. Had he remained within the boundaries of what he could do without passion, our domestic chronicle would have been as monotonously successful as a recipe that never varies.

But there is a temptation in cookery, as in art: applause. An occasional tea or supper party would kindle Dan’s ambition; old Deleglise, who for twenty years had made cooking his hobby, would lure him toward perilous heights. Dan would “experiment.”

Pastry, it became plain, was his enemy. His hand was heavy, his temperament too earnest; he attacked flour as if it were a moral problem. There was a thing called lemon sponge which demanded the beating of eggs until they held air as a net holds fish. In the cookery-book—a fat volume, lubricious with pictures of brightly coloured dishes—it rose airy and dazzling, an edible cloud. In Dan’s hands it came to table suggesting, in both colour and architecture, a miniature earthquake. It was spongy, certainly; you could force it apart with spoon and fork and it yielded with a gentle tearing sound, like linen giving up.

He had another favourite: manna-cake. Of this I will only say that if it resembled in any degree the substance that sustained the Children of Israel, then their fretfulness becomes comprehensible and their nostalgic yearnings for Egyptian fleshpots acquire a certain dignity. One slice—solid, crusty outside, marshy toward the centre—filled most people with a sense not only of repletion but of having done their duty.

For supper parties he would attempt trifles—none open to the criticism of being light as air—soufflés that guests, despite my admonishing kicks, persisted in referring to as pudding; and in winter, pancakes. Eventually he became competent with these, but in the beginning he insisted on tossing them, because Thorough was his motto. Turning a pancake by knife and fork is less showy but more certain; it avoids the danger of catching the half-baked thing upon one’s head instead of in the pan, of dropping it into the fire, or among the cinders. Dan, however, regarded these accidents as the price of artistry. And after all, small particles of coal—or a few hairs—can always be detected by the conscientious feeder and removed, thereby adding a mild athleticism to the meal.

For twenty-three hours out of every twenty-four Dan was the most even-tempered man alive. For the remaining hour he became another creature entirely—uncertain, irritable, even ungrateful. At first, in pure good nature, I offered counsel: why the custard was pimply, why the mayonnaise suggested hair-oil. My reward was sneers, insults, abuse, followed—if I did not retreat quickly enough—by spoilt tomatoes, cold coffee-grounds, anything to hand. Wounded, I would withdraw; he would kick the door after me.

His greatest enemy, in his own view, was the oven. The oven thwarted his finest schemes. It was the oven’s fault that the snowy bun emerged like red sandstone, that macaroni cheese assumed the aspect of Cambrian clay. One could have sympathised more, had his language been restrained. As it was, the virulence of his reproaches almost inclined one to take the oven’s side. The poor iron box had no voice in its own defence; it endured insult with the patience of an object, while Dan, the artist, shouted at it as if it were a rival critic.

As for our housekeeping, I can speak in terms of unqualified praise—of myself. There are, alas, fussy house-maids—who has not suffered them?—who overdo the thing, lacking repose, lacking instinct. Perpetual stirring-up of dust is, I have always maintained, a scientific error. Left alone, dust is harmless; it may even be regarded as a delicate domestic bloom, giving homeliness to objects that would otherwise gleam coldly. Nature, in her wisdom, has ordained that dust, on table, floor, picture-frame, shall sink and settle. There it remains, quiet and inoffensive—until some woman with a cloth goes summoning it into the air, where it can be inhaled by innocent people.

So also with crumbs, bits of string, eggshell, match-stumps, cigarette ends: what fitter place than under the nearest mat? To sweep them up is labour and moral peril. They cling, you grow cross, you curse, you deteriorate. Under the mat they go willingly. Compromise is statesmanship. There will come a day when you will be grateful for an excuse not to do something else you ought to be doing; then you may take up the mats and feel virtuous at the quantity of work that must be done—some time or other.

To distinguish between the essential and the non-essential—there, I have observed, woman fails. What is the use of washing a cup that will be dirty again in half an hour? If the cat is willing and able to polish a plate until not a speck of grease remains, why deprive her of pleasure to inflict toil on yourself? If a bed looks made and feels made, then for all practical purposes it is made; why upset it to put it straight again? It would surprise most women how much labour may be avoided by a judicious respect for inertia.

In needlework I confess I never excelled. Dan could handle a thimble; my own second finger was a coward. When called upon it had to be found, other fingers removed, and then it would push feebly, slip, prick itself badly against the needle-head, and retire in terror, incapable of further service. More practical, I found, to press the needle through by means of door or table, employing furniture as an ally against textiles.

The opera, as Dan had predicted, ran far into the following year. When it ended, another took its place—one in which “Goggles” appeared as a principal—and that was even more successful. After Nelson Square, my salary of thirty-five shillings—occasionally forty—seemed princely. Before my eyes floated the possibility of becoming a great opera singer. On six hundred pounds a week, I felt I could be content.

Then the O’Kelly set about puncturing my bubble with the tenderness of a man who dislikes seeing others drift toward rocks.

“Ye’d be makin’ a mistake, me boy,” explained the O’Kelly. “Ye’d be wastin’ ye’re time. I wouldn’t tell ye so if I weren’t convinced of it.”

“I know it is not powerful,” I admitted.

“Ye might almost call it thin,” added the O’Kelly.

“It might be good enough for comic opera,” I argued. “People seem to succeed in comic opera without much voice.”

“Sure, there ye’re right,” he sighed. “An’ of course if ye had an exceptionally fine presence and were strikingly handsome—”

“One can do a good deal with make-up,” I suggested.

He shook his head. “It’s never quite the same thing. It would depend on your acting.”

Immediately I began to dream of becoming a second Kean, of taking Macready’s place. Why should I not combine it with literature? Fill Drury Lane in the evening, write epoch-making novels in the morning, and author my own plays in between meals.

Every day I studied in the reading-room of the British Museum. Wearying of success in Art, I might eventually enter Parliament. Why not a Prime Minister with a thorough knowledge of history? With Ollendorf for guide, I continued French and German. It might be the diplomatic service that would attract me in my old age. An ambassadorship! A pleasant winding-down of a brilliant career. Even the Museum had its own peculiar music—pages turning in waves, pencils rasping, the hush that is not silence but discipline; and outside, the city’s clang pressed at the windows like a salesman. I began to suspect that modern life’s chief assault on the soul is not poverty but noise—noise that flattens thought into slogans, until even art becomes something to be hawked in the street.

There was excuse for my optimism. Everything was going well. A story of mine had been accepted—by a journal whose name escapes me now, and which is dead. Most papers that printed my earliest efforts are dead. My contributions might be considered their swan songs. Proofs had been sent, corrected, returned. But proofs are not facts; once before I had been lifted sky-high only to tumble because the paper collapsed before my story appeared. (Ah, why did they delay? It might have saved them.)

This time I kept my counsel. Each publication day I slipped out early, bought the paper, and scanned it eagerly. For weeks hope teased me and went away laughing. Then at last—one bright winter day in January—walking down the Harrow Road, I stopped dead before a bill outside a small news-vendor’s shop. There, in blunt print, was my name as I had never seen it: “The Witch of Moel Sarbod: a legend of Mona, by Paul Kelver.” For that, I had even risked discovery by the Lady ’Ortensia.

My legs trembled as if I had been asked to sing without rehearsal. I went into the shop. A ruffianly man in dirty shirt-sleeves looked astonished that anyone should want a copy, but at length found one on the floor under the counter—as if my future had been swept down there with the day’s litter. With it in my pocket, I retraced my steps as in a dream. On a seat in Paddington Green I sat and read it.

The hundred best books! I have waded through them all since; none ever charmed me as that one short story in that now-forgotten journal. Need I add it was sad and sentimental? Once upon a time there lived a mighty King—one—(but the names were Welsh and unpronounceable, and their choice had cost me hours among lexicons in the Museum, with gazetteers and translations from early Celtic poets, complete with footnotes). He loved and was loved by a beautiful Princess, whose name, translated, was Purity.

One day the King, hunting, lost his way; and being weary, lay down and slept. By chance the spot lay near a place which, by infinite pains and a magnifying glass, I had discovered upon the map—a name meaning in English the Cave of the Waters—where dwelt a wicked Sorceress. While he slept she cast her spell so that he woke forgetting honour and people, desiring only the Witch of Moel Sarbod.

In that kingdom by the sea lived a great Magician; and Purity, loving the King more than herself, went and begged his aid. There was but one way: with bare feet Purity must climb the rocky path to the Witch’s dwelling, go boldly up, fearing neither sharp claws nor strong teeth, and kiss her upon the mouth. Thus the spirit of Purity would pass into the Witch’s soul and make her a woman; but the Witch’s form and spirit would pass into Purity, transforming her, and she must abide forever in the Cave of the Waters.

So Purity gave herself that the King might live. With bleeding feet she climbed, clasped the Witch, kissed her upon the mouth. The Witch became a woman, and reigned with the King wisely and helpfully. Purity became a hideous witch, and to this day abides on Moel Sarbod, where the Cave of the Waters is. They who climb the mountain still hear, above the cataract’s roar, the sobbing of Purity, the King’s betrothed—though many say it is rather a joyous song of love triumphant.

No writer worth his salt is satisfied with what he writes; so I am told, and so I try to believe. Evidently I am not worth my salt. Candid friends—and others—to whom I had shown my work in my salad days, asking for “a frank opinion,” meaning, of course, unadulterated praise, assured me for my good that this first-born was a feeble, ill-shaped child: its attempted early-English a cross between The Pilgrim’s Progress and Old Moore’s Almanac; its scenery (which had cost me weeks of research) an apparent attempt to sum up, in the manner of a local guide-book, the Garden of Eden combined with Dante’s Inferno; its pathos of the penny-plain and two-penny-coloured order.

Perhaps they were right. Much have I written since that seemed good at the time and later made me blush and frown. But of this—my first to whom the gods gave life—I am no judge. Touching those yellowing, badly printed pages, I felt again the deep thrill that ran through me when first I unfolded and read them. Again I was young, and life opened out immeasurable, no goal too high. This child of my brain—my work—would spread my name throughout the world. It would be a household word in lands I should never see. Friends whose voices I should never hear would speak of me. I would die, but it would live—yield fresh seed, bear fruit I could not imagine. Generations unborn would read it and remember me. My thoughts, my words, my spirit: in it I should live again; it would keep my memory green.

During the lean years—the years when my dinners were mostly hope and a crust bought with yesterday’s courage—I kept clear of old Hasluck. He was the sort of man who, if you admitted to being hungry, would clap you on the back hard enough to dislodge your backbone and insist on feeding you until your buttons resigned. He could not understand a misery that wanted privacy. His sympathy came in a roaring, practical key, like a butcher’s cleaver brought down with the friendliest intention.

Why I preferred half-starving under the brisk, cheerful dishonesty of Lott and Co. to earning honest bread from Hasluck, whose very name smelt of roast joints and respectable gravy, I can’t entirely say. It was not that Lott and Co. were more virtuous—if anything they were less so, only quieter about it. Nor, if I’m truthful, was it because he was Barbara’s father. In my mind her parents were no more connected with her than a kitchen range is connected with the sunrise. Barbara belonged to a different pantry altogether: the one where gods keep their offerings and mortals are not allowed to touch, only to kneel and inhale.

There are instincts in us that refuse to be translated into reason’s flat, public language. I have always been one of those people who will walk three miles out of his way guided by a map, rather than ask a polite official who is paid to tell him he has taken the wrong turning. Possibly some ancient ancestor of mine was a sour, solitary cave-man who disliked the tribe’s dancing and insisted on making his own stone spoon, just to prove he could starve independently.

But independence is a luxury one can only afford after one has eaten. When at last I had got my own footing—my own small, decent shelf in the larder of life—I felt able to hold out my hand without fearing it would be mistaken for begging. I wrote to old Hasluck.

By the next post came a note so hearty it might have been written with a carving-knife. Barbara, he told me, had just returned from abroad. He added, as if it were the most natural thing in the world, that she would be delighted to see me; and, as I knew he would, he flung his doors wide, with the exuberance of a man proud of his table and pleased at the thought of another mouth to praise it.

Of my boyish passion for Barbara I had never spoken—not to my mother, not to my dearest friend, not to my own pillow. I do not think anyone guessed it, save Barbara herself. My mother liked her, certainly, but to my mother Barbara was still a girl with charms and faults, and she would talk of the faults freely, in the kindly, domestic dialect of mothers. It hurt me as a theological discussion hurts a new convert: not because it is cruel, but because it refuses to treat one’s idol as sacred.

Often, at dusk or later, I would go up to Stamford Hill and wander round and round that great red-brick house, set in its old garden like a respectable pudding in its basin. Then I would descend again into the clamorous streets—their carts, their cries, their metal-on-stone racket—feeling as a man feels who has left a chapel and re-entered a market. Even the sound changed. Up there the wind made a mild, leafy music, and the birds spoke in their own careful grammar. Down below, everything shouted in one vulgar tongue: Commerce.

During Barbara’s four years’ absence my adoration did not fade; it fermented. Like a sauce left to simmer too long, it grew rich, potent, and rather removed from its original ingredients. The living girl I had known—the child I had played with, talked to, touched—receded. In her place stood a figure compounded of memory, desire, and that strange human habit of building temples out of what ought to remain a simple room. I looked forward to seeing her again, but without impatience. The anticipation was its own feast.

Indeed, the thought of having her too near—of sitting opposite her while she ate, watching the ordinary mechanics of a mortal appetite, seeing her hair in curl-papers, hearing her complain of a tooth or a chilblain—filled me with a peculiar horror. It would have been like hearing a sacred hymn sung by a street-organ whose handle squeaks. I did not want to drag her down into the everyday; and I was oddly grateful there was no danger of my doing so. She would be older now—a woman. I felt, with equal certainty, that I was not yet a man. She would marry; and the thought gave me no jealousy. My feeling for her was devoid of hunger. It was worship, not appetite. No one but myself could close the temple I had built; no jealous priest could deny me entry. Since then I have learned that what I loved in her was not a living woman walking beside me through life, but that impalpable religion of sex by which we turn human clay into idols—then, if the idol insists on being human, we become angry with it for not fulfilling a dream we forged with our own hands.

A week passed before I went. It was late afternoon in early summer, when the day’s heat has been eaten up and the garden begins to smell more intensely of itself: warm earth, bruised leaf, the sweet, faintly peppery breath of flowers closing for evening. Hasluck had not yet returned from the City. Mrs. Hasluck was out visiting. Miss Hasluck, said the supercilious footman, was in the garden.

I told him not to trouble. I would find her.

I knew the place. It had always been her favourite: a sunny corner bright with flowers, fenced in by a thick yew hedge clipped into quaint Dutch animals and birds—silent green shapes that seemed to listen. The hedge, trimmed into imitation life, made no sound at all; it stood there absorbing the garden’s small acoustic world—the bee’s drone, the leaf’s whisper, the faint click of a distant gate. Even then I noticed how a place can hold you by its noises, or by their absence.

She was walking there, as I had expected, a book in her hand.

And again, as I saw her, something in me leapt back into boyhood: that sensation of fairy tales abruptly made real. Only now, instead of Princess, she was Queen.

She was taller; and where once she had lacked dignity, she had it now as naturally as she wore her dress. She did not hear me. My steps made no sound on the short grass. For a little while I stood in the yew’s shadow, drinking her in as a thirsty man drinks in the smell of bread from a bakery door. Her clear-cut profile bent over the page; the long line of her neck; her white hand—so white it seemed to catch the light and give it back—resting against the lilac of her dress.

I did not speak. If I could have chosen, I would have remained there, an unseen worshipper, content with the sight.

But at the end of the path she turned, saw me, and came forward, holding out her hand.

I knelt on the path and raised it to my lips. I did it without thinking; the body sometimes performs its own old rituals before the mind has time to object. When I looked up, she was smiling—amusement with the faintest seasoning of contempt, like a dish garnished with something sharp. Yet she looked pleased; and if there was contempt, it did not wound me. One does not feel insulted by a goddess’s weather.

“So you are still in love with me?” she said. “I wondered if you would be.”

“Did you know,” I asked, “that I was in love with you?”

“I should have been blind if I had not.”

“But I was only a boy.”

“You were not the usual type of boy. You are not going to be the usual type of man.”

“You do not mind my loving you?”

“I cannot help it, can I? Nor can you.”

She sat on a stone bench facing the sun-dial, leaned back, clasped her hands behind her head, and laughed—a laugh as light as the clink of a spoon against thin china.

“I shall always love you,” I said, “but it is a curious sort of love. I do not understand it myself.”

“Tell me,” she commanded. “Describe it to me.”

I stood opposite her, resting my arm on the dial’s stone column. The sun was sinking, throwing long shadows on the velvet grass and gilding her upturned face as if the light itself were her servant.

“I wish,” I said, “that you were some great queen of old days, and I might always be near you—serving you, doing your bidding. Your love in return would spoil all. I shall never ask it, never desire it. Only that I might look upon you, touch at rare intervals your hand with my lips, kiss in secret the glove you had let fall, the shoe you had flung off, know that you knew of my love—know I was yours to do with, to live or die according to your wish. Or that you were priestess in some temple of forgotten gods, where I might steal at daybreak and dusk to gaze upon you; kneel and watch your sandalled feet coming and going on the altar steps; lie with my lips pressed to the stones your trailing robes had touched.”

She laughed—a light, mocking laugh. “I should prefer to be the queen. Priestess would not suit me. Temples are so cold.” A slight shiver ran through her, as if the very word had let in a draught. She beckoned, and I came nearer.

“That is how you shall love me, Paul,” she said. “Adoring me—worshipping me—blindly. I will be your queen and treat you as it chooses me. All I think, all I do, I will tell you, and you shall tell me it is right. The queen can do no wrong.”

She took my face between her hands and bent over me, staring into my eyes with a steadiness that was almost fierce.

“You understand, Paul—the queen can do no wrong. Never, never.”

“My queen can do no wrong,” I repeated.

She laughed again and let her hands drop into her lap.

“Now you may sit beside me. So much honour, Paul, shall you have to-day, but it must last you a long time. Tell me what you have been doing. It may amuse me. Afterward you shall hear what I have done, and you shall say it was right and good.”

So I sat and told her. I sketched it briefly—my struggle, my work, the shifts and humiliations—and I left nothing out, not even that transit of the Lady ’Ortensia, though the memory of it tasted sour in my mouth, like wine gone wrong.

At that she grew a little grave.

“You must do nothing again, Paul,” she commanded, “to make me ashamed of you, or I shall dismiss you from my presence for ever. I must be proud of you, or you shall not serve me. In dishonouring yourself you dishonour me. I am angry with you, Paul. Do not let me be angry with you again.”

And so that passed. Her love—her demand for worship, her insistence that I remain worthy of her—did not save me always from the chattering, simian voices that whisper to the animal in us. But it did help my life, as only love can: by making one ashamed to be small.

Later, the morning glory fades, the veil tears, and we see with cold eyes. My love was a woman. She lies dead. They mocked her white, sweet limbs with rags and tatters, but they cannot cheat love’s eyes. God knows I loved her in all purity. Only with false love do we love the false. Beneath the unclean clinging garments she sleeps fair.

When my tale was done, she said, “Now I will tell you mine. I am going to be married soon. I shall be a Countess, Paul—the Countess Huescar. I will teach you how to pronounce it. And I shall have a real castle in Spain.”

I must have looked as if someone had unexpectedly put pepper in my tea, for she laughed.

“You need not look so frightened. We shall not live there. It is half-ruined and gloomy, among the mountains, and he loves it even less than I do. Paris and London will be my courts—so you will see me often. You shall know the great world, Paul: the world I mean to conquer, where I mean to rule.”

“Is he very rich?” I asked.

“As poor,” she laughed, “as a Spanish nobleman. The money I shall have to provide—or rather, poor dear Dad will. He gives me title, position. Of course I do not love him, handsome though he is. Don’t look so solemn, Paul. We shall get on together well enough. Queens do not make love matches; they contract alliances. I have done well. Congratulate me. Do you hear? Say that I have acted rightly.”

“Does he love you?” I asked.

“He tells me so,” she answered, laughing. “How uncourtier-like you are, Paul! Do you suggest that any man could see me and not love me?”

She sprang to her feet, and there was a sudden crackle in her, as if the smooth surface had split and fire showed underneath.

“I do not want his love,” she cried. “It would bore me. Women hate love they cannot return. I don’t mean love like yours, devout little Paul,” she added, with a laugh that tried to make a jest of the bitterness. “That is sweet incense wafted round us, that we like to scent with our noses in the air. Give me that, Paul; I want it, I ask for it. But the love of a hand, the love of a husband one does not care for—it would be horrible!”

For a moment I felt myself grow older, as if I had been handed a burden I had not expected. My goddess looked suddenly like a child who needed help and did not know how to ask for it plainly.

“But have you thought—” I began.

“Yes, yes,” she cut in quickly. “I have thought and thought till I can think no more. There must be some sacrifice; it must be as little as need be, that is all. He does not love me. He is marrying me for my money—I know that, and I am glad of it.

“You do not know me, Paul. I must have rank, position. What am I? The daughter of rich old Hasluck, who began life as a butcher in the Mile End Road. As the Princess Huescar, society will forget; as Mrs.”—and there she checked herself, abruptly—“Jones or Brown it would remember, however rich I might be. I am vain, Paul—caring for power—ambition. I have my father’s blood in me. All his nights and days he has spent gaining wealth; he can do no more. We upstarts have our pride of race. He has done his share; I must do mine.”

“But you need not be mere Mrs. Anybody,” I argued. “Why not wait? You will meet someone who can give you position and whom at the same time you can love. Would that not be better?”

“He will never come,” she said. “Because, my little Paul, he has come already. Hush. The queen can do no wrong.”

“Who is he?” I asked. “May I not know?”

“Yes,” she answered. “You shall know. I want you to know—then you shall tell me I have acted rightly. Do you hear me? Quite rightly—that you still respect me and honour me.

“He could not help me. As his wife I should be less even than I am: a mere rich nobody giving long dinner-parties to other rich nobodies; living among City men and retired trades-people; envied only by their fat, vulgarly dressed wives; courted by seedy Bohemians for the sake of my cook; with perhaps an opera singer or an impecunious nobleman or two out of Dad’s City list for show-guests. Is that the court where you would have your queen reign?”

“Is he so commonplace a man,” I said, “the man you love? I cannot believe it.”

“He is not commonplace,” she answered. “It is I who am commonplace. The things I desire are beneath him. He will never trouble himself to secure them.”

“Not even for love of you?”

“I would not have him do so even were he willing. He is great—with a greatness I cannot even understand. He is not the man for these times. In old days I should have married him, knowing he would climb to greatness by sheer strength of manhood. But now men do not climb; they crawl to greatness. He could not do that. I have done right, Paul.”

“What does he say?” I asked.

“Shall I tell you?” She laughed, but there was a harshness in it now, like a knife scraping a plate. “I can give you his exact words: ‘You are half a woman and half a fool, so woman-like you will follow your folly. But let your folly see to it that your woman makes no fool of herself.’”

The words rang in my mind with a voice I knew—strong, contemptuous, honest in its brutality.

“Hal!” I cried. “It is he.”

“So you never guessed even that,” she said. “I thought at times it would be sweet to cry it aloud—that it could have made no difference—that everyone who knew me must have read it in my eyes.”

“But he never seemed to take much notice of you,” I said.

She laughed. “You needn’t be so unkind. What did I ever do for you much more than snub you? We boys and girls—there isn’t so much difference between us: we love our masters. Yet you must not think so poorly of me. I was only a child to him then. But we were locked up in Paris together during the entire siege. Have you not heard? He did take a little notice of me there, Paul, I assure you.”
