Would it have been better, I sometimes wonder, had she followed the woman and not the fool? It sounds an easy question. Yet my mind loops back—always back—to a winter night years later at Tiefenkasten in the Julier Pass, as if life insists on repeating its refrains until we learn the tune.

I was travelling from St. Moritz to Chur. I was the sole passenger. The sledge ride had turned my face into something that felt carved from salted meat. At the inn I stood thawing before the stove in the common room, while the room’s noises—boots stamping, a spoon clinking in a mug, a log shifting—seemed louder for the thick hush outside, where snow muffled the world as thoroughly as a pillow over a bell.

A waiter handed me a pencilled note:

“Come up and see me. I am a prisoner in this damned hole till the weather breaks. Hal.”

I went up.

I hardly recognised him. He was only a ghost of the Hal I had known—a magnificent body reduced, by privation and savage exertion during the Paris siege, and then by the loose, wild living that followed, into a ruin that still somehow looked defiant about being ruined.

His room was a gloomy vaulted place that had once been a chapel. It was dimly lit by a cheap lamp that smelt of bad oil and worse economies, and it was heated to suffocation by one of those great green-tiled German ovens that seem built less to warm you than to persuade you to give up and melt.

He sat propped on pillows near one of the high windows. His eyes—those deep eyes—flared out of his drawn face like two caverns with fire at the bottom.

“I saw you from the window,” he said. “It is the only excitement I get: twice a day when the sledges come in. I broke down crossing the Pass a fortnight ago, on my way from Davos. We were stuck in a drift for eighteen hours; it nearly finished my last lung. And I haven’t even a book. By God, lad, I was glad to see your frosted face ten minutes ago in the lantern-light.”

He seized my hand with long bony fingers.

“Sit down, and let me hear my voice using again its mother tongue. For the last eight years I have hardly spoken it. You were always a good listener. Can you stand the room? The windows ought to be open, but what does it matter? I may as well get accustomed to the heat before I die.”

I drew a chair close. For a while, between his fits of coughing, he talked and talked—boisterously, wildly—laughing at my remonstrances until the laughter became a struggle for breath. The room had its own ugly accompaniment: the oven’s faint ticking, the lamp’s sickly hiss, the building’s old stones holding the silence like a taste you cannot rinse away. Outside, the wind made a long, lonely sound through the Pass—a clean, natural voice, far kinder than the commercial clatter of cities. It struck me then how much of our sanity depends upon what we hear all day. In towns we are bullied by noise. Here the world spoke softly and humanly, and Hal, in his feverish life, had somehow lost even that.

Suddenly he stopped.

“What is she doing?” he asked. “Do you ever see her?”

“She is playing in—” I named a comic opera then running in Paris. “No; I have not seen her for some time.”

He laid his wasted hand on mine. “What a pity you and I could not have rolled ourselves into one, Paul—you, the saint, and I, the satyr. Together we should have made her perfect lover.”

I remembered, absurdly and painfully, those long nights of my childhood when I had lain awake listening to my father and mother’s voices through the thin wall—anger seeping through plaster, love and cruelty sharing one cramped vocabulary. It seemed my fate to stand helpless between people I loved, watching them hurt one another against their will.

“Tell me,” I said. “I loved her, knowing her. I was not blind. Whose fault was it—yours or hers?”

He laughed.

“Whose fault? God made us.”

For an instant I hated him for the laugh. But then I looked into his eyes and saw the pain living there like a tenant who would never be evicted, and my hatred drained away into pity.

A smile twisted his ugly mouth.

“You’ve been on the stage, Paul,” he said. “You must have heard it: ‘Ah, well, the curtain must come down, however badly things are going.’ It is only a play. We do not choose our parts. I did not even know I was the villain till I heard the booing of the gallery. I even thought I was the hero—full of noble sentiment—sacrificing myself for the happiness of the heroine. She would have married me in the beginning, had I plagued her sufficiently.”

I tried to speak, but he waved me down and went on, his voice pushing forward as if it had to empty itself before it ran out.

“Ah yes, it might have been better. That is easy to say, not knowing. So too it might have been worse—in all probability much the same. All roads lead to the end. You know I was always a fatalist. We tried both ways.

“She loved me well enough, but she loved the world also. I thought she loved it better. So I kissed her on her brow, mumbled a prayer for her happiness, and made my exit to a choking sob. So ended the first act. Wasn’t I the hero throughout that, Paul? I thought so—slapped myself on the back, told myself what a fine fellow I had been.

“Then—you know what followed. She was finer clay than she fancied. Love is woman’s kingdom, not the world. Even then I thought more of her than of myself. I could have borne my share of the burden if I had not seen her fainting under hers—shamed, degraded. So we dared to think for ourselves, injuring nobody but ourselves; played the man and woman; lost the world for love. Wasn’t it brave? Were we not hero and heroine? They printed the playbill wrong, Paul, that was all. I was really the hero, but the printing devil made a slip—so instead of applauding you booed. How could you know? It was not your fault.”

“But that was not the end,” I said. “If the curtain had fallen then, I could have forgiven you.”

He grinned. “That fatal last act. Even yours don’t always come right, so the critics tell me.”

The grin went. He stared past me a moment, as if he saw the rest of his story written on the wall like a play he had to perform to the end.

“We may never see each other again, Paul. Don’t think too badly of me. I found I had made a second mistake—or thought I had. She was no happier with me, after a time, than she had been with him. If all our longings were one, life would be easy. But they are not. What is to be done but toss for it? If it comes down head, we wish it had been tail; and if tail, we think of what we have lost through its not coming down head.

“Love is no more the whole of a woman’s life than it is of a man’s. He did not apply for a divorce—that was smart of him. We were shunned, ignored. To some women it might not have mattered; but she had been used to being sought, courted, feted. She made no complaint—did worse: made desperate effort to appear cheerful, to pretend that our humdrum life was not boring her to death. I watched her growing more listless, more depressed; grew angry with her, angrier with myself.

“There was no bond between us except our passion. That was real enough—‘grand,’ I believe, is the approved literary adjective. It is good enough for what nature intended it: a summer season in a cave. It makes a poor marriage settlement in these more complicated days. We fell to mutual recriminations—vulgar scenes. Ah, most of us look better at a little distance.

“The sordid side of life became important. I was never rich; by contrast with all she had known, miserably poor. The mere sight of the food our twelve-pound-a-year cook put on the table would take away her appetite. Love does not change the palate, give you a taste for cheap claret when you’ve been accustomed to dry champagne. We have bodies to think of as well as souls; we forget that in moments of excitement.”

He coughed then—a deep tearing cough that made me want to strike the green oven for its heat and the lamp for its stench, as if either were to blame. When he spoke again his voice had lost its bravado and sounded like something scraped raw.

“She fell ill. And it seemed to me I had dragged her from the soil where she had grown only to watch her die.

“And then he came—precisely at the right moment. I cannot help admiring him. Most men take revenge clumsily, hurting themselves. He was neat. He had been patient. I am not even ashamed of falling into his trap; it was admirably baited.

“Maybe I had despised him for seeming to submit meekly to the blow. What cared he for me and my opinion? It was she he cared for. He knew her better than I did—knew that sooner or later she would tire, not of love but of the cottage; look back with longing eyes to all she had lost.

“Fool! Cuckold! What was it to him that the world would laugh at him, despise him? Love such as his makes fools of men. Would I not give her back to him?

“Return—”

Of old Deleglise’s Sunday suppers—those solemn, jubilant rites performed in a great kitchen as bright as a conscience that has forgotten everything except cleanliness—much has already been written by persons who knew, or at least who knew the correct people to say they knew. Deleglise, buttoned into linen from throat to wrist as though he were about to be served instead of serving, moved about his stove with a flushed, almost devotional earnestness. The stove shone; the copper shone; the saucepans shone; and in the intervals of that shining there would be the soft, brisk whisper of Madeline’s white cap as she leaned, nodded, darted—an ancient bonne with hands that could fold a napkin into a swan while her nose judged a béchamel.

Behind him, round the massive oaken table, sat guests waiting in the reverent hush that falls when food is not yet on the tongue. The glass cut the lamplight into little crystals. The silver made small, domestic chimes when a spoon was shifted or a fork was set down. People talked in underbreaths until Deleglise turned, radiant and reborn, bearing the savoury triumph steaming between his hands. Then, as though somebody had struck a chord, talk would swell again—first a murmur, then a roar—like music returning after a pause that had been holding everybody’s nerves by the collar.

They were famous men—names that were already household words and in some cases have remained so. Artists, musicians, actors, writers, scholars, and those droll persons who do not belong to a profession so much as to an atmosphere. Their epigrams and anecdotes can be had in memoirs; and if you, gentle reader, find them less dazzling on the page than we did in the room, blame time and print and the fact that wit, like soufflé, does not travel.

So I shall not attempt to warm up old dishes you can order elsewhere. My own little entry into that charmed circle was brief; what matters more to my tale are the afternoon At Homes that Dan and I held in Queen Square, on the second floor of a quiet old Georgian house where even the staircase seemed to creak in an accent older than modern London. On those days cook and housemaid had a morning of it. And because we had no sensible authority to keep us from quarrelling, Dan and I made a compact: each should supervise the other’s department. I judged Dan’s cooking; Dan judged my housekeeping. It was as harmonious as asking two cocks to arrange the sunrise.

“Too much soda,” I would say, tasting the cake with the wary air of a man stepping on ice.

“You silly Juggins! It’s meant to taste of soda—it’s a soda cake.”

“I know it is. It isn’t meant to taste of nothing but soda. There wants to be some cake about it also. This thing is a solidified Seidlitz powder. You can’t give people Seidlitz powders for tea, even if you set them in a plate and call them ‘home-made.’”

Dan would fume, but I would remain granite. The soda cake would be laid aside like a moral error, and something else attempted. Dan’s cookery was the one thing he was obstinate about. He would never admit wrong in it; and his most ghastly failures he would later devour himself with an expression of martyr-like enjoyment. I have known him finish a sponge cake whose centre needed a teaspoon, and declare it delicious—maintaining, with a theorist’s passion, that a dry sponge cake was like eating dust, that sponge cake ought to be “a trifle syrupy towards the centre.” Afterwards he would be strangely silent and drink brandy out of a wine-glass, as though to rinse his principles.

Then Dan would turn on me.

“Call these knives clean?”

“Yes, I do.”

Dan would draw his finger across one and produce a mark like a landscape in chiaroscuro.

“Not if you go fingering them. Why don’t you leave them alone and go on with your own work?”

“You’ve just wiped them, that’s all.”

“Well, there isn’t any knife-powder.”

“Yes, there is.”

“Besides, it ruins knives, over-cleaning them—takes all the edge off. We shall want them sharp to cut those lemon buns of yours.”

“Over-cleaning them! You don’t take any pride in the place.”

“Good Lord! Don’t I work from morning to night?”

“You lazy young devil!”

“Makes one lazy, your cooking. How can a man work when he is suffering all day long from indigestion?”

But Dan would not rest until I had fetched the board and polished every blade to his entire satisfaction, as if each knife were being prepared to carve a reputation. Perhaps it was as well; once a week at least the household was driven, by mutual insult, into order. After lunch cook and housemaid vanished. Two carefully dressed gentlemen—who had the air of men who would rather have stayed in aprons—were left alone to receive their guests.

Our company came partly from Dan’s journalistic acquaintance, partly from my theatre friends. Occasionally Minikin and Jarman appeared; even Mrs. Peedles once or twice came breathless up our stairs. Left to myself I should not, perhaps, have invited them to mingle with the rest; they belonged to a different London—one that smelt less of cut flowers and more of honest damp and lodging-house stair carpets. But Dan, once introduced, insisted.

“My dear Lord Chamberlain,” Dan would reply when I hinted timidly at “fitness,” “an ounce of originality is worth a ton of convention. Little tin ladies and gentlemen stamped out to pattern—one can find them everywhere. Your friends would be an acquisition to any society.”

“But are they quite—good form?” I ventured.

“I’ll tell you what we will do,” said Dan. “We will forget that Mrs. Peedles keeps a lodging-house in Blackfriars. We will speak of her as our friend, ‘that dear, quaint old creature, Lady P.’ A title that is an oddity, whose costume always suggests the wardrobe of a provincial actress! My dear Paul, your society novelist would make a fortune out of her. Her anecdotes, instead of being about third-rate theatrical folk, shall concern Earl Blank and the Baroness de Dash. Society journals shall pay me a shilling a line. Jarman—yes, Jarman shall be the son of a South American millionaire. Vulgar? Nonsense! you mean racy. Minikin—who looks much more like forty than twenty—shall be an eminent scientist. His head will then appear natural size; his glass eye can be the result of a chemical experiment; his uncompromising rudeness will become a lovable peculiarity. We’ll put a yard of red ribbon across his shirt-front and call him Herr Professor. It will explain his English and his accent. They shall be our lions. We will invite commonplace people to meet them.”

And to my terror Dan did it.

Jarman entered into the spirit of the joke as though he had been born into it. I am convinced that, so far as he was concerned, our guests were deceived from beginning to end. The more he swaggered, the more he boasted, the more he talked about himself—the greater became his success. It amused me to see Dan’s journalistic friends trying to excite his cupidity with visions of “new papers” that needed only a couple of thousand pounds to command, by the sheer brilliance of their “ideas,” a circulation of hundreds of thousands. But when I watched my actress friends exert themselves to fascinate him—luring him into corners, gazing at him with languishing eyes, performing all their little tricks for his benefit, quarrelling about him like children over a toy—I began to feel my conscience prick. Jokes, like soufflés, can collapse into a mess if you open the oven door at the wrong moment.

Fortunately Jarman was a gentleman of uncommon sense. He enjoyed his good time while it lasted. Later he married the eldest daughter of a small blacking factory. She was plain, but pleasant; and she brought him, in due course, possession of the factory itself. When I meet him now—stout and rubicund—he gives the impression of a man who has attained his ideals and sits down inside them, comfortable as in an armchair.

Minikin gave us more trouble. Some guests came equipped with scientific smatterings; we had to explain that the Professor never talked shop. Others turned out to know German and insisted on using it; we had to explain that Herr Professor was in London to learn English and had taken a vow neither to speak nor listen to his native tongue during his stay. It was remarked that his acquaintance with English slang was unusually complete for a foreigner. Occasionally he was too rude even for a scientist: informing ladies, clamouring to know how he liked English women, that he didn’t like them silly; telling an important friend of Dan’s, who asked what he got his yard of ribbon for, that he got it for fourpence. We explained him as a man soured by a love disappointment. Ladies forgave him; gentlemen said it was a damned lucky thing for the girl. In short, Minikin took explaining as a rich sauce takes stirring.

Lady Peedles, our guests decided among themselves, must be the widow of somebody in the City who had been knighted in a crowd. They made fun of her behind her back but were effusive to her face. “My dear Lady Peedles” became the refrain whenever she was present. At the theatre “my friend Lady Peedles” was much spoken of—generally in loud tones. My own social position improved because her ladyship liked me. It went abroad that I was her presumptive heir. I was courted as a gentleman of expectations, which is a title that costs nothing but earns you many smiles.

The fishy-eyed young man became a regular visitor. Dan won his heart by never laughing at him.

“I like talking to you,” he said one afternoon to Dan. “You don’t go into fits of laughter when I remark it’s been a fine day; most people do. On the stage I don’t mind. I know I’m a funny little devil. I get my living by being a funny little devil. There’s a photograph of me in the lobby. I saw a workman stop and look at it the other day; I was just behind him. He burst into a roar. ‘Little—! He makes me laugh to look at him!’ he cluttered to himself. Well, that’s all right. I want the man in the gallery to think me funny, but it annoys me when people laugh at me off the stage. If I’m out to dinner and ask somebody to pass the mustard, I never get it; instead they burst out laughing. I don’t want people to laugh at me when I’m having my dinner. I want my dinner. It makes me very angry sometimes.”

“I know,” said Dan sympathetically. “The world never grasps that a man is a collection, not a single exhibit. I remember being at a house once where the chief guest happened to be a great Hebrew scholar. One tea time a Miss Henman, passing the butter in a hurry, let it slip. ‘Why is Miss Henman like a caterpillar?’ asked our learned guest in a sepulchral voice. Nobody knew. ‘Because she makes the butter fly.’ It never occurred to any one of us he could joke. Dead silence for a minute. Then our hostess, grave as a judge, remarked, ‘Oh, do you really think so?’”

“If I were to enter a room full of people,” said the little man, “and tell them my mother had been run over by an omnibus, they’d think it the funniest story they’d heard in years.”

He was playing a principal part now in the opera, and he undoubtedly drew the house. Yet he was not happy.

“I’m not a comic actor, really,” he explained. “I could play Romeo, so far as feeling is concerned, and play it damned well. There’s a fine vein of poetry in me. But of course it’s no good with this face of mine.”

“But are you not sinning your mercies?” Dan retorted. “There’s young Kelver here. At school his trouble was he could give us a good time and make us laugh, which nobody else could do. His ambition was to kick a ball as well as a hundred other fellows could kick it. He could tell us a good story now if he’d only write what the Almighty intended him to write, instead of gloomy rigmaroles about suffering Princesses in Welsh caves. I don’t say it’s bad, but a hundred others could write the same sort of thing better.”

“Can’t you understand,” said the little man, “the poorest tragedian that ever lived never wished himself the best of low comedians. The court fool had an excellent salary, no doubt, and likely enough had got two-thirds of the brain in the palace. But not a wooden-headed man-at-arms but looked down on him. Every gallery boy who pays a shilling to laugh at me regards himself as my intellectual superior; while to a fourth-rate spouter of blank verse he looks up in admiration.”

“Does it matter so very much,” suggested Dan, “how the wooden-headed man-at-arms or the shilling gallery boy happens to regard you?”

“Yes, it does,” snapped Goggles, “because we happen to agree with them. If I could earn five pounds a week as juvenile lead, I’d never play a comic part again.”

“There I can’t follow you,” said Dan. “I can understand the artist who would rather be the man of action, the poet who would rather be the statesman or the warrior—though my sympathies are the other way, with Wolfe who thought it more glorious to write a great poem than to burn so many cities and kill so many men. We all serve the community. Some feed it, some clothe it. The churchman and the policeman look after its morals and keep it in order. The doctor mends it; the lawyer helps it to quarrel; the soldier teaches it to fight. We Bohemians amuse it, instruct it. We can argue we’re the most important: the others cater for the body, we for the mind. But their work is showier and attracts more attention—and attracting attention is the aim and object of most of us. For Bohemians to quarrel among themselves which is greatest is without reason. The story-teller, the musician, the artist, the clown—we’re members of a sharing troupe; one makes the people’s flesh creep; another makes them hold their sides with laughter. The tragedian, soliloquising on his crimes, shows us how wicked we are; you, looking at lovers from under a scratch wig, show us how ridiculous we are. Both lessons are necessary. Who shall say which is the superior teacher?”

“Ah, I’m not a philosopher,” sighed the little man.

“Ah,” sighed Dan, “and I’m not a comic actor on my way to a salary of a hundred a week. We all want the other boy’s cake.”

The O’Kelly became another of our frequent visitors. The attic in Belsize Square had been closed. In vain had the O’Kelly wafted incense, burned pastilles, sprinkled eau-de-Cologne. In vain had he talked of rats and hinted at drains.

“A wonderful woman,” groaned the O’Kelly, in tones of sorrowful admiration. “There’s no deceiving her.”

“But why submit?” we argued. “Why not say you are going to smoke, and do it?”

“It’s her theory, me boy,” he explained, “that the home should be kept pure—a sort of a temple, ye know. She’s convinced it’s bound in time to exercise an influence upon me. It’s a beautiful idea, when ye come to think of it.”

Meanwhile, in the rooms of half-a-dozen sinful men the O’Kelly kept his own particular pipe and his own particular smoking mixture; and one pipe and one tobacco jar stood always on our mantelpiece like contraband displayed with shameless honesty.

In the spring the forces of temptation raged round that feeble but excellently intentioned citadel—the O’Kelly’s conscience. The Signora had returned to England and was performing then at Ashley’s Theatre. The O’Kelly would sit for long spells of silence, puffing vigorously, as if to smother thought in smoke. Or he would fortify himself with paeans in praise of Mrs. O’Kelly.

“If anything could ever make a model man of me”—he spoke in the tones of one whose doubts were stronger than his hopes—“it would be the example of that woman.”

It was a Saturday afternoon; I had just returned from the matinee.

“I don’t believe,” he continued, “I don’t really believe she has ever done one single thing she oughtn’t to, or left undone one single thing she ought, in the whole course of her life.”

“Maybe she has, and you don’t know of it,” I suggested, thinking the notion might ease him.

“I wish I could think so,” he answered. “I don’t mean anything really wrong,” he corrected himself quickly, “but something just a little wrong. I feel—I really feel I should like her better if she had.”

“Not that I mean I don’t like her as it is, ye understand,” he corrected himself again. “I respect that woman—I cannot tell ye, me boy, how much I respect her. Ye don’t know her. There was one morning, about a month ago. That woman—she’s down at six every morning, summer and winter; we have prayers at half-past. I was a trifle late meself: early rising was never me strong point, as ye know. Seven o’clock struck; she didn’t appear, and I thought she had overslept. I won’t say I didn’t feel pleased for the moment; it was an unworthy sentiment, but I almost wished she had. I ran up to her room. The door was open, the bedclothes folded down as she always leaves them. She came in five minutes later. She had got up at four that morning to welcome a troupe of native missionaries from East Africa on their arrival at Waterloo Station. She’s a saint, that woman; I am not worthy of her.”

“I shouldn’t dwell too much on that phase of the subject,” I said.

“I can’t help it, me boy. I feel I am not.”

“I don’t for a moment say you are,” I returned; “but I wouldn’t harp on it. It’s not good for you.”

“I never will be,” he persisted, gloomily. “Never!”

He was on a dangerous train of reflection. To shunt him, I led the talk to champagne.

“Most people like it dry,” he admitted. “Meself, I’ve always preferred it with just a suggestion of fruitiness.”

“There was a champagne,” I said, “you used to be rather fond of—years ago.”

“I think I know the one ye mean,” he said. “It wasn’t at all bad, considering the price.”

“You don’t happen to remember where you got it?”

“It was in Bridge Street,” he recalled, “not so very far from the Circus.”

“It’s a pleasant evening,” I remarked. “Let us take a walk.”

We found the place—half wine-shop, half office—with the air of somewhere that did business by day and conscience by night.

“Just the same,” commented the O’Kelly as we pushed in. “Not altered a bit.”

As barely twelve months had likely elapsed since his last visit, the miracle of stability was not extraordinary; but clearly the O’Kelly was counting time by sensation. I ordered a bottle and we sat. Being prejudiced against the brand, I called for claret. The O’Kelly finished the bottle. The virtue of that wine had not departed. With every glass he became morally more elevated, as though bubbles rose not only in the drink but in his resolutions.

He left the place determined to be worthy of Mrs. O’Kelly. Walking down the Embankment, he asserted he would buy an alarm-clock that very evening. At Westminster Bridge he fell suddenly silent. Looking to discover why, I saw his eyes fixed on a poster representing a charming lady standing on one leg upon a wire, with mountain peaks beneath her; the artist had even caught the likeness. I cursed our luck; then, lacking experience, felt reassured.

“Me dear Paul,” said the O’Kelly, laying a fatherly hand on my shoulder, “there are fair-faced, laughing women—sweet creatures, that ye want to put yer arm around and dance with.” He shook his head disapprovingly. “There are the sainted women, who lead us up, Paul—up, always up.”

A look, such as the young man with the banner might have carried to fields of snow and ice, suffused his handsome face. Without another word he crossed the road and entered an American store, where for six-and-elevenpence he purchased an alarm-clock the man assured us would awake an Egyptian mummy. With this in his hand he waved me good-bye, jumped on a Hampstead ’bus, and I strolled alone towards the theatre, reflecting that modern civilisation can sell you machinery for waking, but cannot provide the corresponding mechanism for going to bed.

Hal returned a little after Christmas and set himself up in chambers in the City—the nearest, so he said, that he dared venture to civilisation.

“I’d be no good in the West End,” he explained. “For a season I might attract as an eccentricity, but your swells would never stand me longer—no more would any respectable folk, anywhere. We don’t get on together. I commenced at Richmond. It was fashionable then, and I thought I was going to do wonders. I had everything in my favour, except myself. I do know my work; nobody can deny that. My father spent every penny he had, poor gentleman, in buying me an old-established practice: fine house, carriage and pair, white-haired butler—everything correct, except myself. It was no use. I can hold myself in a month or two; then I break out, the old original savage that I am under my frock coat. I feel I must run amuck, stabbing, hacking at the prim, smiling Lies mincing round about me.

“I can fool a silly woman for half-a-dozen visits—bow and rub my hands, purr round her sympathetically. All the while I’m longing to tell her the truth:

“‘Go home. Wash your face; don’t block up the pores of your skin with paint. Let out your corsets. You’re thirty-three round the abdomen if you’re an inch: how do you expect digestion to do its work when you’ve squeezed it into twenty-one? Give up gadding about half the day and most of the night. You’re old enough to have done with that. Let the children come, and suckle them yourself. You’ll be the better for them. Don’t loll in bed all the morning. Get up like a decent animal and do something for your living. Use your brain—what there is of it—and your body. At that price you can have health to-morrow, and at no other. I can do nothing for you.’

“And sooner or later I blurt it out.” He laughed his great roar. “Lord! you should see the real face coming out of the simpering mask.

“Pompous old fools strutting into me like turkey-cocks! By Jove, it was worth it! They’d dribble out looking half their proper size after I’d done telling them what was the matter with them.

“‘Do you want to know what you’re really suffering from?’ I’d shout when I could contain myself no longer. ‘Gluttony, my dear sir; gluttony and drunkenness and over-indulgence in other vices that shall be nameless. Live like a man. Get a little self-respect from somewhere. Give up being an ape. Treat your body properly and it will treat you properly. That’s the only prescription that’ll do you any good.’”

He laughed again. “‘Tell the truth, you shame the Devil.’ But the Devil replies by starving you. It’s a fairly effective retort. I’m not the stuff successful family physicians are made of. In the City I may rub along. One doesn’t see so much of one’s patients; they come and go. Clerks and warehousemen chiefly. The poor man doesn’t mind being told the truth about himself; it’s a blessing he’s accustomed to.”

We spoke but once of Barbara. Her photograph, in her bride’s dress, stood upon my desk. Occasionally—first fitting the room for the ceremony, sweeping away all impurity even from under the mats, and dressing myself with care—I would set it amid flowers, and kneeling, kiss her hand where it rested on the back of that absurd, top-heavy chair without which no photographic studio seems complete.

One day Hal took it up and looked at it long and hard.

I went out of London with martial music in my ears, the sort you can’t quite tell whether it’s a band or merely your own vanity marching up and down your ribs. A little more than a year later—wet December, fog that tasted of old pennies and river-mud—I came back the way a cat comes back to a scullery: low, quick, and hoping nobody notices the smell.

Defeat is a thing you can repair with work. Disgrace has the awkward habit of sticking like burnt sugar to the bottom of the pan. I returned to London as to a crowd thick enough to pickle a man in anonymity, and I intended—if I am being honest—to waste away there until I had courage for some final, quiet stupidity.

I had been ambitious. I write “had,” as if ambition were a hat one mislaid in a railway carriage and never claimed again. I had wanted a name, a place, and not merely the admiration of the world, but its respect; the sort of respect you imagine is served to you on a clean plate, with a sprig of honour laid across it like parsley. I had meant to be Galahad; I discovered I was—when the heat came on and the fat began to spit—a very poor cook of my own virtues, a man who turned cowardly the moment temptation cracked its whip.

The first sour ingredient in the mess was Urban Vane.

I discovered, not with any dramatic thunderclap, but with the slow certainty of a food historian finding a modern label pasted over an older one, that Vane was a common swindler. The play we had been touring—our precious “Drama, in five acts, by Mr. Horace Moncrieff”—had been stolen from the desk of a well-known dramatist whose acquaintance Vane had made in Deleglise’s kitchen.

Deleglise’s kitchen—yes, that kitchen again, with its clatter of pans and the singing hiss when stock meets a hot stove, that honest orchestra of labour. How absurd that theft should begin in a place where everything is weighed and tasted and watched, where you can tell who has been at the sauce because the spoon lies a quarter-inch wrong. Yet there Vane had insinuated himself, visiting the dramatist while he was ill, sitting by his bed with the patience of a man waiting for a joint to cool so he can carve it without losing the juices. The poor fellow partially recovered, went off to Italy, and was expected—so we all said, with that theatre cheerfulness that calls death “a bad turn”—to die there. Had he done so, the robbery might have passed unchallenged, like a stale joke repeated in a new town.

But word came to us, in some small northern place where rain sounded like thrown peas on window-glass, that he had taken a fresh lease of life and was on his way back to England.

That night, Vane invited me to a bottle of wine and a cigar, as if we were going to discuss lighting cues and royalties. He told me the bald truth with the airy wit of a man sprinkling pepper over somebody else’s dinner. Six months of companionship with Urban Vane had done what damp does to decent bread: if it hadn’t destroyed the roots of what manhood I had, it had certainly made it soft and liable to mould.

At first I stormed. Vane sat and listened, amused, as though I were a kettle obligingly coming to the boil.

“Your language, my dear Kelver,” he said at last, when my stock of invective had simmered down to a thin gruel, “might wound me were I able to accept you as an authority upon this vexed question of morals. With the rest of the world you preach one thing and practise another. I have noticed it so often. The preaching has ceased to interest me.

“You profess to be indignant with me for making use of another man’s ideas. It is done every day. You yourself were quite ready to take credit not due to you. For months we have been travelling with this play: ‘Drama, in five acts, by Mr. Horace Moncrieff.’ Not more than two hundred lines of it are your own—excellent lines, I admit, but they do not constitute the play.”

The thought struck me like the smell of something left too long on the stove: sharp, undeniable, humiliating.

“But you asked me to put my name to it,” I stammered. “You said you didn’t want yours to appear—for private reasons. You made a point of it.”

He waved cigar-smoke aside as if it were a trivial steam from a saucepan. “The man you are posing as would never have put his name to work not his own. You never hesitated; on the contrary, you jumped at the chance of so easy an opening to your career as playwright. My need, as you imagined it, was your opportunity.”

“But you said it was from the French,” I insisted, clinging like a bad cook to a bad recipe. “You’d merely translated it; I adapted it. I don’t defend the custom—but it is the custom. The man who adapts a play calls himself the author.”

“I know,” he said. “It has always amused me. Our sick friend himself—whom I am sure we are both delighted to welcome back to life—has done it more than once, and made a fair profit. Indeed, from internal evidence I am strongly of opinion that this present play is a case in point. Well, chickens come home to roost: I adapt from him. What is the difference?”

He poured himself more wine with the calm of a man salting his own dish. “Simply this: that whereas, owing to the anomalous state of copyright laws, stealing from the foreign author is legal and commendable, against stealing from the living English author there is a certain prejudice.”

“And the consequences,” I ventured, “may be unpleasant.”

He laughed—a rare sound from him, and all the more disquieting for being genuine. “You mean, my dear Kelver, that you will.”

I stared. He leaned forward, enjoying my confusion the way some men enjoy listening for the snap of a crust they know they’ve overbaked.

“Don’t look so dumbfounded. You cannot be so stupid as you are pretending. The original manuscript at the Lord Chamberlain’s office is in your handwriting. You knew our friend as well as I did, and visited him. Why, the whole tour has been under your management. You have arranged everything—most excellently; I have been quite surprised.”

In that moment my anger was too far away to reach. Fear took up all the room, like a horrible industrial noise that drowns out every softer sound in the mind. I could hear my own blood battering at my ears, as if some unseen drummer had set up in my skull.

“But you told me,” I began. “It was only a matter of form—that you wanted to keep your name out of it because—”

He looked at me with real astonishment, and my own words began to sound absurd, like a sentimental speech delivered over the clatter of plates in a cheap restaurant.

“I am sorry,” he said. “Really sorry. I took you for a man of the world. I thought you merely did not wish to know anything.”

Still—God forgive me—fear was uppermost.

“You’re not going to put it all on to me?” I said, and I hate myself for the pleading.

He rose and laid his hand on my shoulder. Instead of knocking it away, I was grateful for its pressure, as a man in a loud, crowded street is grateful for any touch that seems human.

“Don’t take it so seriously,” he said. “He will merely think the manuscript has been lost. As likely as not he will be unable to remember whether he wrote it or merely thought of writing it. No one in the company will say anything: it isn’t their business.

“We must set to work. I altered it a good deal before you saw it, and changed all the names. We will retain the third act: it is the only thing of real value. The situation is not original; you have as much right to dish it up as he had. In a fortnight we will have the whole thing so different that if he saw it himself he would only imagine we had got hold of the idea and forestalled him.”

Dish it up. That phrase—culinary, domestic—made the crime sound as homely as reheated stew. And perhaps that is how evil gets you: not with horns and sulphur, but with the tone you use when you explain how to use yesterday’s bones for today’s broth.

There were moments in those weeks when my better self—my good angel, if you like old names—spoke plainly. I should go to the dramatist and tell him the truth. I could see it as clearly as a recipe written in honest ink.

But Vane never left my elbow. If I confessed, he hinted, he would deny me, and my tale would be treated as the frightened invention of a young rogue trying to wriggle off a hook. At best I should wear suspicion like a greasy apron for the rest of my career. At worst—well, there are reputations that are murdered in broad daylight and nobody calls it a crime.

Besides, what harm had we done? So Vane argued, and I, already softened, let the arguments enter. A play presented in twenty little towns, soon forgotten. Most plays were “something like.” Our friend would produce his version and reap a rich harvest; ours would vanish. If there was talk, it would advertise him. We’d replace it quickly with a new piece. A young man of my genius could surely write something better than this hotch-potch; all I lacked was experience. And as for conscience—wasn’t “honesty” merely a convention, a word in a dominant lexicon used to police the weak? The world’s real commandment was not “Be good,” but “Don’t get found out.”

So I fell—less like a man falling heroically from a cliff, more like a thing sinking in thick mud, inch by inch.

We worked. Feverishly. Vane had brains; I had diligence and the sort of imagination that can be bent to any purpose, like a good knife in the wrong hand. I tried to persuade him to let me write a new play altogether, offering to give it him for nothing—anything to escape the taste of theft that was always in my mouth, bitter as burnt coffee. He thanked me warmly but declined, his professed faith in my talent never quite robust enough to risk his own profits.

“Later on, my dear Kelver,” he said. “For the present this is doing very well. Going on as we are, we shall soon improve it out of all recognition, while at the same time losing nothing essential. All your ideas are excellent.”

By the end of three weeks we had made a concoction that, in dialogue and character, might pass as our own. Here and there it was good work. In other circumstances I might have been proud. As it was, I felt only the thief’s tight-lipped listening, always expecting the footstep on the stair.

There was one speech in the play—addressed by the hero to the villain—“Yes, I admit it; I do love her. But there is that which I love better—my self-respect!” Our juvenile lead—who in life led a rabbit-like existence, always diving into holes to avoid writs for alimony—would step down to the footlights, slap his chest (stage convention makes the male torso a sort of moral larder crammed with virtues), and bring down the house.

Every night, as the applause rolled over that line like surf over a rock, I would shudder. I remembered writing it with cheeks hot, not from inspiration but from shame—shame that felt like being caught licking the spoon when you’ve been told the pudding is not yours.

There was also a character—a vicious old man—whom Vane had from the beginning wanted me to play. I had refused then, choosing instead a high-souled countryman, the kind of part in which a young fool can preen his virtue in public. Now Vane renewed the argument. My power of resistance had become thin as watered soup. I accepted the exchange.

The old man’s scenes had more snap, and I found, to my further degradation, that I could get a coarse laugh from the audience by giving the role all the ugliness we dared. Under an older actor the effect might have been harmless, but a young man’s nature takes impressions as fresh dough takes a stamp. I found myself enjoying the laughter that rewarded my suggestive leers. I began to call up all the evil in me as if it were a useful spice. Vane praised me, helped me, urged me to make the part nastier. I had genius, he said; I understood human nature. One proof of my deterioration was that I was beginning to like him.

Looking back now, I see him plainly: a very ordinary scamp, his pretensions not even amusing. But then he seemed, to my boyish mind, a superior being by some freak of fate compelled to live among inferiors. He affected a contempt for conventions as though decency were merely a suburban accent. Every decent instinct was, in his vocabulary, “bourgeois,” and therefore unintellectual. It was the first time I had heard that particular dialect—this clever, fashionable language that makes vice sound like art and sincerity sound like a shopkeeper counting coppers.

He talked of Hedonism with the air of discovering a new continent. Pan, coaxed from dark groves, was to sit upon Olympus. There was no evil, only Nature; repression was for dullards; advancement lay through joyous expression of the animal.

My lower nature—always hungry, always eager for permission—rose up and accepted his nonsense as gospel. Boys are impressed by such talk; they do not yet know how often it is merely an excuse for bad manners.

And yet, villains who do wrong for the aesthetic pleasure of it belong more properly to novels than to life. I think Vane believed much of the rubbish he spoke. Since then, I hear, he has turned respectable: an excellent husband and father, a considerate master, a delightful host. I can almost see him now carving a joint with propriety, smiling at his guests, and never once tasting the bitterness he fed to others. I prefer to think of him not as devilish but as foolish. It grants him no grandeur.

Our leading lady was handsome and coarse, over-developed, a creature of loud health and hotter hands than were pleasant. She had begun as a music-hall singer, then married a small tradesman in the south of London. Three or four years before, her Juno-like charms had turned the head of a youthful novelist—refined and sensitive, from whom great things had been expected. He ran away with her and married her; the scandal was still fresh. She had already repented. Such women look on lovers as rungs of a ladder; he had not carried her up as far as she expected.

Under her spell his ambition had collapsed like a soufflé in a draught. He no longer wrote; he no longer cared for anything but his own debasement. He was with us in the company, playing small parts badly, and would have stayed as bill-poster rather than be sent away. It was a domestic tragedy performed nightly in the wings, unheard above the public laughter—the acoustic ecology of misery: the soft, constant drip of a ruined talent, drowned out by clapping.

Vane planned to bring her and me together. To her he represented me as a young gentleman of means, a coming author who would soon earn enough to keep her in luxuries. To me he hinted that she had fallen in love with me. I was never attracted to her by any deeper feeling than one has for a handsome animal. It was my vanity he worked upon: he envied me; any man would envy me; I only needed experience. Great intellects must learn all lessons, even if the lesson involves other people bleeding.

As years earlier I had painfully trained myself to like cigars and whisky—as if bitterness on the tongue were a credential for literature—so I now attempted to cultivate a taste for passion. Vane supplied me with novels and histories in which all “men of note” were sad dogs. That I had neither instinct nor inclination for being a sad dog struck my idiot self as a defect. Her languishing glances irritated rather than inflamed me; her covert pressure of her hot, thick hand left me cold. I felt reproached by my own lack of appetite, as though manhood were measured by what you can swallow.

I would fall in love with her. Surely my blood was as red as other men’s. Besides—was I not an artist, and was not profligacy the hallmark of the artist?

But I grow tired of confession, and Fate—who sometimes acts like a brusque, practical cook snatching your hand away from the flame—saved me from completing the part Vane had written for me in that vulgar comedy. She was rough, but the mud was creeping upward fast, and a quick hand must needs be rough.

Our dramatic friend—the one who had actually written the stolen play—produced his version sooner than we expected. It crept out that something very like it had been seen in the provinces. Talk began, and enquiries. Vane said it would blow over. But the wind seemed to be turning our way, and it carried with it an ominous sound, like distant carts on a cobbled road: the approaching rumble of consequences.

As a rule the salaries were paid by me on Friday night. Vane would bring me the money during the evening for me to distribute after the performance. We were in the north of Ireland. I had not seen Vane all day. As soon as I had changed, I went to look for him. The box-office keeper met me and put a note in my hand.

It was short, neat, and to the point—like the slash of a knife across a string.

Vane had pocketed the evening’s takings and left by the seven-fifty train. He regretted the inconvenience; life was full of little comedies; the wise man attached no seriousness to them. We should probably meet again and laugh over our experiences.

I looked up from the letter to find myself ringed by faces—hard, suspicious, suddenly hungry. Rumour had gone ahead of the truth, as it always does. With dry lips I told them what had happened. They did not believe me. Vane had contrived that, to them, I was the manager, the responsible man. My tired explanations sounded like lies, and perhaps they were: not lies of fact, but lies of character—because my character had indeed agreed to this mess.

They talked of giving me “a damned good hiding.” I doubt whether I would have known by morning if they had. A dull apathy had settled on me; the whole scene felt stupid, ridiculous, like some farce played too long. It dawned on me that they had known, all along, the true history of the play, the reason for the sudden alterations. They had saved their virtuous indignation for the moment it could be most useful to themselves.

Someone suggested I was drunk—whether kindly or not, I cannot tell. It was accepted. No further trains left the town that night. They allowed me to go, with a promise that a deputation would visit my lodgings early in the morning.

Our leading lady had left the theatre the moment the curtain fell; she did not need to wait, her husband acting as her business man. When I reached my rooms, I found her sitting by the fire.

It reminded me that our agent in advance had fallen ill, and her husband, at her suggestion, had been appointed in his place, leaving on Wednesday to prepare the next town. I supposed she had come for money; the thought amused me, in a weary way.

“Well?” she said, with her one smile. I had been labouring for months to see it as soul-consuming, but it never quite succeeded.

“Well,” I said.

“You don’t seem overjoyed to see me. What’s happened?”

I laughed. “Vane’s bolted and taken the week’s money with him.”

“The beast!” she said. “I knew he was that sort. What ever made you take up with him? Will it make much difference to you?”

“It makes a difference all round,” I said. “There’s no money to pay any of you. There’s nothing to pay your fares back to London.”

She rose. “Here, let me understand. Are you the rich mug Vane’s been representing you to be, or only his accomplice?”

“The mug and the accomplice both,” I answered, “without the rich. It’s his tour. He put my name to it because he didn’t want his to appear—for family reasons. It’s his play; he stole it—”

She whistled. “I thought it looked a bit fishy, all those alterations. But funny things do happen in this profession! Stole it, did he?”

“The whole thing. I put my name to it for the same reason.”

She dropped back into her chair and laughed—loud, long, good-tempered. “Well, I’m damned!” she said. “The first man who ever took me in. I should never have signed if I’d thought it was his show. I could see the sort he was with half an eye.”

She sprang up. “Here, let me get out of this. I just looked in to know what time to-morrow; I’d forgotten. You needn’t say I came.”

Her hand on the door, she was seized again with laughter and had to lean against the wall.

“Do you know why I really did come?” she said. “You’ll guess when you come to think it over, so I may as well tell you. It’s a bit of a joke. I came to say ‘yes’ to what you asked me last night. Have you forgotten?”

I stared at her. Last night felt years away, like a meal eaten in another life.

She laughed again. “So help me, if you haven’t. Well, you asked me to run away with you—that’s all, to let our two souls unite. Damned lucky I took a day to think it over! Good-night.”

“Good-night,” I said, without moving. I gripped a chair because a furious impulse came over me to thrust her out and lock the door. Not from desire—Heaven knows—but from the intolerable noise of other people in my misery. I wanted silence. I wanted to hear nothing but my own thoughts, and even those were too loud.

I heard her turn the handle.

“Got a pound or two to carry you over?” The voice was purely practical now; women can do that turn as quickly as snapping shut a cupboard door.

I put my hand in my pocket. “One pound seventeen,” I said, counting it. “It will pay my fare to London—or buy me a dinner and a second-hand revolver. I haven’t quite decided yet.”

“Oh, you get back and pull yourself together,” she said. “You’re only a kid. Good-night.”

I put a few things into a small bag and walked thirty miles that night into Belfast. There is a particular sound to walking in the dark with disgrace for company: your own footsteps, too loud; the wind in hedges, whispering like people at the back of a theatre; and, far off, the steady industrial hum of a world that does not care whether you live or die, only that you do not impede the traffic.

In London I took lodgings in Deptford, where I was least likely to meet any face from the old life. I kept myself alive by giving singing lessons at sixpence the half-hour, evening lessons in French and German—may the Lord forgive me—to ambitious shop-boys at eighteenpence a week, and by making up tradesmen’s books. A few pieces of jewellery I had retained helped over bad patches. For four months I existed there and hardly went beyond the neighbourhood.

The streets in that part of London have their own cuisine of odours: frying fat that never quite freshens, damp bricks, coal smoke, stale beer, and the river’s low, mouldy breath. At night there were other sounds—shouting, cart-wheels, the clink of bottles, the thin crying of infants, the harsh, mechanical laughter of men who have forgotten what joy sounds like. The sonic landscape pressed on the nerves like constant pounding on a door. It is difficult to be good in a place where quiet has died.

Sometimes, wandering listlessly, an object or a vista would strike me as familiar—as if memory were a smell caught unexpectedly in a cold alley: for an instant vivid, then gone. Then I would turn and hurry back into my dim, weltering grave of streets.

Of thoughts and emotions during those days I seemed unconscious. Somewhere in me they may have been stirring and contending; but I lived as in a long, dull dream. I ate and drank and woke and slept and walked and walked, and lounged at corners staring for hours, seeing nothing.

It surprises me now how clearly the mind can photograph what the heart refuses to feel. Tragedies, dramas, farces were played before me in that teeming underworld—domestic rows, drunken reconciliations, children bargaining for crusts, women haggling over scraps as if they were relics of a lost language of plenty. They stand in my memory complete and distinct; yet I have no recollection of ever having seen them at the time, as if my eyes were open only to the spectacle and not to the meaning, and as if the meaning, like a dialect nobody speaks any more, had died in my mouth.

It took me three years to win that handshake; and I earned it, odd to say, with nothing more romantic than ink, shoe-leather, and the sort of laughter that can be fried up out of anything if you keep the pan hot.

For the first six months I remained in Deptford, where the river-smell—tar, mud, wet rope, stale ale—clung to your clothes like an opinion. There was good stuff there for any writer: tragedy by the yard, pathos by the pint, humour in cracked teacups and broken promises. But I owed a hundred and fifty pounds—over two hundred, once I stopped pretending arithmetic was a rumour. So I laid the melancholy aside for other people to pickle, and I stuck to my own trade: I harvested laughter, bundled it, and took it to market.

At the beginning I sent each manuscript to Norah. She copied it out—neat as a recipe transcribed by a cook who means you to succeed—debited me with the cost, collected payment, and sent me the balance. My earnings began as the merest scrapings, the sort of thin broth you could see your childhood through; but Norah had the appetite and the nerve of a born caterer. Rapidly, she made my little jokes fetch real money.

Dan grew quite cross with her, wrote in pained surprise at her greed. The “matter” was fair, he said, but in no way remarkable. Any friend of hers, of course, he was anxious to assist; but business was business. Miss Deleglise, replying curtly in the third person, found herself in perfect accord with Mr. Brian upon that very point. If Mr. Brian could not afford Miss Deleglise’s price for material so excellent, other editors—whom Miss Deleglise was equally well acquainted with—could and would. Answer by return would greatly oblige; pending which the manuscript then in her hands she retained.

Mr. Brian, understanding he had found his match, grumbled but paid. Whether he suspected who “Jack Homer” might be he never confessed; but he would have played the game either way. Nor was he allowed to decide the question for himself. Competition was introduced—deliberately, like a dash of mustard in a sauce to prevent it going flat. A certain proportion of my work my agent sent elsewhere. “Jack Homer” grew to be a commodity in demand. For there, seated at my rickety table, I laughed as I wrote, and the fourth wall of the dismal room would fade—like steam lifting off a saucepan—to show me vistas beyond.

Still, it was slow work. Humour is not an industrious maid; she won’t be bustled, will only come into the kitchen when it pleases her, gives herself a thousand airs, and if you scold her she tosses her head and runs out into the street. She comes back when she chooses and expects you to be grateful. So to my literary labours I added journalism.

I lacked Dan’s magnificent assurance. Fate never befriends the nervous. Had I burst into an editorial sanctum, the editor would assuredly have been out; if in, he would have been a man of short ways and would have seen to it I went out quickly. Besides, the idea of me bursting anywhere in my one coat was absurd. Robert Macaire himself would have been apologetic in it.

So I joined the ranks of the penny-a-liners—to be literally exact, three-halfpence-a-liners. In company with half a dozen other shabby outsiders—some young men seeking to climb; others older men who had slipped and were still sliding—I attended inquests and police courts; flew after fire engines; rejoiced in street accidents; yearned for murders. Somewhat vulture-like, we lived on misfortunes, picking at them with blunt pencils. We made occasional half-crowns by giving the public scandal, occasional crowns by keeping our information to ourselves.

“I think, gentlemen,” our spokesman would whisper hoarsely on returning to our table, “I think the corpse’s brother-in-law is anxious that the affair, if possible, should be kept out of the papers.”

The closeness with which we would then follow that case, the fulness and completeness of our notes, became exemplary. Our spokesman would rise, drift away as if he were merely stretching his legs, and return five minutes later wiping his mouth.

“Not a very interesting case, gentlemen, I don’t think. Shall we say five shillings apiece?” Sometimes the dignity of our calling induced us to stand out for ten.

And here my sense of humour helped me, perhaps giving me an unfair advantage. Twelve good men and true were asked to say how a Lascar sailor met his death. It was perfectly clear: a plumber, working on the roof of a small two-storeyed house, slipped and fell on him. The plumber got off with bruises; the sailor was picked up dead. Some blame attached to the plumber, and his mate—an excellent witness, with the sort of honest voice that rings like a spoon against a mug—told the whole story.

“I was fixing a gas-pipe on the first floor,” said the man. “The prisoner was on the roof.”

“We won’t call him ‘the prisoner,’” interrupted the coroner. “At least, not yet. Refer to him, if you please, as the ‘last witness.’”

“The last witness,” corrected the man, obedient as a lad being taught the proper name for a cut of meat. “He shouts down the chimney to know if I was ready for him.

“‘Ready and waiting,’ I says.

“‘Right,’ he says; ‘I’m coming in through the window.’

“‘Wait a bit,’ I says; ‘I’ll go down and move the ladder for you.’

“‘It’s all right,’ he says; ‘I can reach it.’

“‘No, you can’t,’ I says. ‘It’s the other side of the chimney.’

“‘I can get round,’ he says.

“Well, before I knew what had happened, I hears him go, smack! I rushes to the window and looks out: I see him on the pavement, sitting up like.

“‘Hullo, Jim,’ I says. ‘Have you hurt yourself?’

“‘I think I’m all right,’ he says, ‘as far as I can tell. But I wish you’d come down. This bloke I’ve fallen on looks a bit sick.’”

The other reporters headed their flimsy “Sad Accident,” which was truthful but as tempting as cold suet. I altered mine to “Plumber in a Hurry—Fatal Result,” and, saying as little as possible about the unfortunate sailor, called the attention of plumbers generally to the coroner’s very just remarks upon the folly of undue haste; pointed out to them, as a body, the trouble that would arise if somehow they could not cure themselves of this tendency to rush through their work without a moment’s loss of time.

It established for me a useful reputation. The sub-editor of one evening paper condescended to come out in his shirt-sleeves and shake hands with me.

“That’s the sort of thing we want,” he told me; “a light touch, a bit of humour.”

From then on I snatched fun from fires (I sincerely trust the insurance premiums were not overdue), culled quaintness from street rows, extracted merriment from catastrophes most painful—and prospered. The city’s soundscape became my larder: the clang of the engine-bell, the shriek of a whistle, the rattle of wheels over cobbles, the chorus of voices at a curb—all of it chopped, tasted, seasoned, and served up before it cooled. But it was curious: the louder London became, the more it seemed to demand that its life be translated into brisk, standard phrases for print. The individual note—the private accent of grief, the unrepeatable inflection of a man saying “smack!”—was always being flattened into the dominant lexicon of headlines. I made my living doing the flattening, while privately loving the bumps.

Though often within a stone’s throw of the street, I unremittingly avoided the old house at Poplar. I was suffering inconvenience by reason of being two distinct individuals, contending with each other. My object was to encourage the new Paul—the sensible, practical, pushful Paul whose career began to look promising; to drive away his strangely unlike twin—the old childish Paul of the sad, far-seeing eyes.

Sometimes out of the cracked looking-glass his wistful, yearning face would plead to me, but I would sternly shake my head. I knew well his cunning. Had I let him have his way, he would have led me through the maze of streets he knew so well, past the broken railings (outside which he would have left my body standing), along the weedy pathway, through the cracked and dented door, up the creaking staircase to the dismal little chamber where we once—he and I together—had sat dreaming foolish dreams.

“Come,” he would whisper; “it is so near. Let us push aside the chest of drawers very quietly, softly raise the broken sash, prop it open with the Latin dictionary, lean our elbows on the sill, listen to the voices of the weary city, voices calling to us from the darkness.”

There was always that sound in it—the city at night, never quite silent, never quite speaking your name—like a kettle that refuses to boil yet insists on simmering. But I was too wary to be caught.

“Later on,” I would reply; “when I have made my way, when I am stronger to withstand your wheedling. Then I will go with you, if you are still in existence, my sentimental little friend. We will dream again the old impractical, foolish dreams—and laugh at them.”

So he would fade away, and in his place would nod to me approvingly a businesslike-looking, wide-awake young fellow.

Yet to one sentimental temptation I succumbed. My position was by now assured; there was no longer any reason for hiding myself. I determined to move westward. I had not intended to soar so high, but passing through Guildford Street one day, the creeper-covered corner house my father had once thought of taking came back to me with the plain certainty of a remembered flavour.

A card was in the fanlight. I knocked and made enquiries. A bed-sitting-room upon the third floor was vacant. I remembered it the moment the loquacious landlady opened its door.

“This shall be your room, Paul,” said my father.

So clearly his voice sounded behind me that I turned, forgetting for the moment it was only memory. “You will be quiet here, and we can shut out the bed and washstand with a screen.”

So my father had his way. It was a pleasant, sunny little room, overlooking the gardens of the hospital. I followed his suggestion and shut out the bed and washstand with a screen, as if I were separating the raw material of living from the part I meant to show the world. And sometimes, of an evening, it amused me to hear my father turn the handle of the door in my mind.

“How are you getting on—all right?”

“Famously.”

Often there came back to me words he had once used: “You must be the practical man, Paul, and get on. Myself, I have always been somewhat of a dreamer. I meant to do such great things in the world, and somehow I suppose I aimed too high. I wasn’t—practical.”

“But ought not one to aim high?” I had asked.

My father had fidgeted in his chair. “It is very difficult to say. It is all so—so very ununderstandable. You aim high and you don’t hit anything—at least, it seems as if you didn’t. Perhaps, after all, it is better to aim at something low, and—and hit it. Yet it seems a pity—one’s ideals, all the best part of one—I don’t know why it is. Perhaps we do not understand.”

For some months I had been writing over my own name. One day a letter was forwarded to me by an editor to whose care it had been addressed. It was a short, formal note from the maternal Sellars, inviting me to the wedding of her daughter with a Mr. Reginald Clapper.

I had almost forgotten the incident of the Lady ’Ortensia, but it was not unsatisfactory to learn that it had terminated pleasantly. Also, I judged from an invitation having been sent me that the lady wished me to witness the fact that my desertion had not left her disconsolate. So much gratification I felt I owed her, and accordingly—purchasing a present as expensive as my means would permit—I made my way on the following Thursday, clad in frock coat and light grey trousers, to Kennington Church.

The ceremony was already in progress. Creeping on tiptoe up the aisle, I was about to slip into an empty pew when a hand was laid upon my sleeve.

“We’re all here,” whispered the O’Kelly; “just room for ye.”

Squeezing his hand as I passed, I sat down between the Signora and Mrs. Peedles. Both ladies were weeping: the Signora silently, one tear at a time clinging fondly to her pretty face as though loath to fall; Mrs. Peedles copiously, with explosive gurgles, as of water poured too quickly from a bottle.

“It is such a beautiful service,” murmured the Signora, pressing my hand as I settled myself down. “I should so—so love to be married.”

“Me darling,” whispered the O’Kelly, seizing her other hand and kissing it covertly behind his open Prayer Book, “perhaps ye will be—one day.”

The Signora through her tears smiled at him, but with a sigh shook her head.

Mrs. Peedles—clad, so far as the dim November light enabled me to judge, in the costume of Queen Elizabeth, nothing regal, the sort of thing one might assume to have been Her Majesty’s second-best, say third-best, frock—explained that weddings always reminded her how fleeting a thing was love.

“The poor dears!” she sobbed. “But there, there’s no telling. Perhaps they’ll be happy. I’m sure I hope they may be. He looks harmless.”

Jarman, stretching out a hand to me from the other side of Mrs. Peedles, urged me to cheer up. “Don’t wear your ’eart upon your sleeve,” he advised. “Try and smile.”

In the vestry I met old friends. The maternal Sellars, stouter than ever, had been accommodated with a chair—at least, I assumed so, she being in a sitting posture; the chair itself was not in evidence. She greeted me with more graciousness than I had expected, enquiring after my health with a pointed tenderness that, until the explanation broke upon me, puzzled me.

Mr. Reginald Clapper was a small but energetic gentleman, much impressed, I was glad to notice, with a conviction of his own good fortune. He expressed the greatest delight at being introduced to me, shook me heartily by the hand, and hoped we should always be friends.

“Won’t be my fault if we’re not,” he added. “Come and see us whenever you like.” He repeated this three times. I gathered the general sentiment to be that he was acting, if anything, with excess generosity.

Mrs. Reginald Clapper—as I was relieved to know she now was—received my salute to a subdued murmur of applause. She looked to my eyes handsomer than when I had last seen her, or maybe my taste was growing less exacting. She also trusted she might always regard me as a friend. I replied that it would be my hope to deserve the honour; whereupon she kissed me of her own accord, and embracing her mother, shed some tears, explaining the reason to be that everybody was so good to her.

Brother George, less lank than formerly, hampered by an enormous pair of white kid gloves, superintended my signing of the register, whispering sympathetically: “Better luck next time, old cock.”

The fat young lady—or maybe the lean young lady grown stouter; I could not say for certain—who feared I had forgotten her (a thing I assured her utterly impossible) was good enough to say that in her opinion I was worth all the others put together.

“And so I told her,” added the fat young lady—or the lean one grown stouter, “a dozen times if I told her once. But there!”

I murmured my obligations.

Cousin Joseph, whom I found no difficulty recognising by reason of his watery eyes, appeared not so chirpy as of yore.

“You take my tip,” advised Cousin Joseph, drawing me aside, “and keep out of it.”

“You speak from experience?” I suggested.

“I’m as fond of a joke,” said the watery-eyed Joseph, “as any man. But when it comes to buckets of water—”

A reminder from the maternal Sellars that breakfast had been ordered for eleven o’clock caused a general movement and arrested Joseph’s revelations.

“See you again, perhaps,” he murmured, and pushed past me.

What Mrs. Sellars, I suppose, would have alluded to as a cold col-la-shon had been arranged for at a restaurant near by. I walked there in company with Uncle and Aunt Gutton; not because I particularly desired their companionship, but because Uncle Gutton, seizing me by the arm, left me no alternative.

“Now then, young man,” commenced Uncle Gutton kindly, but boisterously, so soon as we were in the street and a little behind the others, “if you want to pitch into me, you pitch away. I shan’t mind, and maybe it’ll do you good.”

I informed him that nothing was further from my desire.

“Oh, all right,” returned Uncle Gutton, seemingly disappointed. “If you’re willing to forgive and forget, so am I. I never liked you, as I daresay you saw, and so I told Rosie. ‘He may be cleverer than he looks,’ I says, ‘or he may be a bigger fool than I think him, though that’s hardly likely. You take my advice and get a full-grown article, then you’ll know what you’re doing.’”

I told him I thought his advice had been admirable.

“I’m glad you think so,” he returned, puzzled; “though if you wanted to call me names I shouldn’t have blamed you. Anyhow, you’ve took it like a sensible chap. You’ve got over it, as I always told her you would. Young men out of story-books don’t die of broken hearts, even if for a month or two they do feel like standing on their head in the water-butt.”

“Why, I was in love myself three times,” explained Uncle Gutton, “before I married the old woman.”

Aunt Gutton sighed and said she was afraid gentlemen didn’t feel these things as much as they ought to.

“They’ve got their living to earn,” retorted Uncle Gutton.

I agreed that life could not be wasted in vain regret.

“As for the rest,” admitted Uncle Gutton handsomely, “I was wrong. You’ve turned out better than I expected you would.”

I thanked him for his improved opinion, and as we entered the restaurant we shook hands.

Minikin we found there waiting for us. He explained that, having been able to obtain only limited leave of absence from business, he had concluded the time would be better employed at the restaurant than at the church. Others were there also whom I was unacquainted with—young sparks, admirers, I presume, of the Lady ’Ortensia in her professional capacity; fellow-clerks of Mr. Clapper, who was something in the City. Altogether we numbered a score.

Breakfast was laid in a large room on the first floor. The wedding presents stood displayed upon a side-table. My own, with my card attached, had not been seen by Mrs. Clapper till that moment. She and her mother lingered, examining it.

“Real silver!” I heard the maternal Sellars whisper. “Must have paid a ten pound note for it.”

“I hope you’ll find it useful,” I said.

The maternal Sellars, drifting away, joined the others gathered at the opposite end of the room.

“I suppose you think I set my cap at you merely because you were a gentleman,” said the Lady ’Ortensia.

“Don’t let’s talk about it,” I answered. “We were both foolish.”

“I don’t want you to think it was merely that,” continued the Lady ’Ortensia. “I did like you. And I wouldn’t have disgraced you—at least, I’d have tried not to. We women are quick to learn. You never gave me time.”

“Believe me, things are much better as they are,” I said.

“I suppose so,” she answered. “I was a fool.” She glanced round; we still had the corner to ourselves. “I told a rare pack of lies,” she said. “I didn’t seem able to help it; I was feeling sore all over. But I have always been ashamed of myself. I’ll tell them the truth, if you like.”

Even in that babble of restaurant noise—knives clicking, chairs scraping, voices swelling and sinking like tides—there was a different sound in her confession: not theatrical at all, but plain, like a spoon set down carefully.

I thought I saw a way of making her mind easy. “My dear girl,” I said, “you have taken the blame upon yourself, and let me go scot-free. It was generous of you.”

“You mean that?” she asked.

“The truth,” I answered, “would shift all the shame on to me. It was I who broke my word, acted shabbily from beginning to end.”

“I hadn’t looked at it in that light,” she replied. “Very well, I’ll hold my tongue.”

My place at breakfast was to the left of the maternal Sellars, the Signora next to me, and the O’Kelly opposite. Uncle Gutton faced the bride and bridegroom. The disillusioned Joseph was hidden from me by flowers, so that his voice, raised from time to time, fell upon my ears, embellished with the mysterious significance of an unseen oracle.

For the first quarter of an hour or so the meal proceeded almost in silence. The maternal Sellars, when not engaged in whispered argument with the perspiring waiter, was furtively occupied in working sums upon the table-cloth by aid of a blunt pencil, as if balancing a household ledger were more sacred than any blessing. The Signora, strangely unlike her usual self, was not in a talkative mood.

“It was so kind of them to invite me,” said the Signora, speaking low. “But I feel I ought not to have come.”

“Why not?” I asked.

“I’m not fit to be here,” murmured the Signora in a broken voice. “What right have I at wedding breakfasts? Of course, for dear Willie it is different. He has been married.”

The O’Kelly, who never, when the Signora was present, seemed to care much for conversation in which she was unable to participate, took advantage of his neighbour’s being somewhat deaf to lapse into abstraction. Jarman essayed a few witticisms of a general character, of which nobody took any notice. The professional admirers of the Lady ’Ortensia, seated together at a corner of the table, appeared to be enjoying a small joke among themselves; occasionally one or another would laugh nervously, like a man coughing into his napkin.

But for the most part the only sounds were the clatter of knives and forks, the energetic shuffle of the waiter, and a curious hissing noise as of escaping gas, caused by Uncle Gutton drinking champagne.

With the cutting—or rather, the smashing into a hundred fragments—of the wedding cake (a work taxing the united strength of bride and bridegroom to the utmost), the atmosphere lost something of its sombreness. The company, warmed by food and showing signs of being nearly done, commenced to simmer. The maternal Sellars, putting away with her blunt pencil considerations of material nature, embraced the table with a smile.

“But it is a sad thing,” sighed the maternal Sellars the next moment, shaking her huge head, “when your daughter marries, and goes away and leaves you.”

“Damned sight sadder,” commented Uncle Gutton, “when she don’t go off, but hangs on at home year after year and expects you to keep her.”

I credit Uncle Gutton with intending this as an aside for the exclusive benefit of the maternal Sellars; but his voice was not of the timbre that lends itself to secrecy. One of the bridesmaids, a plain, elderly girl, bending over her plate, flushed scarlet. I concluded her to be Miss Gutton.

“It doesn’t seem to me,” said Aunt Gutton from the other end of the table, “that gentlemen are as keen on marrying nowadays as they used to be.”

“Got to know a bit about it, I expect,” sounded the small, shrill voice of the unseen Joseph.

“To my thinking,” exclaimed a hatchet-faced gentleman, “one of the evils crying most loudly for redress at the present moment is the utterly needless and monstrous expense of legal proceedings.” He spoke rapidly and with warmth. “Take divorce. At present, what is it? The rich man’s luxury.”

Conversation appeared to be drifting in a direction unsuitable to the occasion; but Jarman was fortunately there to seize the helm.

“The plain fact of the matter is,” said Jarman, “girls have gone up in value. Time was, so I’ve heard, when they used to be given away with a useful bit of household linen, maybe a chair or two. Nowadays—well, it’s only chaps wallowing in wealth like Clapper there as can afford a really first-class article.”

Mr. Clapper, not a gentleman in other respects of exceptional brilliancy, possessed one quality popularity-seekers might have envied: the ability to explode on the slightest provocation into a laugh instinct with all the characteristics of genuine delight.

“Give and take,” observed the maternal Sellars, as soon as Mr. Clapper’s roar had died away; “that’s what you’ve got to do when you’re married.”
